Boundary graph deformations for the spacetime Penrose inequality
                                              OpenAI

                                       September 27, 2026


                                             Abstract
       We construct graph and conformal deformations of trapped initial-data exteriors in three
    and four spatial dimensions. The resulting metrics have nonnegative scalar curvature, strictly
    negative inner mean curvature, a lower bound protecting every enclosing cut, and an arbi-
    trarily small upper error in ADM energy. We also give a direct four-dimensional maximal
    vacuum construction that retains a noncompact decaying second fundamental form. These
    constructions give boundary routes to the corresponding numerical Penrose inequalities.

1    Introduction
The spacetime Penrose inequality compares the invariant ADM mass mADM = E 2 − |P |2 with
                                                                                 p

the area needed to enclose a trapped region, where E and P are the energy and linear momentum
of asymptotically flat initial data. The deformations below control energy in a chosen end; for
general weak data, the invariant-mass conclusion uses a separate passage to rest ends, where P = 0.
Reducing the problem to the Riemannian Penrose inequality already exposes the main difficulty.
A deformation must create nonnegative scalar curvature while controlling both quantities in
the comparison: energy at infinity and the area of every enclosing cut. If the deformation is
performed on an exterior with a genuine inner boundary, its boundary flux is a third quantity
that must be controlled.
    This paper constructs such boundary deformations in three and four spatial dimensions. It
also develops a direct four-dimensional maximal construction that retains a noncompact decaying
second fundamental form. The common output is a smooth complete Riemannian exterior with
nonnegative scalar curvature, strictly negative inner mean curvature, a metric lower bound
protecting all enclosing areas, and an arbitrarily small upper error in energy. A minimal enclosure
then supplies the boundary to which the Riemannian Penrose inequality applies.
    To explain the area comparison, let (M, g, K) be an initial data exterior with compact
boundary and one asymptotically Euclidean end. The tensor K is the second fundamental form.
Orient an inner boundary toward the end, let H be its mean curvature, and write θ+ = H +trtan K
for its future expansion. A full enclosing cut is the entire compact boundary of a connected
closed outer domain that contains the distant end and has interior in the open exterior. Every
boundary component counts, including any part coincident with the original boundary. Denote
the infimum of their g-areas by A∗ (g). The infimum need not be attained. The precise asymptotic
and constraint hypotheses for each construction are stated at its beginning.
    Both the area and the horizon predicate matter. In spatial dimension three, Ben-Dov’s
example has an outermost future apparent horizon S with |S| > 16πm2ADM [7]. Carrasco–Mars
construct outer area-minimizing generalized apparent horizons, defined by H = | trtan K|, with
the same strict area inequality [12]. These concern distinct formulations. We use full enclosing
area and specify the expansion condition in each theorem.
    The area comparison uses an elementary point that remains valid for all these cuts. In spatial
dimension d, if h ≥ cg as quadratic forms for some c > 0, then the restriction to each tangent




                                                2
(d − 1)-plane gives
                      |Γ|h ≥ c(d−1)/2 |Γ|g   and hence    A∗ (h) ≥ c(d−1)/2 A∗ (g).
No selection of a minimizing component and no convergence of minimizers is needed for this
inequality. We use it after constructing a metric of the form
                              gb = e4t/(d−2) g + l(t)2 df 2 ,
                                                           
                                                                  t ≥ −ε.
Here f is the graph height, t is the conformal unknown, and l > 0 is the warping factor. The
graph term is nonnegative on every tangent plane. Thus the lower bound for t protects the full
enclosing area before the graph is known to have bounded slope.

1.1   The constructions and their relationship
The first two constructions start with strict dominant-energy data, a strictly future-trapped
boundary, and a prepared end with compactly supported K. The third keeps a maximal tensor
tail. The following map separates these geometric outputs from their original-data applications;
the cited statements give the full hypotheses. Here ω3 = |S 3 | = 2π 2 .

 Construction                 Geometric output                  Original-data consequence
 Prepared dimension three;    Theorem 2.3                       mADM ≥ A∗ /(16π) for weak decay
                                                                          p

 compact K                                                      q > 1/2; Theorem 2.4
 Prepared dimension four;     Theorem 8.2                       mADM ≥ 12 (A∗ /ω3 )2/3 for weak decay
 compact K                                                      q > 1; Corollary 14.1
 Direct maximal dimension     Proposition 16.2                  E ≥ 12 (|S|g /ω3 )2/3 in the maximal-
 four; decaying K                                               vacuum application; Theorem 16.1

    In the first two applications, weak decay means g − δ = O2 (r−q ) and K = O1 (r−1−q ). The
exteriors satisfy the dominant energy condition, integrable constraint densities, finite timelike
ADM charges E > |P |, and weak future trapping. Their separate rest preparations control all
enclosing cuts. In dimension three, the prepared energies tend to mADM and the full enclosing-area
infima converge. In dimension four, the distant-end replacement supplies a one-sided lower area
bound; strictification is removed at each fixed repaired end before the replacement radius tends
to infinity. These inputs are [34, Proposition 7.13 and Proposition 8.8/Lemma 8.9, respectively].
    The direct maximal theorem has its own approximation and retains the tensor tail. Its
stated application has q > 1, ball-exterior topology, a connected spherical marginally outer
trapped boundary (θ+ = 0), a torus action, P = 0, E > 0, outermostness, and outer area
minimization against all enclosures. Here P = 0 identifies energy with invariant mass, and outer
area minimization identifies A∗ with |S|g . The strict geometric output in the middle column has
its own hypotheses; the theorem records this particular original-data application. The broader
numerical comparison in Corollary 14.1 uses the distinct end-replacement route.
    The difference at infinity is concrete. With compactly supported K, the distant trace equation
is a homogeneous graph equation with a positive height term. When trg K = 0, the retained
tensor contributes a term quadratic in ∇f , which becomes a drift along the solution. The equation
remains homogeneous and still forces exponential graph decay at fixed deformation parameter.
The scalar equation behaves differently: its zero-graph limit retains |K|2 /2. Section 15 derives
this term and the tail weights needed to control it. That calculation identifies both the shared
argument and the additional preparation, threshold geometry and estimates required for the
maximal route.

                                                   3
1.2   Why the inner boundary matters
The scalar-curvature identity separates the original constraint densities, a nonnegative quadratic
expression, and the divergence of a vector field V . Prescribing this divergence produces the
required curvature sign. At infinity, the integral of V measures the ADM energy change. A
boundary condition for V also prescribes the mean curvature of the deformed inner boundary.
The divergence theorem therefore links the two ends of the construction.
    The useful boundaries are threshold trapped-region frontiers. For general K, two signs of the
tensor produce black and white faces, whose names distinguish their expansion equations and the
corresponding Dirichlet heights of the graph. The collars outside these faces supply geometric
barriers for the graph before uniform ellipticity is available. They first confine its scaled height
to the required interval and then force a signed normal slope. That slope makes the possible
negative inner flux exponentially small relative to the positive bulk terms. The all-black maximal
construction has its own threshold argument, using maximality in place of the two-sided trace
budget.
    This order is essential. Uniform ellipticity is an outcome of the geometric height, floor and
slope estimates; it is not assumed to prove them. Once the coefficients lie in a bounded elliptic
range, local scalar estimates give the boundary regularity needed for a compact continuation
map. The four-dimensional proof retains the critical interpolation step and the interface measures
produced by reflection. Each application verifies its own boundary conditions and completes its
own degree and bootstrap argument.
    There are two kinds of constants throughout. Polynomial bounds in the large deformation
parameter N are used in the final flux comparison. Classical regularity constants may depend
arbitrarily on fixed N . One first exhausts the outer radius with N fixed, then lets N tend
to infinity using only the polynomial estimates. This distinction prevents a local compactness
argument from being mistaken for a uniform estimate in the final mass limit.

1.3   Relation to earlier methods
Penrose’s proposed inequality arose from the relation between gravitational collapse, horizons
and total energy [36]. In the time-symmetric setting the second fundamental form vanishes and
the dominant-energy condition becomes nonnegative scalar curvature. Geroch’s smooth-flow idea
and the conditional argument of Jang–Wald preceded the weak-flow theory [17, 23]; see also the
historical discussion in [21, p. 359]. Huisken–Ilmanen’s weak inverse-mean-curvature flow proved
the three-dimensional Riemannian inequality for each connected component of the horizon [21,
Main Theorem, p. 356]; Bray’s conformal flow gives the bound for the total area of a disconnected
horizon [8, Theorems 1 and 19]. Bray–Lee extended the numerical conformal-flow theorem to
dimensions below eight [11, Theorem 1.4]. The final comparison here uses the numerical clauses
of these theorems after constructing their complete Riemannian inputs.
    The graph strategy comes from Jang’s equation and the Schoen–Yau positive-energy ar-
gument [22, 38]. The earlier Schoen–Yau proof establishes the positive mass theorem in the
Riemannian and maximal-data cases by minimal surfaces [37]. A classical Jang graph can develop
a cylindrical end at an apparent horizon; Metzger analyses this blow-up and its exponential
cylindrical convergence near a strictly stable component [30]. Conformal normalization of such
a graph can lose the horizon-area comparison. Bray and Khuri developed the warped-graph
approach to address this obstruction [9, 10]; their 2010 work also proves a sharp spherical case
under its stated hypotheses. The general warped scalar identity already retains a trace discrepancy
before the generalized Jang equation is imposed [10, Identity 9, p. 580].

                                                 4
    Han–Khuri prove existence and quantitative boundary blow-up for prescribed warping fac-
tors [19]. Their later coupling to conformal flow gives a reduction contingent on solving the
coupled system with the required boundary behavior [20]. The direct conclusion of that argu-
ment concerns ADM energy, as its Remark 2 explains. Here the coupled equations prescribe a
nonzero divergence source, a trace depending increasingly on graph height, and a penalty near
the conformal lower bound. The boundary analysis proves global existence for these equations
and supplies the area and energy comparisons needed for the subsequent limits.
    This distinction concerns the equations, not only their interpretation. Jaracz proves radial
nonexistence for a specified generalized-Jang and zero-divergence system [24]; that result does not
apply to the nonzero-source system studied here. His spacetime Poisson method also provides
a predecessor for conformal strictification [25]. The maximal construction below solves its
own problem with nonzero inner Neumann data and proves the energy and full enclosing-area
comparisons needed to remove the strict approximation.
    Trapped-region boundaries and their stability enter through the work of Andersson–Metzger
and Andersson–Eichmair–Metzger [4, 6]. Eichmair’s Perron and almost-minimizing constructions
provide the original higher-dimensional method [14]; the precise total-region theorem used below
is the one recorded by Andersson–Eichmair–Metzger. For the stability operator of marginally
outer trapped surfaces (MOTS) and its principal-eigenfunction characterization of stability we
use Andersson–Mars–Simon [5]. The local arguments below explain the additional threshold
continuity, support transport and collar choices used by the deformation. The analytic part
combines scalar elliptic estimates with the Leray–Schauder degree principle [27]; the particular
boundary homotopies and their admissible open sets are constructed explicitly.
    Recent spacetime results also distinguish the relevant scopes. Khuri–Kunduri prove the sharp
inequality for cohomogeneity-one SU(n + 1)-invariant data in spatial dimensions 2(n + 1) ≥ 4, with
their outermost future-or-past horizon condition [26, Theorem 1.1]. The direct maximal theorem
here has different symmetry and outer-area-minimization hypotheses. Allen–Bryden–Kazaras–
Khuri prove a three-dimensional invariant-mass bound by a universal suboptimal constant times
the square root of minimum enclosing area, with additional trace decay and a specified horizon
selection [3, Theorem 1.1 and Remark 1.2].
    Two companion manuscripts supply specified inputs: scalar algebra and weak-end prepara-
tions from [34], and full-perimeter enclosure geometry from [35]. Their exact hypotheses and
normalization are checked at each use. The numerical comparisons at the end of the boundary
constructions use Bray and Bray–Lee directly.

1.4   Reading the proof
The three routes retain their own geometry, equations, global bounds and end comparison. Shared
arguments are proved at their first use in the three-dimensional route: threshold continuity,
stable collars and exact support transport in Section 3; the passage from lower tests to sublevel
supports in Lemma 5.1; and scalar measure-source regularity and degree on varying open sets in
Lemmas 6.2 and 6.5. Each later use verifies its tensor, coefficients and boundary conditions.
    For dimension three, Sections 4–7 proceed from the coupled equations and conformal floor
through geometric bounds, boundary existence, and the complete-end comparison. Dimension
four follows the same order in Sections 9–13, with its own compact total-region construction and
the critical interpolation in Theorem 12.1. Corollary 14.1 then gives its separate weak-decay
transfer.
    Section 15 explains the maximal tensor tail before Sections 17–22 construct the direct route.


                                                5
Its pointwise scalar and boundary identities come from Section 10, its axial estimate from
Theorem 12.1, and its enclosure input from Proposition 8.3. The maximal part supplies its own
strict approximation, noncompact drift bounds, scalar solve, reflected boundary checks and final
flux estimate.


2     A boundary deformation in three dimensions
The aim of this part is to replace an initial-data exterior by a Riemannian exterior while keeping
a genuine inner boundary. Three quantities have to be controlled together: scalar curvature, the
area of every enclosing cut, and the ADM energy. The inner boundary creates a flux term in
the energy balance. We choose its geometry and the boundary conditions so that this term is
absorbed by a positive bulk integral.

2.1   Initial data, normals, and enclosing area
We work in three spatial dimensions, with zero cosmological constant and G = c = 1. For a
Riemannian metric g and a symmetric covariant two-tensor K, define the constraint densities by
                 16πµ = Rg + (trg K)2 − |K|2g ,           8πJ = divg K − (trg K)g .          (2.1)
                                                                                 

Here J is a covector field. The dominant energy condition is
                                               µ ≥ |J|g .                                    (2.2)
All initial data considered here are smooth, including at a compact boundary when one is present.
    On an asymptotically flat end with Euclidean coordinates x and r = |x|, our basic assumptions
are
                       gij − δij = O2 (r−q ),   Kij = O1 (r−1−q ),    q > 21 .              (2.3)
The notation Oj (r−a ) includes the corresponding coordinate derivative bounds through order j.
We assume that µ and |J|g are integrable and that the following ADM limits exist and are finite:
                                 1
                                           Z
                           E=         lim  (∂j gij − ∂i gjj )ni dAδ ,                        (2.4)
                                16π r→∞ Sr
                                 1
                                         Z
                           Pi =     lim    Kij − (trg K)gij nj dAδ .                         (2.5)
                                                               
                                8π r→∞ Sr
The normal and area form in these definitions are Euclidean. Whenever E > |P |δ , write
                                               q
                                        m=         E 2 − |P |2δ .                            (2.6)
    For a two-sided surface Σ with specified unit normal ν, we use
                            HΣ = divΣ ν,          θ+ (Σ) = HΣ + trΣ K.
Thus Euclidean spheres have positive mean curvature for the normal toward infinity. A future
marginally outer trapped surface, abbreviated MOTS, satisfies θ+ = 0. A weakly future outer
trapped surface satisfies θ+ ≤ 0. Our spacetime sign convention is
                                     K(Y, Z) = g(∇Y n, Z),                                   (2.7)
where n is the future unit timelike normal. Equivalently, in Gaussian normal coordinates the
spatial metric has normal derivative 2K.

                                                    6
Definition 2.1 (Exterior and enclosing area). An exterior is a connected orientable smooth
three-manifold Ω with nonempty compact smooth boundary, complete as a metric space with
its boundary included, and with one asymptotically flat coordinate end whose complement is
compact. An enclosing cut is the entire compact smooth embedded two-sided intrinsic boundary
of a connected smooth end-containing domain D closed in Ω, whose intrinsic interior lies in int Ω.
It separates the entire inner boundary from the distant end. The inner side includes the original
obstacle and every bounded complementary pocket. All components of the cut and every portion
coincident with the inner boundary are counted. Its normal points into D, toward the end. We
put
                            ag (∂Ω) = inf{|Γ|g : Γ is an enclosing cut}.
When using perimeter compactness, we take the closure of this class among filled inner sets and
include the area of any frontier coinciding with the obstacle. Smooth outward approximation gives
the same infimum. Enclosing cuts and minimizing sets are taken with bounded complementary
pockets filled. Auxiliary perimeter arguments may use competitors containing the inner obstacle
before filling: filling a bounded pocket deletes its frontier, cannot increase perimeter, and leaves
the enclosing infimum unchanged.

   The definition permits coincidence because first variations of the minimum need not be realized
by a cut lying strictly outside the obstacle. It permits disconnected cuts because neither the
minimizing hull nor an intermediate trapped boundary need be connected. The smooth-cut
and full-perimeter formulations have the same infimum; we use the independent full-enclosure
geometry of [35, Lemma 2.1].

2.2   Prepared data and the geometric output
The construction itself starts from the following explicit geometric conditions. Its hypotheses do
not require that the data have been obtained by a particular end-replacement procedure.

Definition 2.2 (Prepared three-dimensional data). Let (Ω0 , g, K) be an exterior in Definition 2.1,
with inner boundary S0 . Suppose K is compactly supported and, on its single coordinate end,
g − δ = Oj (r−1 ) for every fixed j ≥ 0. Assume the physical constraint densities in Equation (2.1)
are integrable, the ADM energy is E > 0, and the ADM momentum is zero. There are 0 < δ < 1,
c > 0, and a smooth radius extension ϱ ≥ 1, equal to r sufficiently far out, such that

                           Rg = O(r−3−δ ),      8π(µ − |J|g ) ≥ cϱ−3−δ .

The boundary is strictly future trapped with its normal into the exterior: θ+ (S0 ) < 0 on every
component. Its full enclosing-area infimum is denoted by A∗ = ag (S0 ) > 0.

Theorem 2.3 (Three-dimensional boundary deformation). For every prepared exterior (Ω0 , g, K)
there is a connected one-ended subexterior Ω ⊂ Ω0 whose smooth compact boundary B separates
S0 from infinity, with the following property. For each sufficiently small fixed ϵ > 0 and every
sufficiently large integer N , there is a smooth metric gbϵ,N on Ω, complete with its boundary
included, for which

                    Rgbϵ,N ≥ 0,                         H∂Ω (gbϵ,N ) < 0,                      (2.8)
                     gbϵ,N ≥ e−4ϵ g,                         Egbϵ,N ≤ E + ηϵ,N ,               (2.9)



                                                 7
where the boundary normal points into Ω and, at fixed ϵ, ηϵ,N → 0. More precisely, for constants
C, a depending on the fixed data and ϵ,

                              0 ≤ ηϵ,N ≤ C(1 + N )a e−ϵN + e−ϵN/2 .
                                                                         

                                                                  ′
The new end has gbϵ,N − δ = O2 (r−1 ) and Rgbϵ,N = O(r−3−δ ) for some δ ′ > 0. Every full enclosing
cut T in Ω satisfies
                                         |T |gbϵ,N ≥ e−4ϵ A∗ .

    The construction begins by selecting maximal black and white trapped regions. Their outer
collars prescribe opposite signs for the graph slope, but the same negative mean-curvature
condition for the final metric. Section 3 proves the required geometry, including the passage
from weak supports to smooth enclosures. Section 4 specifies the trace and divergence equations
and excludes a lower conformal barrier. The estimates of Section 5 then control the solutions
before uniform ellipticity is available. Section 6 supplies the boundary solve and degree argument.
Finally, Section 7 computes the end charge and proves Theorem 2.3.
    The algebraic inputs from [34, Propositions 4.1–4.2 and Lemma 4.3] are its scalar identity,
weighted coercivity, and the pointwise algebraic clause of its floor lemma. Their proofs are
independent of its numerical conclusion. The boundary construction here retains the signed-collar
estimates, boundary flux, homotopy, and degree that a filled construction does not require.

tion 2.1. Assume Equations (2.1)–(2.3), integrability of µ and |J|g , and the finite ADM limits
(2.4)–(2.5). Orient ∂Ω by the normal into Ω, and suppose θ+ (∂Ω) ≤ 0. If E > |P |δ , then

No extension of the data across ∂Ω is required.

    The last assertion will follow from the boundary construction and the weak three-dimensional
preparation theorem of [34, Proposition 7.13]. That preparation supplies compact tensor
                                                                                     p support,
strict trapping and the weighted margin in Definition 2.2; its energies converge to E 2 − |P |2
and its full enclosing areas converge to the original infimum. We will use only this preparation
theorem, not the companion’s numerical inequality. In particular, the ADM normalizations in
Equations (2.4) and (2.5) remain unchanged.


3     Trapped domains, collars, and weak supports
We need a boundary on which the graph height can be constant and its slope has a definite sign.
A future trapped face supplies one sign; a past trapped face supplies the other. We first choose
maximal regions of these two types, then construct outward collars and compatible height offsets.
The original obstacle stays on their excluded side, so every resulting enclosing cut still controls its
full area. Throughout, the data satisfy Definition 2.2. Write S0 for their inner boundary. Thus
S0 is strictly future outer trapped, K has compact support, and the single end has the all-order



                                                   8
O(r−1 ) bounds stipulated in that definition. In particular, sufficiently large coordinate spheres
have positive outward mean curvature. The function
                                   m(x) = 8π µ(x) − |J(x)|g                                  (3.1)
                                                              


is positive, has a positive lower bound on every compact set, and has the positive r−3−δ lower
bound on the end, with 0 < δ < 1. Let A∗ denote the prepared data’s filled enclosing perimeter
infimum.

3.1   The bounding-region convention
For a symmetric tensor Q and a two-sided surface Σ with a specified unit normal ν, put
                                   ΘQ (Σ, ν) = HΣ (ν) + trΣ Q.                               (3.2)
For the boundary of a domain the specified normal always points out of that domain. Notice both
elementary identities
                      ΘQ−(c/2)g = ΘQ − c,       Θ−Q (Σ, −ν) = −ΘQ (Σ, ν).                    (3.3)
The factor 1/2 in the first formula is the reciprocal of the surface dimension.
   We use the following geometric theorem. Its boundary parts may be disconnected.
Theorem 3.1 (MOTS barriers and the total trapped region). Let (N, g, Q) be a smooth compact
three-dimensional initial data set with boundary Γ− ⊔ Γ+ . Assume Γ+ is nonempty and has
positive expansion with its normal out of N . The inner part Γ− may be empty; when it is present,
assume its expansion is negative with its normal into N .
     Consider all smooth domains whose inner boundary is Γ− and whose remaining boundary Σ
lies in the interior of N and satisfies ΘQ (Σ) ≤ 0. They avoid Γ+ , and their normal on Σ points
out of the domain. If their union is nonempty, its closure has a smooth compact embedded stable
MOTS frontier in the interior, separating it from Γ+ . This closure is itself the closure of such
a domain and contains every domain in the defining class. If Γ− is nonempty, the two strict
barriers also give a nonempty smooth embedded enclosing MOTS.
    The total-region assertion is [4, Definitions 7.1–7.2 and Theorem 7.3]; the barrier assertion
is Theorem 3.1 there, with stability furnished by Theorem 4.1. The preliminary conventions
in Section 2 of that paper allow an empty inner boundary. That paper uses open regions;
taking their closures preserves the smooth frontier and its expansion, giving the closed-region
formulation above. These results require no energy condition on Q. We therefore apply them to
the shifted tensors in Equation (3.3), even though these tensors need not satisfy the dominant
energy condition. A component of the complement of a smooth trapped domain which meets no
designated outer boundary may be filled: its entire boundary is then deleted and the remaining
boundary has the same expansion. The maximal region is therefore filled in this relative sense.
    We use compact inner fillings only to express enclosing competitors. One may attach a
smooth collar behind S0 , extend the one-sided metric smoothly, and complete the inner side by
a fixed compact filling. All competitors contain that filling and only their boundaries in the
original exterior are counted. This introduces no geometric condition behind S0 . For perimeter
minimization with this smooth obstacle, the relevant regularity input is [21, Regularity Theorem
1.3(iii)]: the minimizing boundary is C 1,1 and is smooth away from contact. We retain the filled
enclosing class and its smooth approximation from the independent enclosure geometry of [35,
Lemmas 2.1–2.2]. The trapped regions below are not assumed to minimize perimeter.

                                                9
3.2   Choosing the two maximal regions
Fix a large sphere SR0 outside supp K such that all coordinate spheres with radius at least R0
have positive outward mean curvature. A compact smooth domain with Θ±K ≤ c ≤ 0 cannot
reach this part of the end. Indeed, at a point where its boundary maximizes the coordinate
radius, it lies inside the tangent coordinate sphere and has the same outward normal. The graph
second-derivative comparison gives H∂D ≥ HSr > 0 there; also K = 0 there. This contradicts
its assumed expansion. The same argument applies to an enclosing domain with prescribed
inner obstacle, since the maximum radius is attained on its free boundary. Consequently all
the negative-threshold regions used below lie in one fixed compact radial range. They may be
constructed using any sufficiently distant sphere as the outer barrier, without dependence on that
choice.
    For
                                        max ΘK (S0 ) < c < 0,                                (3.4)
                                        S0

let Bc be the closed total trapped region for the condition ΘK ≤ c, with S0 as the required inner
boundary and with its inner filling understood. It is nonempty: a sufficiently short outward collar
of S0 supplies a strict seed. Theorem 3.1, applied to K − (c/2)g, gives its smooth stable frontier
with expansion exactly c. The regions Bc increase with c. Their complement toward the end
has no bounded component, since such a component could be filled to enlarge the total trapped
region.
    We will choose cb < 0 close to zero and set B = Bcb . In the complement of B, use the full
black frontier and a distant coordinate sphere as outer barrier faces. With the normal out of this
complement, the black frontier has expansion for −K equal to −cb > 0. For c < 0 close to zero,
let Wc be the closed total trapped region of compact smooth domains in that complement with

                                      Θ−K = H − trΣ K ≤ c.                                       (3.5)

There is no required inner boundary in this application of Theorem 3.1. The shifted expansion
of a reversed black face is −cb − c > 0, and that of a distant sphere is also positive. Thus the
theorem applies whenever the defining union is nonempty. When the union is empty we simply
put Wc = ∅. When nonempty, it has smooth stable frontier of white expansion c and lies a
positive distance from all black faces. The family Wc is increasing, with the black region held
fixed.
    To control limits of the deformation, we choose thresholds at which these regions are right-
continuous. The following lemma includes empty regions.
Lemma 3.2 (Right-continuous thresholds). Let Ec , c in an interval, be an increasing family of
closed subsets of a fixed compact metric space. Outside a countable set of parameters,

                      Ecj −→ Ec     in Hausdorff distance whenever      cj ↓ c.                  (3.6)

Here continuity at Ec = ∅ means that Ec′ is empty for every sufficiently close c′ > c.
Proof. Choose a countable dense set {xi } and a number L larger than the diameter of the compact
space. Set dc (x) = min{L, dist(x, Ec )}, with the value L when Ec is empty. For each i, c 7→ dc (xi )
is a bounded nonincreasing real function, so its right discontinuities form a countable set. Avoid
their countable union. Then dcj (xi ) → dc (xi ) for all i whenever cj ↓ c. Every dc is 1-Lipschitz. A
finite net chosen from the dense set therefore upgrades this convergence to uniform convergence
on the compact space.

                                                 10
    For nonempty Ec , if points yj ∈ Ecj stayed a fixed distance from Ec , uniform convergence
evaluated at yj would contradict dcj (yj ) = 0. The opposite Hausdorff inclusion follows from
Ec ⊂ Ecj . For empty Ec , uniform convergence to L is incompatible with any nonempty Ecj , since
its distance function has a zero. This proves the stated convention as well.

    Choose cb at such a right-continuity point of the black family, then choose cw < 0 at such a
point of the white family for this fixed black region. Both choices can be made arbitrarily close
to zero. Write W = Wcw , and let Ω be the closure of the component of the complement of B ∪ W
which reaches the selected end. Its boundary B is a finite disjoint union of complete components
of the two smooth frontiers. Write

                        B = Bb ⊔ Bw ,       H = HB (ν),        PB = trB K,                    (3.7)

where ν points into Ω. Thus

                        H + PB = cb     on Bb ,        H − PB = cw   on Bw .                  (3.8)

The set Bw may be empty, and Bb need not contain every black frontier component: a white
component may cut a black face off from the end. We retain exactly the boundary of the
component that reaches infinity. The compact, disjoint frontiers create no intersection corners.
   The excluded side of B contains S0 and a collar of it. Hence every compact smooth enclosing
cut T in Ω, with all its components retained, is an enclosing competitor for the original filled
obstacle after adjoining the discarded regions to its inner side. It follows that

                                            |T |g ≥ A∗ .                                      (3.9)

The same conclusion holds for perimeter competitors. This is a statement about inclusion of
competitor classes; no minimizing property of B is used.
    Every enclosing cut now carries the area lower bound A∗ . The next step is local: on each
retained face we need a smooth outward family whose expansion is strictly larger than its threshold.
Stability alone allows a zero principal eigenvalue, so that case requires the maximality of the
chosen region.

3.3   Outward collars, including zero stability eigenvalue
Lemma 3.3 (Collars of a maximal frontier). Let (N d , g) be smooth, with d ∈ {3, 4} and a fixed
smooth symmetric tensor Q. Let Σ be a compact connected smooth two-sided hypersurface without
boundary, forming a component of the frontier of a closed region D, with normal pointing out of D.
Assume ΘQ (Σ) = c and stability for outward variations. Assume also local outward-replacement
maximality: in some collar of Σ disjoint from the other frontier components and all designated
barrier faces, no positive outward normal graph can replace Σ, leaving D unchanged outside that
collar, and have expansion at most c everywhere on the new component. There is a smooth
foliation Σs , 0 ≤ s < s0 , of an outward collar, with Σ0 = Σ and positive outward normal velocity,
such that
                             ΘQ (Σs ) > c (0 < s < s0 ),    ΘQ (Σ0 ) = c.                   (3.10)
In particular its leaf parameter s is a smooth defining function and |∇s| is bounded above and
below by positive constants.



                                                  11
Proof. Use normal graphs Σ(v) over Σ and pull their expansion back to Σ. The map

                         Φ : C 2,α (Σ) −→ C 0,α (Σ),        Φ(v) = ΘQ (Σ(v)) − c

is smooth near zero. Let ν be the outward normal and II its second fundamental form. The
normal-variation formulas give the linearization L = DΦ(0) explicitly as

                  Lv = −∆Σ v + 2Q(ν, gradΣ v) + trΣ ∇ν Q − |II|2 − Ric(ν, ν) v.
                                                                                        


The hypersurface dimension is d − 1, so ΘQ−cg/(d−1) = ΘQ − c. This constant shift has zero
derivative under hypersurface variations; hence L is also the MOTS stability operator for
Q − cg/(d − 1), with principal part −∆Σ . Stability gives a principal eigenvalue λ ≥ 0 and
a positive smooth eigenfunction φ [5, Proposition 5.1]. If λ > 0, take v = sφ. Smooth dependence
gives Φ(sφ) = sλφ + O(s2 ) in C 0 , which is positive for all sufficiently small s > 0. The graph
velocity is positive as well.
    Suppose λ = 0. The kernel of L is spanned by φ, and the adjoint kernel is spanned by a
positive smooth function φ∗ . These are the principal-eigenfunction facts for a scalar elliptic
operator on a compact connected closed manifold [5, Section 4, Lemma 4.1]. For example,
simplicity of the kernel also follows by writing a kernel element as v = φw and applying the
strong maximum principle to the resulting equation for w, whose zeroth-order term vanishes.
Consider the augmented map
                                                            Z         
                                   G(v, a) = Φ(v) − a,              v dA .
                                                                Σ

Its derivative at (0, 0) is                                Z          
                                    (v, a) 7−→ Lv − a,           v dA .                               (3.11)
                                                             Σ
This map is an isomorphism. Indeed, to solve Lv − a = f with               v = b, the Fredholm compatibility
                                                                       R

condition uniquely fixes
                                                φ∗ f dA
                                             R
                                       a = − RΣ ∗       .
                                               Σ φ dA
The equation for v then has a solution; adding a multiple of φ imposes its prescribed integral
uniquely. Elliptic estimates and this one-dimensional normalization give a bounded inverse
between the indicated Hölder spaces.
     The implicit-function theorem consequently supplies smooth v(s), a(s) with G(v(s), a(s)) =
(0, s). Differentiating at zero and pairing with φ∗ gives a′ (0) = 0 and
                                                     φ
                                         v ′ (0) = R      > 0.
                                                   Σ φ dA

After shortening the interval, all these graphs form an outward foliation and have constant
expansion c + a(s). If a(s) ≤ 0 at any positive s in this interval, replace only this frontier
component by Σ(v(s)). This graph strictly enlarges D inside the chosen collar, leaves every other
frontier component and designated barrier fixed, and has expansion at most c on the replaced
component. This contradicts the assumed local outward-replacement maximality. Therefore
a(s) > 0 for every such s > 0.
    In either case positive graph velocity and compactness give the asserted bounds on |∇s|.
Smooth elliptic bootstrapping of the graph equation gives smooth leaves in the zero-eigenvalue
case.

                                                       12
    In the present application d = 3. The full black and white regions are Bcb and Wcw , with
Q = K and Q = −K, respectively. Theorem 3.1 gives their smooth two-sided stable frontiers,
whose retained components have expansion cb and cw . Total-region maximality verifies the local
replacement hypothesis: a positive outward replacement with expansion at most the threshold,
leaving all other frontier components and designated barriers fixed, would be an admissible strictly
larger trapped region. For white faces the entire replacement lies in the fixed black complement.
Thus the lemma applies separately to each retained face. There are finitely many faces, so
choose disjoint collars in Ω and shorten them to have a common positive parameter range when
convenient. Write νs = ∇s/|∇s| for their outward leaf normal; it agrees with ν at B.

3.4   From weak exterior supports to a smooth enclosure
The sublevel limits used below need not have smooth frontiers. We now show how an expansion
bound on their exterior supports produces a smooth enclosure. The argument applies to arbitrary
closed sets, without positive reach or a curvature bound on the supporting surfaces.

Definition 3.4. Let (N d , g) be a smooth Riemannian manifold, d ∈ {3, 4}, with a smooth
symmetric tensor Q. Let D ⊂ N be closed, and let x ∈ ∂D be an interior point of N . A smooth
exterior support is a smooth function ϕ near x with ϕ(x) = 0, dϕ(x) ̸= 0, and D ⊂ {ϕ ≤ 0}
locally near x. Its normal is n = ∇ϕ/|∇ϕ|. Define

                                       (g ij − ni nj )∇i ∇j ϕ
                         EQ [ϕ](x) =                          + tr Q − Q(n, n).                (3.12)
                                               |∇ϕ|

We say D has exterior-support expansion at most c if every such support at every point of its
frontier has EQ [ϕ] ≤ c.

   For a smooth domain this is exactly the usual upper bound on its outward expansion: at
contact an exterior supporting graph has mean curvature at most that of the enclosed smooth
graph. The definition is unchanged by increasing smooth reparametrizations of ϕ, because the
additional Hessian term is a multiple of dϕ ⊗ dϕ and has zero tangential trace.

Lemma 3.5 (Parallel neighborhoods and additive error). Let (N d , g, Q) be smooth, with d ∈ {3, 4}.
Suppose the frontier of a closed set D lies in a fixed compact interior region and has exterior-
support expansion at most c. Put Dz = {y : dist(y, D) ≤ z}. For sufficiently small z > 0, all
exterior supports of Dz have expansion at most

                                                c + Cz.                                        (3.13)

The allowable radius and C depend only on the compact ambient geometry and Q, and not on the
support, its second fundamental form, or the regularity of D.

Proof. If D = ∅, use dist(y, D) = +∞; then Dz = ∅ and the assertion is vacuous. Otherwise,
take a support ϕ to Dz at x′ . Since x′ ∈ ∂Dz , its distance from D is z. For sufficiently small radii
the relevant distance neighborhood of ∂D lies in a fixed compact interior neighborhood. There
is therefore a nearest point x ∈ D and a length-z minimizing unit-speed geodesic γ : [0, z] → N
from x to x′ . This nearest point lies on ∂D: if it were interior to D, moving a short distance
along γ toward x′ would decrease the distance. The entire segment lies in the fixed smooth
compact neighborhood and is shorter than its injectivity radius. The ball B z (x) is contained in


                                                   13
Dz and touches its exterior support at x′ . Its tangent plane there therefore agrees with that of
the support and
                                        ∇ϕ(x′ )
                                                  = γ̇(z).                                 (3.14)
                                       |∇ϕ(x′ )|
Let Pt : Tx N → Tγ(t) N denote parallel transport and put P = Pz .
   For every X ∈ Tx N solve the Jacobi boundary problem
                     ′′
                    JX  + Rm(JX , γ̇)γ̇ = 0,        JX (0) = X,      JX (z) = P X.                   (3.15)

Here primes denote covariant differentiation along γ. The short interval and absence of conjugate
points make this problem uniquely solvable. Its estimates can be seen without division by an
uncontrolled quantity. In a parallel frame write jX (t) = Pt−1 JX (t) and R(t) for the curvature
coefficient. The integral equation is
                                                                   1
                             Z t                                       Z z
       jX (t) = X + tAX −          (t − s)R(s)jX (s) ds,      AX =           (z − s)R(s)jX (s) ds.
                              0                                    z     0

It implies, by absorption for small z,

                                   sup |jX (t)| ≤ 2|X|,      |AX | ≤ Cz|X|,
                                   0≤t≤z
                                                                                                     (3.16)
                            sup |jX (t) − X| ≤ Cz 2 |X|.
                           0≤t≤z

The second line follows from the same integral equation and the first-line bounds. Also ⟨JX , γ̇⟩ is
affine in t and has equal endpoint values. Thus ⟨AX , γ̇(0)⟩ = 0.
    We may therefore prescribe the first jet of a smooth unit vector field V near x by

                                   V (x) = γ̇(0),      ∇X V (x) = AX .

To realize it with uniform second-jet bounds, choose a smooth local orthonormal frame obtained
by radial parallel transport from x. In that frame take the affine coefficient vector with the
prescribed value and first derivatives, and normalize its length. The orthogonality just proved
means normalization preserves its prescribed first derivatives. The frame has uniformly bounded
derivatives on a fixed small coordinate ball, and Equation (3.16) therefore gives uniformly bounded
second derivatives of V . This construction uses the radial direction and the ambient geometry,
with no derivatives of the support ϕ.
    Define the local endpoint map

                                           Fz (y) = expy zV (y) .
                                                               


Its differential at x is the terminal value of the Jacobi field with initial values X, ∇X V (x). By
Equation (3.15), it has the exact property

                        Fz (x) = x′ ,       ( dFz )x = P,     |(∇ dFz )x | ≤ Cz.                     (3.17)

The last bound follows by differentiating the smooth map (y, v, z) 7→ expy (zv) twice in y, including
the first and second derivatives of V . At z = 0 the map is the identity and its covariant second
derivative vanishes. Its z-derivative is uniformly bounded for the bounded jets just constructed,
giving the displayed Cz bound. The exact middle identity, rather than a near-isometry estimate,
is essential.

                                                    14
    The map Fz takes D locally into Dz , since its defining geodesic from any y ∈ D has length z.
Consequently ψ = ϕ ◦ Fz is an exterior support to D at x. Its derivative is P ∗ dϕ, so its gradient
length is exactly |∇ϕ(x′ )| and, by Equation (3.14), its normal is γ̇(0). The covariant chain rule
gives
                  Hess ψ(X, Y ) = Hess ϕ(P X, P Y ) + dϕ (∇ dFz )(X, Y ) at x.
                                                                          

Trace over an orthonormal basis of γ̇(0)⊥ and divide by the common gradient length. The first
term is exactly the corresponding normalized tangential trace at x′ , and the remaining term has
absolute value at most Cz. Finally, the tensor trace changes by at most Cz, by the C 1 bound on
Q and parallel transport. It follows that

                                    EQ [ψ](x) − EQ [ϕ](x′ ) ≤ Cz.

The assumed support inequality at x proves Equation (3.13). There is no term containing
z| Hess ϕ|.

Lemma 3.6 (A smooth enclosure from weak supports). Let N be a smooth compact connected
three-manifold with boundary Γ− ⊔ Γ+ , where Γ+ is nonempty and Γ− may be empty. Let D be a
nonempty closed subset which contains a relative collar of Γ− when that boundary is prescribed,
and stays a positive distance from Γ+ . Suppose its interior frontier has exterior-support expansion
at most c for a smooth tensor Q.
    For every c′ > c such that ΘQ (Γ+ ) > c′ with its normal out of N , there is a smooth bounding
domain E containing D in its relative interior, containing the required inner collar, and avoiding
Γ+ , whose free boundary satisfies
                                        ΘQ (∂E \ Γ− ) = c′ .                                   (3.18)
Its boundary and the domain may be disconnected. In particular D is contained in the closed total
trapped region at any such larger threshold. No regularity or positive-thickness assumption on D
is required.

Proof. All distance neighborhoods in this proof are relative to N . Since D contains a collar of
Γ− and avoids Γ+ , their new frontiers, at sufficiently small radii, lie in a compact subset of the
interior. A minimizing segment ending on a new frontier has its nearest point on the interior
frontier of D; it cannot start on Γ− because its covered collar has fixed positive width. Thus
the proof of Lemma 3.5 applies unchanged. Choose β > 0 so small that its conclusion holds for
0 < z ≤ β, all these frontiers avoid the prescribed boundary, and

                                            c + Cβ < c′ .                                      (3.19)

    We first construct a smooth inner enclosure without imposing any expansion bound on it.
Smoothly approximate the continuous distance function dD = dist(·, D) on N uniformly, with
error less than β/32, using a finite coordinate cover, mollification, and a partition of unity. Choose
a regular value in (β/3, β/2) of the resulting smooth function. Its sublevel set U has smooth
interior boundary and, for example, satisfies

                                        Dβ/4 ⊂ U ⊂ D3β/4 .                                     (3.20)

The boundary Γ− is contained in its relative interior and Γ+ is excluded. If a component of N \ U
meets no component of Γ+ , fill it. This only deletes components of the smooth free boundary.
After this filling the second inclusion in Equation (3.20) need not hold for its interior, but every

                                                 15
remaining free boundary component still lies in the indicated thin neighborhood, and the first
inclusion still holds.
    Choose η ∈ C ∞ (N ), 0 ≤ η ≤ 1, equal to one on that free boundary and supported in {dD < β}.
Its existence uses the positive separation between this compact smooth boundary and the closed
set {dD ≥ β}; it requires no smoothness of dD . For a sufficiently large finite constant A, the
tensor
                                      QA = Q − 21 (c′ + Aη)g                               (3.21)
makes the free boundary of U a strictly negative inner barrier:

                                ΘQA (∂U ) = ΘQ (∂U ) − c′ − A < 0.

All prescribed outer faces remain strictly positive for QA , because η = 0 there and their original
expansion exceeds c′ . Apply the barrier part of Theorem 3.1 on each component of N \ U . Each
meets Γ+ by the filling step and has nonempty inner boundary by connectedness of N and U ̸= ∅.
We obtain smooth MOTSs Σ bounding a domain E which contains U and avoids Γ+ . They
satisfy, with their normal out of E,

                                       ΘQ (Σ) = c′ + Aη ≥ c′ .                                  (3.22)

    We claim Σ misses {dD < β}. Otherwise set z = minΣ dD < β. The first inclusion in
Equation (3.20) gives z ≥ β/4 > 0. Moreover Dz lies on the inner side of Σ. To see this directly,
if dD (y) < z, a shortest path from y to D of length less than z cannot cross Σ: such a crossing
would have distance from D less than z. Since D ⊂ E, the whole path and y lie in E. Closure
gives Dz ⊂ E.
    At a point attaining the minimum, a defining function for the smooth boundary Σ is therefore
an exterior support to Dz , with the same outward normal as in Equation (3.22). The parallel
support bound and Equation (3.19) imply

                                       ΘQ (Σ) ≤ c + Cz < c′ ,

contradicting Equation (3.22). This excludes the entire modification range, so η = 0 on Σ and
proves Equation (3.18). The contained Dβ/4 gives strict enclosure of D. If desired, fill any
remaining complementary components which meet no designated outer face; this deletes smooth
boundary components and preserves all stated properties.

    For an exterior, truncate by a distant sphere that is strict at the larger threshold. In the
black construction, B already contains the original inner collar. In the white construction there is
no required inner boundary, and the black faces belong to Γ+ . The later applications therefore
need compact avoidance of the foreign faces; they need no boundary regularity of the weak set.
Empty weak sets can be omitted.
    The support argument has supplied the geometric compactness needed for the later height
estimate. We can now fix the small offsets in the trace equation. Their size is measured against
the strict constraint margin, while their normal derivatives distinguish the two boundary signs.

3.5    Small offsets and the prepared domain
Proposition 3.7 (Prepared domain). The thresholds and the domain above can be chosen with
the following properties. The numbers cb , cw < 0 are right-continuity points of their respective full
maximal-region families. The boundary of Ω consists of the faces in Equation (3.8), has disjoint

                                                 16
smooth outward leaf collars as in Lemma 3.3, and preserves the enclosing-area lower bound in
Equation (3.9).
    There are a smooth compactly supported function C on Ω, constants Cb ≥ 0, Cw ≤ 0, and
positive constants kB on its face collars such that
                  Cw ≤ C ≤ Cb ,       C = C b − kB s    in a black collar near Bb ,
                                                                                               (3.23)
                                      C = Cw + kB s in a white collar near Bw .
For an absent type, its corresponding constant is simply a bound. Set

                            b− = cb − Cb < 0,       b+ = −cw − Cw > 0.                         (3.24)

There is a smooth positive function ρ, equal to a positive constant times r−4 on the far end and
satisfying symbol derivative bounds there, such that, for every h ∈ [b− , b+ ],

                       |(h + C) tr K| + |∇C| + ρ ≤ 12 m     everywhere on Ω.                   (3.25)

The thresholds, collars, offset, and ρ are fixed before the deformation parameters are chosen. After
fixing the thresholds and collars, the offset and its first derivatives can be made arbitrarily small.
Proof. The large-sphere comparison above confines all negative-threshold regions to a fixed
compact set, independently of how close the thresholds are to zero. Choose a slightly larger
compact set K containing this range and supp K in its interior. Let

                               m0 = min m > 0,         T0 = sup | tr K|.
                                      K                      K

First restrict both negative thresholds to an interval so close to zero that

                                     max{|cb |, |cw |} T0 < m0 /8,

while retaining Equation (3.4). Lemma 3.2 allows the required successive choices in that interval.
Construct the two regions, select Ω, and fix their disjoint collars inside K as above. All ensuing
constants may depend on these fixed choices.
    Here is an explicit offset construction. On each collar choose a smooth nonincreasing cutoff
χ(s), equal to one near s = 0 and zero near the collar’s far end. Choose κB > 0 so that 1−κB s > 0
throughout that collar. For a common amplitude a > 0, define the contribution of a black collar
to be aχ(s)(1 − κB s), and that of a white collar to be −aχ(s)(1 − κB s). Extend by zero outside
the disjoint collars and sum. This produces a smooth function on the manifold with boundary Ω,
supported in K, with Cb = a, Cw = −a, and kB = aκB on the respective collars. These remain
valid bounds when a type is absent. Its C 1 norm tends to zero with a, even though the collar
widths have already been fixed.
    For h ∈ [b− , b+ ] and C ∈ [Cw , Cb ],

                               |h + C| ≤ max{|cb |, |cw |} + Cb − Cw .

Choose a so small that (Cb − Cw )T0 + ∥∇C∥∞ < m0 /8. The first two terms of Equation (3.25)
are then at most m0 /4 on K and vanish outside K. Finally take a fixed smooth positive weight
equal to r−4 far out and multiply it by a sufficiently small positive constant. The end lower
bound for m and δ < 1, together with compact positivity, allow the choice ρ ≤ m/4 everywhere.
This proves Equation (3.25). All remaining assertions were proved in constructing the regions
and collars.

                                                  17
    At the assigned face heights the quantity h + C consequently has the useful exact values
                           h + C = cb = PB + H             on Bb when h = b− ,
                                                                                               (3.26)
                           h + C = −cw = PB − H on Bw when h = b+ .
These identities determine the deformation’s boundary signs. Right-continuity and Lemma 3.6
will exclude limiting sublevel sets away from the corresponding full trapped regions.


4     The elliptic deformation and exclusion of the floor
We define the coupled system, derive its scalar-curvature identity, and choose a continuation
path along which the conformal factor cannot touch its lower bound. We use the fixed data of
Proposition 3.7. Thus Ω has one controlled asymptotically flat end, K is compactly supported,
and its compact boundary B = Bb ∪ Bw consists of black and white faces. Either type may be
absent. The normal ν points into Ω, and
                                   H = divB ν,             PB = trB K.
The numbers b− < 0 < b+ , the smooth compactly supported offset C, and the positive function
ρ = O(r−4 ) are fixed as in that proposition. In particular,
                     b− + C = PB + H on Bb ,               b+ + C = PB − H on Bw .              (4.1)
All compact sets and geometric constants in this section are fixed after this preparation. Extend
the end radius to a smooth function on Ω bounded below by one. Write ΩR for the large spherical
truncations and SR for their outer boundary.
    A polynomial constant is bounded by C(1 + N )A , with C, A depending on the prepared data
and fixed auxiliary profiles, but not on R, the homotopy parameter, or the solution. Constants
permitted to depend arbitrarily on fixed N will be identified explicitly. First fix 0 < ϵ < 1, then
choose N sufficiently large, and finally require R ≥ Rmin (N, ϵ). We always take N ≥ 4 and
N > 2/ϵ.

4.1   Variables and equations
Choose a smooth nonincreasing ϑ : R → [0, 1] equal to one on (−∞, 0] and zero on [1, ∞). Define
                                                                   Z t         
                           p(t) = N ϑ(N t),           l(t) = exp          p(s) ds ,
                                                                     0                          (4.2)
                                  L(t) = l(t)e , 2t
                                                            pL = p + 2.

Thus l(0) = 1, l(t) = eN t for t ≤ 0, and l is constant for t ≥ 1/N . Derivatives of p of every fixed
order are polynomially bounded. Set
                                      ℓ = e−ϵN ,           τ = ℓ3/2 .                           (4.3)
Whenever t ≥ −ϵ, one has ℓ ≤ l(t) ≤ e.
  For a function f , set
                              σ = |∇f |,            d = D−1 ,
                                              D = 1 + l2 σ 2 ,
                       √                            √
               a = lσ/ D, v = a2 = 1 − d, w = l∇f / D, χ = 4d/(4 + pv),                         (4.4)
                                             √        √
                      Z = t + 18 log D, u = L D, U = l D, h = τ f.

                                                      18
Derivatives and contractions refer to g unless indicated otherwise. At σ > 0, write e = ∇f /σ and
Aj = I + (j − 1)e ⊗ e. The matrices used below have the direction-free expressions

                                                               p+4
                            Ad = I − w ⊗ w,       Aχ = I −           w ⊗ w.                      (4.5)

Vectors and covectors are identified with g.
    The unknowns will be (f, Z). For fixed ∇f , ∂Z/∂t = 1 + pv/4 > 0, and its defining expression
tends to −∞ and +∞ at the two ends of the t-axis. It therefore defines a unique smooth
t = t(Z, ∇f ) for all inputs, without a floor assumption. The coefficients t, d, v, w, χ, u, U and the
direction-free matrices are smooth at ∇f = 0; occurrences of σ and a in the equations combine
into smooth invariant expressions. One has 0 < χ ≤ d ≤ 1 for finite inputs. Define
                                                         l
              ḡ = g + l2 df 2 ,   ĝ = e4t ḡ,   H f = √ Hess f,         S = K + Hf .           (4.6)

The original trace equation is

                                    trAχ S = F,        F = h + C,                                (4.7)

where trA S is contraction with the contravariant matrix A. Along the continuation path we will
specify modified right sides F . The full flux is

                                     V = uAχ 4∇Z + K(w, ·) .                                     (4.8)
                                                                


Here f is the graph height, while Z combines the conformal variable t with the logarithm of the
graph slope. The numbers d and χ record ellipticity in the gradient direction, and u weights the
divergence flux. The scaled height h = τ f makes the prescribed trace strictly increasing in f . At
SR prescribe f = Z = 0. Initially the inner Dirichlet values are h = b− on Bb and h = b+ on Bw .

4.2    The exact scalar-curvature identity
The graph-curvature strategy originates in Jang’s equation and the Schoen–Yau positive-energy
argument [22, 38]. Its generalized warped-graph form and Penrose coupling were developed by
Bray and Khuri [9, 10]; see in particular Identity 9 of [10]. We use the independently proved
algebraic identity of [34, Proposition 4.1], with the normalization below. The prescribed trace
F = h + C, nonzero source, and floor penalty below define a different coupled system; the identity
does not import global solvability of a zero-divergence coupling.
    The next identity separates the original constraint densities, a nonnegative quadratic term,
and a divergence. The second equation will prescribe that divergence so that the deformed metric
has nonnegative scalar curvature.

Proposition 4.1 (Deformation identity). Let f, t be smooth and F = trAχ (K + H f ). At σ > 0
decompose S relative to Re ⊕ e⊥ :

                  T = S|e⊥ ×e⊥ ,   M = S(e, ·)|e⊥ ,    q = S(e, e),   tr T = F − χq.

Then
                                                                     −1
                   2 e Scalĝ = 8π(µ + J(w)) + T + w(F ) − F tr K − u
                   1 4t
                                                                        div V,                   (4.9)


                                                  19
where
                      T = 21 |T tf |2 + (M + pa∇⊥ t) · (M + (p + 2)a∇⊥ t)
                            + 4(p + 1)| dt|2Ad + (χ − χ2 /4)q 2 + 2(p + 1)χqa ∂e t              (4.10)
                            − χF q/2 + 3F 2 /4.

After substituting F = trAχ S, this expression extends smoothly across σ = 0.

Proof. Apply the scalar identity of [34, Proposition 4.1] in spatial dimension three, so its surface
dimension is k = 2. To distinguish the two conventions temporarily, add a subscript A to its
variables. The exact dictionary is

                         tA = 2t,   ZA = 2Z,        lA (s) = l(s/2),   pA = p/2,
                                                                                                (4.11)
                               µA = 8πµ,           JA = 8πJ,      SA = S.

The quantities D, d, w, a, v, H f , F, U are identical. In particular,
                      2d        4d                       √
                            =        = χ,      uA = etA l D = u,         2∇ZA = 4∇Z,
                   2 + pA v   4 + pv

and the companion metric e2tA ḡ is precisely ĝ = e4t ḡ. Thus the divergence term, trace prescription
and metric match without rescaling their flux. The physical density conversion gives 8π(µ + J(w));
the ADM energy and momentum are not changed by that conversion.
    For the quadratic expression, the companion coefficient sp = pA + (k − 1)/2 is (p + 1)/2, and
dtA = 2dt. Its transverse square is |M + (p + 1)a∇⊥ t|2 . Combining its residual −[a2 |∇⊥ t|2 ] with
that square gives (M + pa∇⊥ t) · (M + (p + 2)a∇⊥ t). The remaining gradient contribution is
4(p + 1)|dt|2Ad . Its normal terms become

                         (χ − χ2 /4)q 2 + 2(p + 1)χqa ∂e t − χF q/2 + 3F 2 /4.

These substitutions give Equations (4.9) and (4.10). Smoothness at zero gradient is part of the
invariant identity: the temporary axis has not been used to define its coefficients. The import is
pointwise and requires neither an energy condition nor a solved divergence equation.

Lemma 4.2 (Polynomial coercivity). The weighted coercivity theorem of [34, Proposition 4.2],
with Equation (4.11), makes T nonnegative. For 0 ≤ p ≤ N ,

                          | dt|2Ad + |T |2 + |M |2 + dq 2 + F 2 ≤ C(1 + N )A T                  (4.12)

with universal C, A. At dt = 0 there is the stronger comparison, uniform in p, d,

                c |T tf |2 + |M |2 + χq 2 + F 2 ≤ T ≤ C |T tf |2 + |M |2 + χq 2 + F 2 .         (4.13)
                                                                                      


Proof. With x = ∂e t and y = ∇⊥ t, exact completion gives

                        T = 12 |T tf |2 + |M + (p + 1)ay|2 + [4(p + 1) − v]|y|2
                                              χaq 2
                                                         
                              + 4(p + 1)d x +                                                   (4.14)
                                               4d
                                                4d(9 − d) 2
                                          2
                                       χq
                                  
                              + 34 F −       +            q .
                                       3       3(4 + pv)2

                                                     20
Completing first in x leaves 3χq 2 /(4 + pv); completing next in F leaves 3χ/(4 + pv) − χ2 /12 =
4d(9−d)/[3(4+pv)2 ]. The latter coefficient is at least 32d/[3(4+N )2 ]. It controls dq 2 polynomially.
The F -square then controls F 2 , since χ2 q 2 ≤ dq 2 . The transverse gradient coefficient is at least
three, and the shifted M -square controls M with an additional factor (1 + N )2 . The squared
d-norm of the shift in the x-square is χ2 vq 2 /(16d) ≤ dq 2 /16, so it also controls dx2 . Finally
|T |2 = |T tf |2 + (F − χq)2 /2. This proves (4.12). If dt = 0, the sole cross term is −χF q/2. Using
|χF q|/2 ≤ χq 2 /4 + χF 2 /4 and 0 < χ ≤ 1 proves (4.13).

    The scalar identity separates curvature from divergence. At the inner boundary we must also
relate this divergence flux to mean curvature. Differentiating the definition of Z gives, wherever
df ̸= 0,
                          4 dt + 12 d log D = 4 dZ = (4 + pv) dt + v d log σ.               (4.15)
This relation is smooth in its direction-free form and will be used only in contractions that extend
across df = 0.
Lemma 4.3 (Boundary identity). On a face where f is constant,

                             Vν /u = d(H + 4∂ν t) − H + wν (F − PB ).                           (4.16)
                                                     √
Its mean curvature in ĝ, with normal into Ω, is e−2t d (H + 4∂ν t).
Proof. The tangential Hessian is Hess f |T B = (∂ν f ) IIg . Thus tr⊥ H f = wν H and χq = F − PB −
wν H. The normal component of (4.8), using (4.15), is

                       Vν /u = 4d∂ν t + χwν q = 4d∂ν t + wν (F − PB ) − vH.
                 √                             √
The ḡ-normal is d ν. Its mean curvature is √d H, because the tangential metric is unchanged
and its normal first variation is multiplied by d. The conformal mean-curvature formula gives
the asserted mean curvature for ĝ.

4.3   The source, boundary condition, and continuation path
We now prescribe the divergence and inner flux. For the original system, the source is chosen
to give the deformed metric nonnegative scalar curvature. A penalty near the lower bound will
exclude contact with that bound along the continuation path.
    Choose 3/4 < γ < 1 and fixed positive smooth weights

                                      ρ0 ≍ r−4 ,        ρ1 ≍ r−2γ ,

with differentiated end bounds. Let m : R → [0, 1] be smooth, equal to one on (−∞, 0] and zero
on [1, ∞), and set
                         m0 (t) = m(N (t + ϵ)),     P = CN (ρ0 + vρ1 ).
The positive δ0 is fixed independently of N, R in Proposition 4.9; thereafter CN is a sufficiently
large polynomial constant. Define

                                  Ξ = δ0 T + ρ + δ0 τ aσ − m0 (t)P.                             (4.17)

Choose a smooth face function NB > 1 + supB |H|. The original second equation and its inner
condition are
                    div V = uΞ,    Vν /u = −H + wν (F − PB ) − NB d.                 (4.18)

                                                   21
Lemma 4.3 gives H + 4∂ν t = −NB . Only −ϵ ≤ t ≤ −ϵ + 1/N is penalized in the admissible range.
Since N > 2/ϵ, this band has negative t, and

                 ℓ ≤ l ≤ eℓ,       cℓ ≤ L ≤ Cℓ    on the admissible penalty band.            (4.19)

The latter constants can be uniform for 0 < ϵ < 1.
Proposition 4.4 (Specified homotopy). There is a continuous piecewise smooth family of systems
indexed by s ∈ [0, 4], beginning at (4.7), (4.18) and the original Dirichlet values, and ending at
f = 0 and the scalar problem with zero source and zero inner conormal flux. Throughout its first
three stages the source is (4.17), with T evaluated using the actual trace F . Each trace equation
has right-side derivative at least one in h, with x, Z, ∇f fixed.
Proof. Fix a smooth j : R → [0, 1] which vanishes for t ≤ 0 and equals one for t ≥ 1. For
0 ≤ λ ≤ 1 put

                Vλ = uAχ (4∇Z + λK(w, ·)),           B = −H + wν (F − PB ) − NB d.

Before the last stage use the boundary condition

                               (Vλ )ν /u = [1 − (1 − λ)j(t)]
                                                                                             (4.20)
                                           · [B − (1 − λ)(Aχ K(w, ·))ν ].

Outer values remain f = Z = 0 throughout.
    We specify two smooth collar modifications. Let νs = ∇s/|∇s| be the outward unit normal to
a collar leaf; the leaf coordinate s here is local and is separate from the homotopy parameter.
Choose disjoint collar cutoffs equal to one at the faces. Take η > 0 so small that b− + 3η < b+ .
A black contribution to D0 (x, w, h) is a negative constant times a collar cutoff, times a smooth
directional cutoff supported where w · νs < −1/2 and equal to one at −1, times a nonincreasing
height cutoff equal to one below b− + η and zero above b− + 2η. A white contribution has positive
sign, its directional cutoff supported where w · νs > 1/2 and equal to one at 1, and a nondecreasing
height cutoff equal to zero below b+ − 2η and one above b+ − η. Decrease η to separate these
ranges. Their sum D0 is smooth, bounded, supported in the collars, and nondecreasing in h. It is
nonpositive for h ≤ b− + η, nonnegative for h ≥ b+ − η, and vanishes on black outward and white
inward directions, for every length 0 ≤ |w| ≤ 1.
    Choose the magnitudes larger than 2 supB |H|+1. Equation (4.1) then gives, for F = h+C+D0
at the assigned boundary height b = b± ,

                        F (x, +ν, b) ≥ PB + H,         F (x, −ν, b) ≤ PB − H.                (4.21)

These are limiting directional evaluations at a = 1, χ = 0. The compatible direction has equality;
D0 corrects the other one. Its derivatives on bounded height ranges are independent of N . Choose
a smooth compactly supported extension b(x), constant equal to b± on each smaller collar. On
these collars let D1 (x, w) = A1 w · νs , extended by a collar cutoff, with A1 > supB |H| + 1. Then
D1 (x, 0) = 0 and D1 (x, ±ν) = ±A1 on B.
    The four stages are the following.
  (i) For 0 ≤ s ≤ 1, set λ = 1 − s, keep F = h + C and h|B = b, and use div Vλ = uΞ and (4.20).

 (ii) For 1 ≤ s ≤ 2, set λ = 0, α = s − 1, retain h|B = b, and replace the trace right side by
      F = h + C + αD0 (x, w, h). Keep the same scalar equation and boundary prescription.

                                                  22
 (iii) For 2 ≤ s ≤ 3, set λ = 0, ζ = s − 2, prescribe h|B = (1 − ζ)b, and use

                               F = h + (1 − ζ)[C + D0 (x, w, h + ζb(x))]
                                                                                              (4.22)
                                     + ζ[trAχ K + D1 (x, w)].

      Keep the same scalar equation and boundary prescription. At a face, h + ζb = b. The value
      at w = ±ν is the convex combination of the preceding directional value and PB + D1 (x, ±ν),
      so (4.21) persists.

 (iv) For 3 ≤ s ≤ 4, retain the trace problem at ζ = 1 and multiply both Ξ and the right side of
      (4.20) by ηs = 4 − s.

The prescriptions agree at the common endpoints. Their h-derivatives are 1, 1 + α∂h D0 , or
1 + (1 − ζ)∂h D0 , all at least one.
    At ζ = 1 the trace equation is trAχ H f = h + D1 (x, w) with zero Dirichlet values. At a positive
interior maximum of f , one has w = 0, Aχ = I, D1 = 0, and l∆f = τ f > 0, a contradiction. A
negative minimum is excluded in the same way. Thus f = 0 and t = Z in the last stage. At s = 4
the remaining equation is div(4L(Z)∇Z) = 0, with zero inner conormal flux and Z = 0 on SR .
Multiplication by Z and integration give Z = 0. For the fixed-point construction in Section 6,
the final linear scalar problem has zero source and zero inner flux for every input; consequently
that last map has output identically zero.

4.4   Elementary height and end barriers
Lemma 4.5 (Height control). Every smooth trace solution in Proposition 4.4 satisfies |h| ≤ C0 ,
independently of N, R, the homotopy parameter, and the input Z. Its actual right side satisfies
|F | ≤ CF with the same independence.

Proof. The collar modifications vanish at w = 0. At an interior extremum the trace equation
reads either tr K + l∆f = h + C or tr K + l∆f = h + (1 − ζ)C + ζ tr K. The maximum and
minimum inequalities bound h by fixed bounds for | tr K| + |C|. Boundary values range between
b− , 0, b+ . This proves the height bound without using the floor. Boundedness of D0 , D1 and
| trAχ K| ≤ 3|K| proves the assertion for F .

Lemma 4.6 (End height and outer boundary). There are r0 , C > 0, independent of N, R and
the homotopy parameter, such that for R ≥ 2r0 ,

                               |h| ≤ C(r−γ − R−γ )     (r0 ≤ r ≤ R).                          (4.23)

In particular,
                                   |∇f | ≤ CR−1−γ /τ     on SR .                              (4.24)
For fixed ϵ, N , a sufficiently large lower bound for R gives t > −ϵ on SR .

Proof. Take r0 outside the supports of K, C, D0 , D1 . There F = h in the first three stages, and
h = 0 in the last one. For ψ = r−γ , with axis ∇ψ/|∇ψ|, the controlled end gives, uniformly for
0 < χ ≤ 1,
                      trAχ Hess ψ = γ((γ + 1)χ − 2)r−γ−2 + O(r−γ−3 ) < 0
after enlarging r0 . At a comparison contact, coefficients
                                                  √        use the given Z and the common gradient
of test and solution. The positive factor l/(τ D) preserves this sign. Height monotonicity

                                                 23
therefore makes C(ψ − R−γ ) an upper barrier and its negative a lower barrier. Choose C to
dominate C0 on r = r0 for every R ≥ 2r0 . Differentiating at the common outer value gives (4.24);
tangential derivatives there vanish.
    At SR , Z = 0. The monotonicity of its defining expression implies t > −ϵ if ℓ2 |∇f |2 < e8ϵ − 1.
It suffices to require C 2 ℓ2 τ −2 R−2−2γ < e8ϵ − 1, which is a permissible lower bound depending on
fixed N, ϵ.

4.5    Exclusion of a contact with the floor
We exclude a proposed floor contact using the equations at that point. These estimates require
neither an upper bound for Z nor a bound for |∇f |.

Lemma 4.8 (Homotopy errors at a floor contact). There is a fixed compact set K ⊂ Ω containing
