Proposition 4.9 (Floor exclusion). Choose δ0 as in Lemma 4.8. There is a polynomial choice
CN = C(1 + N )A in the penalty such that, for all sufficiently large N and all R ≥ Rfloor (N, ϵ),
no smooth solution anywhere along Proposition 4.4 can satisfy t ≥ −ϵ and attain t = −ϵ. In
particular every such admissible solution has t > −ϵ on ΩR .
Proof. Choose Rfloor to include the requirement of Lemma 4.6. A floor contact cannot occur on
SR . In the first three stages, an inner contact has j(t) = 0. Equation (4.20) then cancels the
omitted flux on both sides and gives Vν /u = B. Equation (4.16) forces H + 4∂ν t = −NB , so
∂ν t < 0. This contradicts the nonnegative derivative into ΩR at a boundary minimum.
     Consider an interior contact. Let EN = C(1 + N )A [1K + v(ρ0 + ρ1 )] denote the error in
(4.30). Because ρ0 has a positive minimum on K, 1K ≤ CK ρ0 . Also vρ0 ≤ ρ0 , and ρ/ρ0 is globally
bounded. We can therefore choose a polynomial CN so large that, pointwise for all 0 ≤ v ≤ 1,
                                        P ≥ EN + 2ρ + ρ0 .                                   (4.35)
At the floor m0 = 1, so the scalar equation and the lower estimate give
                               0 ≥ δ0 T + (1 − δ0 )τ aσ + P − EN − ρ
                                 ≥ ρ + ρ0 > 0,
a contradiction. Enlarging CN below preserves this inequality and preserves its polynomial
dependence.
    In the last stage f = 0, t = Z, u = L, and v = 0. At an interior minimum, T =
(|K|2 + (tr K)2 )/2. Increase CN polynomially if necessary so that δ0 T + ρ − CN ρ0 < 0 at all
such points. For ηs > 0, the scalar equation at a floor minimum has left side 4L∆t ≥ 0 and
strictly negative right side. At an inner floor minimum its flux is 4∂ν t = ηs (−H − NB ) < 0, also
impossible. If ηs = 0, multiplication of div(4L(t)∇t) = 0 by t, with zero outer value and zero
inner flux, gives t = 0. Thus the last stage has no floor contact either.

   We have excluded floor contact for every smooth admissible solution along the specified
homotopy. Section 5 supplies the remaining a priori bounds, and Section 6 proves existence. The
choice of P uses only polynomial losses in N ; it is independent of all fixed-N Schauder constants.

                                                 26
5     A priori estimates for the deformation
We bound the height, boundary slopes, and conformal variable before using classical regularity.
Fix the prepared exterior of Proposition 3.7, its thresholds and collars, and ϵ > 0. We use the
variables and four stages of Proposition 4.4. A solution is smooth on a finite truncation and
satisfies t ≥ −ϵ, so the estimates include putative floor-contact solutions. No Hessian bound or
upper bound for Z is assumed.
    A quantity denoted by ΠN is bounded by C(1 + N )A , with C, A independent of the truncation,
the homotopy parameter, and N . It can increase at successive uses. The fixed data, collars, and
ϵ can enter C, A. A constant denoted by C(N ) can depend arbitrarily on these fixed data and on
N, ℓ, τ ; it is still independent of the truncation and the homotopy parameter. Constants explicitly
described as fixed do not depend on N . Recall that
                                                                             4d
           ℓ = e−ϵN ,        τ = ℓ3/2 ,     ℓ ≤ l ≤ e,     0 ≤ p ≤ N,           ≤ χ ≤ d.        (5.1)
                                                                            4+N
In particular ΠN τ /ℓ → 0. This small factor will absorb only terms whose constants are polynomial.

5.1   Sublevels and exclusion of boundary layers
The limiting height will be only lower semicontinuous. We first show how an expansion bound on
its lower tests controls every exterior support of a closed sublevel, including a plateau. This local
step will also apply to the four-dimensional constructions.

Lemma 5.1 (Exterior supports of a closed sublevel). Let U be an open subset of a smooth
Riemannian manifold of dimension d ∈ {3, 4}, let Q be a smooth symmetric tensor on U , and let
u : U → R be finite and lower semicontinuous. Fix k < k ′ and q0 ∈ R. A smooth lower test of u
at x is a smooth function ϕ with ϕ(x) = u(x) and ϕ ≤ u near x. Suppose every such test with
u(x) < k ′ and dϕ(x) ̸= 0 satisfies
                                        EQ [ϕ](x) ≤ q0 ,
where EQ is the expansion in Equation (3.12) of the regular level through x, oriented toward
increasing ϕ. Equivalently, subtract ϕ(x) before applying that formula. Then every smooth exterior
support of the relatively closed set Dk = {x ∈ U : u(x) ≤ k}, at a frontier point in U , has
expansion at most q0 .

Proof. For integers j ≥ 1 define
                                                             −1
                        Sj (s) = 1 + exp[−j 2 (s − k − j −1 )]     ,   vj = Sj ◦ u.             (5.2)

These functions are lower semicontinuous and take values in (0, 1). Their lower relaxed limit
lim inf j→∞, y→x vj (y) is the function Ik (x) equal to zero on Dk and one on its complement. Indeed,
at x ∈ Dk the constant sequence gives vj (x) ≤ Sj (k) → 0, while vj ≥ 0 everywhere. If u(x) > k,
lower semicontinuity gives a neighborhood on which u ≥ k + δ for some δ > 0, and vj → 1
uniformly there.
    Let ϕ define an exterior support of Dk at x: ϕ(x) = 0, dϕ(x) ̸= 0, and ϕ ≤ 0 on Dk near x.
Shrink to a small normal-coordinate ball compactly contained in U on which |ϕ| < 1/2. Then ϕ
is a lower test of Ik at x. Subtract a small positive multiple of the fourth power of the distance
from x to make this contact strict without changing its two-jet; write the resulting function as ϕ0 .
Minimize vj − ϕ0 on the closed ball. The preceding relaxed-limit description, the strict contact,

                                                    27
and vj (x) → 0 show that the minimizers xj lie in its interior for large j, satisfy xj → x, and have
contact values vj (xj ) → 0. Thus ϕ0 + aj is a lower test of vj at xj , where aj → 0.
   For each fixed large j, restrict to a neighborhood of xj on which ϕ0 + aj takes values in (0, 1).
The increasing function Sj−1 makes Sj−1 (ϕ0 + aj ) a smooth lower test of u. Its contact height is

                                                         vj (xj )
                        u(xj ) = k + j −1 + j −2 log                ≤ k + j −1 < k ′
                                                       1 − vj (xj )

for all sufficiently large j. Its gradient is nonzero, since dϕ0 (xj ) → dϕ(x) ̸= 0. An increasing
smooth composition preserves the oriented level expansion: it preserves the unit normal, and for
θ′ > 0,

                                                            tr( dϕ0 )⊥ Hess(θ ◦ ϕ0 )   tr( dϕ0 )⊥ Hess ϕ0
     Hess(θ ◦ ϕ0 ) = θ′ Hess ϕ0 + θ′′ dϕ0 ⊗ dϕ0 ,                                    =                    .
                                                                  | d(θ ◦ ϕ0 )|              | dϕ0 |

The hypothesis therefore gives EQ [ϕ0 + aj ](xj ) ≤ q0 . Letting j → ∞ yields EQ [ϕ](x) ≤ q0 , because
ϕ0 and ϕ have the same two-jet at x.

   For notation in this section, let Bc be the full closed maximal black region at threshold c,
and let Wc be the corresponding white region with Bcb held fixed. These are the families used in
Proposition 3.7.

Lemma 5.2 (Uniform separation from the wrong height). In the first two homotopy stages, the
following holds. If Q ⊂ Ω̄ is compact and disjoint from Bcb , there are ηQ > 0 and NQ such that

                                            h ≥ b− + ηQ       on Q                                            (5.3)

for every N ≥ NQ , every sufficiently large allowed truncation, and every admissible or floor-contact
solution in those stages. If Q is disjoint from Wcw , the analogous conclusion is h ≤ b+ − ηQ . The
compact sets are relative to the full closure: they may contain faces of the opposite color.

Proof. We prove the lower assertion. It is enough to consider an arbitrary sequence Ni → ∞,
Ri → ∞ and solutions in either of the first two stages. The elementary height and end estimates
in Lemmas 4.5 and 4.6 give

               |hi | ≤ C0 ,        |hi | ≤ C(r−γ − Ri−γ )    on the end,        3/4 < γ < 1.                  (5.4)

Near each white face extend hi to the other side by the constant b+ . Extend the fixed geometric
coefficients smoothly there. The extended functions are continuous. Define the lower relaxed limit

                              h(x) = lim inf{hi (y) : i ≥ j, dist(x, y) < 1/j}.
                                     j→∞

It is lower semicontinuous, bounded, and satisfies the limiting end bound. No equicontinuity of
hi is asserted.
    Choose b− < k ′ < min{0, b− + η}, where η is the low-height sign range of the added term D0 .
Suppose that a smooth function ϕ touches h from below at x, with h(x) ≤ k ′ and dϕ(x) ̸= 0.
Exclude for the moment the black faces. Subtracting a small fourth-order localization term from
ϕ makes the contact strict without changing its two-jet. Minimization on a small closed ball then
produces, after passing to a subsequence, points xi → x and constants ci → 0 such that ϕ + ci
touches hi from below at xi , and hi (xi ) → h(x). The boundary of this ball cannot contain xi by

                                                     28
strictness. Nor can xi lie on a white face or on the extended side: their values there are b+ > k ′ .
Thus every sufficiently large i uses the interior trace equation.
    At these contacts,

                                               τi2
             ∇fi = τi−1 ∇ϕ,       di ≤                  −→ 0,     ai −→ 1,    χi −→ 0.
                                         ℓ2i |dϕ(xi )|2
                    √
Here ai = li |∇fi |/ Di is the deformation variable. Positivity of Aχi and the Hessian inequality
at a lower contact imply
                                             l
                              trAχi K +     √i trAχi Hess ϕ ≤ Fi (xi ).
                                          τi Di
In the second stage D0 ≤ 0 at these values; in the first stage it is absent. Consequently
lim sup Fi (xi ) ≤ k ′ + Cb . Passing to the limit gives

                               tr(dϕ)⊥ Hess ϕ
                                              + tr(dϕ)⊥ K ≤ k ′ + Cb .                          (5.5)
                                    |dϕ|

This is the expansion of the level surface of ϕ, oriented toward increasing ϕ. The only Hessian
used in this passage is the fixed test Hessian. Removing the localization proves the assertion for
non-strict contacts as well.
   Fix b− < k < k ′ and put Ek = {h ≤ k}. Apply Lemma 5.1 with u = h, tensor Q = K, and
q0 = k ′ + Cb . The function is finite and lower semicontinuous, and Equation (5.5) supplies the
required bound for every nonzero-gradient lower test below k ′ . We apply the lemma locally away
from black faces, using the constant extensions to obtain open neighborhoods across white faces.
Every exterior support of Ek there consequently has expansion at most k ′ + Cb , with no regularity
assumption on the sublevel.
   This argument also rules out a hidden layer at a white face. At every point strictly on the
added side, the relaxed limit equals b+ > k. Thus Ek lies locally in the original closed exterior,
and the reversed white face is an exterior support of Ek at any contact there, with expansion

                                      −HB + PB = −cw > 0.

For k ′ sufficiently close to b− , this is strictly greater than k ′ + Cb . The high extension ensured
that all preceding low-value tests came from the interior equation, so the support contradiction
applies at the face itself. Hence Ek avoids every white face. The end estimate makes Ek compact.
    Adjoin the entire closed region Bcb . At a point on its smooth black frontier, every exterior
support of the union also contains the black region locally. The tangency comparison therefore
bounds its expansion by cb < k ′ + Cb . At all other frontier points the preceding sublevel argument
applies. The resulting compact closed set contains the required original inner barrier with a
collar, because the black region already does. It avoids the outer barriers. Lemma 3.6 places
it in a smooth black trapped region at any threshold c′′ > k ′ + Cb sufficiently close to cb . It is
consequently contained in Bc′′ .
    Finally let Q be as in the lemma. Hausdorff right continuity at cb gives a δ > 0 such that
Bcb +δ ∩ Q = ∅; decrease δ so that the enlarged threshold is still negative and all barriers remain
strict. Choose k, k ′ , c′′ with

                              b− < k < k ′ ,      k ′ + Cb < c′′ < cb + δ.


                                                   29
The construction implies Ek ∩ Q = ∅ for every sequence under consideration. If the uniform
estimate h > k on Q failed, choose violating solutions with Ni → ∞, Ri → ∞ and xi ∈ Q,
hi (xi ) ≤ k. A subsequence has xi → x ∈ Q and h(x) ≤ k, a contradiction. This gives (5.3), after
decreasing its positive margin.
    For the upper assertion apply the entire argument to (−h, −K, −C), interchanging colors. The
reversed black faces have strictly positive white expansion −cb . Their exclusion and compactness
give positive distance from the black region before applying the white version of Lemma 3.6.
Adjoining the full white region is therefore legitimate in the fixed black complement. Empty
white regions and missing face types cause no change: only an actually nonempty sublevel would
supply a trapped seed and hence a contradiction.

    For the truncation quantifier, start with any Rmin (N ) → ∞ that satisfies the outer estimates
of Section 4, and enlarge it when necessary. Failure of any asserted estimate for arbitrarily large
N and R ≥ Rmin (N ) supplies exactly the sequence excluded above. Only finitely many compact
sets will be needed below, so their thresholds can be chosen simultaneously.
    The weak-set argument has kept low heights near black faces and high heights near white
faces. We now sharpen this separation into a nonzero signed boundary slope. That slope makes
the otherwise uncontrolled inner flux small enough for the mass estimate.

5.2   Signed collar barriers
Lemma 5.3 (Boundary slopes). There are fixed constants c, C∗ > 0 such that, for sufficiently
large N and the allowed truncations, the first two stages satisfy

                              c/τ ≤ ∂ν f ≤ C∗ /τ      on black faces,
                                                                                               (5.6)
                              c/τ ≤ −∂ν f ≤ C∗ /τ     on white faces.

During the third stage one still has |∂ν f | ≤ C∗ /τ . On every fixed compact region needed for the
final scalar-curvature estimate, the first two stages satisfy b− ≤ h ≤ b+ .
Proof. Write s for the smooth leaf parameter in an outward collar and νs = ∇s/|ds|. All geometric
coefficients of this fixed collar are bounded, with |ds| bounded above and below. At a comparison
contact with h0 = b− + ψ(s), ψ ′ > 0, the shared gradient gives

                            τ2
                     d ≤ C 2 ′ 2,        1 − a ≤ d,       N χ ≥ c1 d    (N ≥ 4).               (5.7)
                          ℓ (ψ )
If the slopes are in a fixed positive interval, the first bound is d ≤ Cℓ. None of these comparisons
uses an upper bound for Z.
    The trace operator on this test is
                            l
                                           
                  trAχ K + √ Hess h0
                          τ D
                                                    Hess s(νs , νs )       ψ ′′
                                                                                  
                     = Ps + aHs + χK(νs , νs ) + aχ                  + |ds| ′ ,                (5.8)
                                                        |ds|               ψ
where Ps is the tangential trace of K on the leaf. Thus it is the leaf expansion Hs + Ps , with an
error of absolute value at most Cd, plus the displayed logarithmic-slope term. The same formula
for a negative slope has a replaced by −a in the Hessian terms. This is an evaluation at the
contact variables; it does not differentiate the unknown t.

                                                30
    On a black collar Hs + Ps ≥ cb and C = Cb − kB s. Choose small positive slopes so that
ψ(0) = 0 and ψ(s) ≤ kB s/2. At the outer end of a sufficiently short fixed collar, Lemma 5.2 lets
this test lie below the solution. In the compatible direction the add-on D0 vanishes. The residual
of the trace equation on the test is therefore at least
                                                                      ψ ′′
                                           kB s/2 − Cd + aχ|ds|            .                     (5.9)
                                                                      ψ′
Choose a smooth nonnegative βN (s) equal to AN for 0 ≤ s ≤ 1/N , supported in 0 ≤ s ≤ 2/N ,
and satisfying βN ≤ 2A. Define
              R

                                                     Z s                      
                                       ′
                                  ψ (s) = m exp                   βN (q) dq .
                                                          0

Choose the fixed A large enough that the final term of (5.9) dominates Cd on s ≤ 1/N , using
(5.7) and a ≥ 1/2 there. On s ≥ 1/N , the first term dominates Cd ≤ Cℓ for large N ; the taper
contributes with the good sign. Since the slope multiplier is at most e2A , a fixed sufficiently small
m > 0 enforces all the previous small-slope requirements. This constructs a strict lower barrier
with slopes bounded above and below independently of N .
    An upper barrier from b− is constructed in the same collar with
                                                         Z s                  
                                   ′
                                 ψ (s) = M exp −                   βN (q) dq .
                                                              0

The smooth leaf expansion differs from its boundary value by O(s). Choose the fixed M so large
that ψ(s) ≥ C2 s dominates this variation, the offset variation, and the height bound at the outer
collar end. The logarithmic-slope term is now negative. On s ≤ 1/N it dominates Cd; on the rest
of the collar the negative margin proportional to s does so. The upper barrier is strict at every
possible positive contact. Its slopes lie in [M e−2A , M ], an interval independent of N .
    To check the comparison signs, at a negative interior minimum of h − h0 the Hessian of h
dominates that of h0 , the gradients and Z agree, and hence so do t, l, Aχ . The right side is
strictly increasing in the height. Thus a strict positive residual for a lower barrier contradicts the
equation. At a positive maximum the inequalities reverse, proving the upper-barrier assertion.
Taking the one-sided derivative at s = 0 proves the black part of (5.6). Replacing (h, K, C) by
(−h, −K, −C) proves the white part.
    In the third stage the boundary height is the assigned interpolated constant, say bζ . Construct
tests bζ + ψ(s) and bζ − ψ(s) with large positive ψ ′ and (log ψ ′ )′ = −βN . At s = 0, the two
directional inequalities of Proposition 4.4 say precisely that the positive-slope test has nonpositive
limiting residual and the negative-slope test nonnegative limiting residual. At every comparison
contact, the fixed positive barrier slope and Equation (5.7) give d ≤ Cℓ, so a ≥ 1/2 for large N .
On 1/2 ≤ |w| ≤ 1, the first w-derivatives of Aχ in Equation (4.5) are bounded independently
of 0 ≤ p ≤ N . The other collar terms have fixed smooth coefficients on the bounded height
range. Thus, at fixed boundary height the coefficient and geometric errors are O(s + d + χ),
since |w − (±νs )| = 1 − a ≤ d and the radial comparison segment stays in this range. Changing
the test height supplies, by height monotonicity, a residual of magnitude at least ψ(s) with the
required sign. Large fixed slopes dominate the O(s) error and the outer-end height bound. The
logarithmic-slope term has the required sign for both tests, as follows from (5.8); it dominates the
O(d) error on the initial 1/N interval. The remaining margin dominates it beyond that interval.
This proves the two-sided upper derivative bound, without using sublevel exclusion in the third
stage.

                                                     31
    Finally, on each own-color collar the lower barrier just constructed gives the required one-sided
height bound. On the rest of a fixed compact region use Lemma 5.2; the opposite height bound
on an own-color collar follows from that lemma as well, since the closed collar is disjoint from the
other region. A finite cover proves b− ≤ h ≤ b+ on the compact regions in the statement.

5.3   A trace estimate with polynomial constants
The collar trace estimate must have polynomial constants: it will control the boundary flux both
here and in the mass estimate. We therefore prove it before comparing with the ordinary product
graph.

5.5   Sobolev inequality and iteration on the ordinary graph
The high-level estimate gives integral control, but degree requires a pointwise upper bound for
Z. The graph Sobolev inequality and iteration supply that bound. The earlier polynomial trace
estimate, not the constants in this iteration, will control the mass limit.
    The comparisons in this subsection are uniform in the solution, R, and the homotopy parameter,
but may depend arbitrarily on N . If d0 = (1 + σ 2 )−1 , then

                                                          d     1 + σ2

therefore compares Ad0 , Ad , Aχ without any bound for Z.

and Hess Z ≤ 0. The outer boundary value is zero. The compact bound on that fixed sphere
consequently extends the first-stage upper bound to the whole truncation.

5.6   The remaining stages and the bounded-variable conclusion
In the second and third stages the drift is zero. Their source has the same high-Z positivity as
(5.15), with a uniform threshold for the bounded homotopy range. By Lemma 5.3, on an inner
face
                        Z = t + 18 log(1 + l2 σ 2 ) ≤ t + 81 log(1 + e2 C∗2 /τ 2 ).       (5.26)
Thus sufficiently high boundary Z forces j(t) = 1. The prescribed boundary flux is then zero,
since λ = 0. At the face Aχ ν = χν, so this is exactly ∂ν Z = 0. The maximum principle excludes

                                                          36
a high interior maximum, and the boundary point principle excludes a high maximum on a
smooth inner face with this zero conormal derivative. Both principles are applied to the smooth
individual solution on its finite domain; its coefficients are positive definite there. Locating the
maximum requires no uniform ellipticity constant. The outer value is zero, so these two stages
also have a uniform upper bound.
    In the fourth stage f = 0 and t = Z. When the common source/flux scale is positive, the
same maximum and boundary argument works with its positive high-level source. At scale zero
the homogeneous mixed problem and the outer zero Dirichlet value give Z = 0. The upper bound
is consequently uniform also as the scale tends to zero.
Proposition 5.6 (Bounds before regularity). Fix the prepared data and ϵ > 0. There are N0 and
Rmin (N ) → ∞ such that, for each fixed N ≥ N0 and every R ≥ Rmin (N ), all smooth solutions of
the four-stage homotopy with t ≥ −ϵ satisfy

             |h| ≤ C0 ,     |f | ≤ C0 /τ,    −ϵ ≤ t ≤ Z ≤ C(N ),          |∇f | ≤ C(N ).      (5.27)

The constants are uniform in R and in the homotopy parameter. They include putative floor-
contact solutions, which are then excluded by Proposition 4.9. The fixed signed slopes, the
third-stage upper slopes, and the polynomial trace estimates are those of Lemmas 5.3 and 5.4.
Proof. It remains to extract the bounds for the conformal variable and the gradient. Since
Z = t + log D/8 and D ≥ 1, the floor gives −ϵ ≤ t ≤ Z. The upper bound for Z gives

                                    e8(Z−t) − 1
                             σ2 =               ≤ ℓ−2 e8(C(N )+ϵ) − 1 .
                                                                     
                                        l 2

This proves the asserted fixed-N gradient bound. The height bounds are Lemma 4.5. The finite
collection of sublevel and collar estimates and the polynomial absorption choose N0 once. Enlarge
Rmin (N ) to include the outer estimates and all fixed compact patches used above. The next
section derives Hessian and continuity estimates from these bounds and uses them to prove
existence.


6    Solving the three-dimensional boundary deformation
The boundary estimates have bounded f , Z and Df for every admissible solution of the contin-
uation equations. We now turn those bounds into existence. Two points need attention. The
divergence equation for Z contains |D2 f |2 , so the bounded variables do not immediately give
a classical elliptic estimate. Also, a compact solution map must be defined on an open set of
arbitrary inputs, before imposing the admissibility inequality t > −ϵ.
    We address these points in order. A gradient-aligned scalar equation gives both continuity
of Df and a local integral bound for D2 f . An oscillation argument then gives continuity of Z,
after which ordinary boundary regularity applies. Separately, a scalar Dirichlet problem can be
solved for every bounded input Z, even when its associated t lies below the floor. Freezing that
scalar solution in the divergence equation produces the compact map. The floor exclusion and
the preceding estimates keep its fixed points inside the admissible set along the homotopy of
Proposition 4.4.
    All regularity constants in this section may depend on the fixed deformation parameters N , ϵ
and τ . They are not used in any earlier argument requiring polynomial dependence on N . The
estimates needed for exhaustion will be uniform in the outer radius R at fixed N .

                                                 37
6.1   From a bounded gradient to a Hessian integral bound
The first equation has two equal transverse eigenvalues; its remaining eigenvector is the gradient
of its solution. This structure gives a gradient estimate before the axial eigenvalue is continuous.
The accompanying Hessian estimate will control the source in the second equation.
    In the next two lemmas, D denotes coordinate derivatives; it is not the scalar 1 + l2 |∇f |2 from
the deformation. Balls are Euclidean coordinate balls, and a boundary ball is their intersection
with one side of a smooth boundary. A family of boundary patches has bounded geometry here if
normal-coordinate charts, their inverses, the metric and its inverse, and the derivatives through
the orders used below have fixed bounds. We only apply the lemmas to smooth metrics and
smooth boundaries. Their doubled metrics will be Lipschitz and smooth on each side of a single
plane.

Reflection at a constant Dirichlet face. Subtract the boundary value and use normal
coordinates with the face given by x3 = 0. Reflect the metric across this plane and reflect z oddly.
Tangential derivatives of z vanish on the face, so the extension is C 1 . There is no distributional

Equation (6.6) hold with its essential first-derivative bound.

                             (Hess z − ∆z g)∇z ν = −(trT Hess z) ∂ν z.                        (6.26)

Because the boundary value is constant, trT Hess z is its mean-curvature coefficient times ∂ν z.

so that the following divergence is Euclidean. Such a signed density a(x′ ) δ{x3 =0} is the divergence
of the bounded field a(x′ )1{x3 >0} ∂3 . Thus its negative part is another small divergence error in
Equation (6.10). Smooth-side curvature errors are O(r2 ). Weak Harnack and the low-truncation
argument remain valid, and the flatness comparison uses the same Lipschitz metric estimates.
This proves all the preceding alternatives for balls meeting the reflection plane as well.

on a smaller ball: its gradient bound decreases, or its approximation by a nonconstant affine
function improves. We choose the constants once so that either route can be repeated. Keep

for this fixed b∗ . Normalize initially by a fixed multiple of L + Q and choose a uniformly small
initial radius so that the metric and forcing errors satisfy all the preceding smallness conditions.
If the first case of Equation (6.13) holds, rescale the ball by r∗ and the gradient bound by k∗ . The
normalized forcing decreases by r∗ /k∗ < 1. Repeating this step either continues forever or reaches
the second case. In the former case the gradient decays with exponent log k∗ / log r∗ . In the latter
case apply Equation (6.25) successively, retaining exponent α2 . At each affine step, rescale z by
dividing by the spatial scale, subtracting only an additive constant; this preserves its gradient
bound. Its slope changes have total size at most Cb∗ /(1 − λα0 2 ), so the slopes remain in [1/4, 4].

6.2   Continuity in the presence of a quadratic source
The preceding lemma controls |D2 f |2 by its mass on small balls. We next show why that control
suffices for the Z-equation. An exponential change of variable absorbs its quadratic gradient
term. The remaining measure has a small Green potential, and weak Harnack then decreases the
oscillation. We allow a measure source so that the argument also covers the inner boundary after
reflection.



                                                 42
                                                                                        1,2
Lemma 6.2 (Natural growth with a measure source). Let n ∈ {3, 4} and let Z ∈ L∞ ∩ Wloc      on
a ball in Rn . Fix a Borel representative with |Z| ≤ M for products with measures. Suppose, in
distributions, that
                                        div(aDZ + b) = e,                               (6.29)
where a is measurable and symmetric, λI ≤ a ≤ ΛI for 0 < λ ≤ Λ, |b| ≤ B, and e is a signed
measure satisfying
               |e| ≤ C0 (1 + |DZ|2 ) dx + µ,      µ(Br (x)) ≤ Cµ rn−2+β ,         β > 0.       (6.30)
Here µ is a positive measure, and its bound holds at every center and all sufficiently small radii in
a larger patch. Assume the weak exponential product rule (6.31) below is valid with the specified
representative. This is true, in particular, for a function continuous across a flat interface and
smooth on its two closed sides, when the equation includes the normal-flux jump. Then the Sobolev
class of Z has a Hölder representative on smaller balls, with constants depending only on the
displayed bounds and patch separation. If Z is already continuous, this is a uniform estimate for
that function. The conclusion applies up to a smooth face whenever its reflected equation satisfies
these hypotheses.
Proof. The proof has two steps. An exponential change absorbs the quadratic term in DZ; a
small potential correction then permits weak Harnack to decrease the essential oscillation. Both
steps work in the two dimensions stated in the lemma. Restricting all radii to at most one, we
may replace β by min{β, 1} in the measure bound. We retain the symbol β for this positive
reduced exponent; this also makes the small-scale mollification bound below immediate.
   Write L = − div(aD) and, for s ∈ {−1, 1}, set Vs = eskZ . The assumed product rule is
                     LVs = div(skVs b) − skVs e − k 2 Vs aDZ · DZ + b · DZ .                   (6.31)
                                                                                 

The term Vs e uses the fixed bounded representative. For a continuous piecewise smooth function
the identity follows by integration on the two sides; its common trace multiplies the flux jump.
Choose k ≥ max{1, 4C0 /λ} and use B|DZ| ≤ (λ/2)|DZ|2 + B 2 /(2λ). The gradient-square term
from e is absorbed by the negative quadratic term for both signs. Since e−kM ≤ Vs ≤ ekM , this
gives
                     LVs ≤ div Fs + ν,     |Fs | ≤ C,     ν = C( dx + µ) ≥ 0.              (6.32)
Thus no small-ball estimate for |DZ|2 is needed.
   On a ball BR in the smaller patch solve the zero-Dirichlet problem Lws = div Fs + ν. For the
bounded vector source, the scalar bounded-solution estimate with any fixed p > n gives

                    0 ≤ GBR (x, y) ≤ GB2R (x, y) ≤ C|x − y|2−n ,         x, y ∈ BR .

    This potential also gives an energy solution. Mollify the restriction of ν to BR and restrict the

                                 ∥ws ∥∞ ≤ εR ,           εR = C(R + Rβ ).                      (6.34)

  All extrema in the rest of this proof are essential extrema. Put MR = ess supBR Z and
mR = ess inf BR Z. The functions

               H+ = ekMR − ekZ + w+ + εR ,                  H− = e−kmR − e−kZ + w− + εR        (6.35)

are nonnegative almost everywhere and satisfy LH± ≥ 0. At least half of BR/2 satisfies either
Z ≤ (MR + mR )/2 or Z ≥ (MR + mR )/2. On the first set the positive exponential deficit is
at least c(MR − mR ); on the second set the negative exponential deficit has that lower bound.
Their derivatives are bounded above and away from zero on [−M, M ]. Weak Harnack for the
corresponding H± therefore either lowers the essential supremum or raises the essential infimum
on BR/4 by at least c′ (MR − mR ) − CεR . Hence

                            ess osc Z ≤ (1 − c′ ) ess osc Z + C(R + Rβ ).                      (6.36)
                             BR/4                       BR


Choose a positive exponent smaller than min{1, β} and than − log(1 − c′ )/ log 4. Iteration gives
an essential oscillation bound Crγ at every center in a smaller patch. The averages of Z on balls
centered at x are consequently Cauchy as their radii decrease to zero. Their limit defines Z ∗ (x)
at every center; comparison on overlapping balls gives |Z ∗ (x) − Z ∗ (y)| ≤ C|x − y|γ . Lebesgue
differentiation identifies Z ∗ = Z almost everywhere. This proves the representative assertion. For
continuous Z the representatives agree everywhere. No conclusion about arbitrary values of the
initially chosen Borel representative on a null set is required.
    For the boundary application, flatten a face and set S = diag(1, . . . , 1, −1). An even extension
uses a− = Sa+ S and b− = Sb+ ; its plane source is twice the prescribed total normal flux. An
odd extension after subtracting a constant Dirichlet value uses a− = Sa+ S, b− = −Sb+ and
the sign-reflected bulk source; its normal flux is continuous. The coordinate volume density is
included in these coefficients. For the smooth reflected solutions used here the product rule
holds, and a bounded plane density has ball mass O(rn−1 ), giving β = 1 for that part of the
measure. This verifies the boundary clause whenever the remaining reflected measure has the
stated growth.

6.3   Boundary regularity for the coupled equations
We return to the deformation. Its regularity must be obtained in the order Df , then Z, then
D2 f , and finally DZ. In the last step, small oscillation of Z absorbs its quadratic gradient term.
This order avoids assuming the Hessian estimate that the second equation still needs.

                                                       44
Proposition 6.3 (Classical bounds for bounded-variable solutions). Fix the deformation pa-
rameters and assume the bounds in Proposition 5.6. Every smooth solution in the homotopy of
Proposition 4.4, with t ≥ −ϵ, has uniform local classical estimates of every finite order, up to the
inner and outer faces. At fixed N the constants are independent of the homotopy parameter and
of all sufficiently large outer radii R. The local patches can be chosen with a uniform positive
radius throughout the end.

Proof. First, control Df and the Hessian measure. Proposition 5.6 bounds f, Z, t and σ = |∇f |.
At fixed parameters this bounds u, l, D√above and away from zero and gives χ ≥ χ0 > 0. After
dividing the scalar trace equation by l/ D, its right side is
                                         √
                                           D
                                    Gf =       Fs − trAχ K .                            (6.37)
                                                            
                                          l
It is bounded throughout the homotopy, including its added collar terms. Lemma 6.1 therefore
gives a uniform C 1,α estimate for f and
                                      Z

                                       Br

on the uniform patches, including boundary patches where f is constant.
   Second, control the oscillation of Z. After multiplication by the√Riemannian volume density,
the second equation has coordinate principal coefficients aij = 4 det g uAijχ , a bounded flux
vector b, and a source bounded in absolute value by

                                     C 1 + |DZ|2 + |D2 f |2 .                                 (6.39)
                                                              


Indeed t is a smooth function of x, Z, Df on the bounded variable range. Thus Dt is a bounded
linear combination of DZ, D2 f and fixed smooth terms; the expression T is quadratic in these
quantities. All other source terms have lower order. The same volume density is included in b, e;
it is smooth and bounded above and away from zero on the chosen patches, so it preserves these
bounds and uniform ellipticity. The prescribed conormal flux at the inner boundary is bounded.
At the outer face Z = 0. Apply Lemma 6.2, with µ = C|D2 f |2 dx and the bounded reflected
plane density. Equation (6.38) supplies its measure growth hypothesis. We obtain a uniform
positive Hölder exponent for Z.
     Third, gain two derivatives of f . The coefficients and source of the scalar equation are smooth
functions of x, Z, Df on the bounded range. They are therefore Hölder now that Z and Df
are Hölder. Interior and constant-Dirichlet boundary Schauder estimates give f ∈ C 2,θ for a
uniform θ > 0. Expand the Z-equation in nondivergence form. Its principal coefficients are
uniformly elliptic and C 0,θ , while its right side is bounded by C(1 + |DZ|2 ). On an inner face
Df is normal. Therefore Aχ has zero normal–tangential components there and normal eigenvalue
χ. Its boundary equation solves for

                                      ∂ν Z = Hs (x, Z, f, Df ),                               (6.40)

where Hs is smooth on a fixed bounded variable range. Its derivatives with respect to x and
its displayed scalar arguments are bounded at fixed parameters. The outer condition remains
constant Dirichlet. These are different, disjoint smooth boundary components; there is no change
of boundary condition along an edge or a corner.


                                                  45
   Fourth, absorb the quadratic growth and control DZ. Continuity of Z supplies the small factor
needed for this step. Fix p > 3. The linear local W 2,p estimate for the leading nondivergence
operator with Equation (6.40) or outer Dirichlet data has the form, on fixed nested patches
Ux ⋐ Vx ,                                                                  
                      ∥Z∥W 2,p (Ux ) ≤ C 1 + ∥DZ∥2L2p (Vx ) + ∥Z∥W 1,p (Vx ) .            (6.41)
Here the boundary norm is controlled by
                      ∥Hs (x, Z, f, Df )∥W 1−1/p,p (∂Vx ) ≤ C 1 + ∥Z∥W 1,p (Vx+ ) ,            (6.42)
                                                                                 

using trace and composition after extending its smooth expression through the collar. The
derivatives of f, Df entering this extension are already bounded. The leading matrix has a fixed
Hölder modulus, so its small oscillation on sufficiently small patches permits the usual freezing
and absorption in the linear estimate. The normal boundary operator is uniformly oblique and
satisfies the complementing condition. The general boundary theory is developed in the two
papers of Agmon, Douglis and Nirenberg [1, 2]. The scalar estimate used here is [1, Theorem 15.2,
estimate (15.5), p. 704].
    The outer patches Ux , Vx , Vx+ and the constant in (6.41) are fixed before the next radius h
is chosen; only the interpolation is localized further. Let [Z]C 0,θ ≤ H0 . On balls or half-balls of
radius h, the scaled Gagliardo–Nirenberg inequality, applied after subtracting a constant, gives

                                               + Ch3/p−2 (oscBh Z)2 .
One can use a bounded extension operator on a half-ball; its constants are uniform in the
boundary charts. Here the interpolation exponents are spatial dimension 3, derivative orders 2
and 1, top-derivative exponent p > 3, height exponent ∞, and interpolation parameter 1/2; the
resulting gradient exponent is 2p. This is [33, Lecture II, Equation (2.2), p. 125, and Remark 5,
p. 126], including the lower-order term on a bounded patch. Subtracting a constant gives the
oscillation, rather than the absolute height, as the coefficient of ∥D2 Z∥p . Cover Vx by radius-h
patches of bounded overlap and take the ℓp sum of Equation (6.43). It follows that
                        ∥DZ∥2L2p (Vx ) ≤ CH0 hθ ∥D2 Z∥Lp (Vx+ ) + CH02 h2θ−2 .                 (6.44)
Also, for any η > 0, ∥Z∥W 1,p (Vx ) ≤ η∥D2 Z∥Lp (Vx+ ) + Cη . Each enlarged patch Vx+ is covered by a
bounded number of the patches Uy , independently of R. Set S = supy ∥Z∥W 2,p (Uy ) , finite for each
smooth finite-domain solution. Equations (6.41) and (6.44) give
                                   S ≤ C(H0 hθ + η)S + C(h, η).                                (6.45)
Choose h and then η so the first coefficient is less than 1/2. We obtain a uniform W 2,p bound
and hence a C 1,1−3/p bound for Z.
    Finally, obtain all remaining derivatives. The bounds f ∈ C 2,θ and Z ∈ C 1,1−3/p make the
                                                                               ′
expanded Z source Hölder and its normal datum Hs (x, Z, f, Df ) of class C 1,θ , after reducing the
positive exponent. Its principal coefficients are Hölder as well, so both the interior and normal-
boundary Schauder estimates apply. First the nondivergence Z-equation has Hölder source, giving
          ′
Z ∈ C 2,θ ; the scalar f -equation then gains another derivative. Repetition gives every finite
classical derivative supported by the smooth data. The same procedure holds on the uniform unit
patches of the asymptotic end and on the large outer spheres, whose normal-coordinate constants
are uniform. This proves the asserted independence of R. The prepared background has end
symbol bounds of every order by Definition 2.2; no regularity constant here requires additional
derivatives of the original weak-decay data.

                                                   46
6.4   Solving the scalar equation for arbitrary inputs
The previous proposition estimates solutions of the coupled system. To construct such a solution
by degree, we first need an operator defined on all of a Banach space of trial functions Z. In
particular, t ≥ −ϵ cannot be assumed at this stage. The scalar equation remains solvable because
its loss of axial ellipticity at large slope is at most reciprocal-linear.
Lemma 6.4 (Scalar solve for unrestricted bounded inputs). Fix N ≥ 4, τ > 0, a finite smooth
truncation ΩR , and a homotopy parameter s ∈ [0, 4]. For every Z ∈ C 1,b (ΩR ), 0 < b < 1, the
scalar trace equation of Proposition 4.4, with its prescribed componentwise constant Dirichlet
values for f , has a unique solution. It lies in C 3,b and is smooth to the extent allowed by the
input. The solution operator
                                            f = Ss (Z)                                      (6.46)
is continuous in (s, Z), including across the concatenation points of the homotopy. On bounded
subsets of C 1,b its C 3,b norm is uniformly bounded at these fixed parameters. No lower bound for
t is required.
Proof. For the scalar continuation below, use the affine space of C 3,b functions with the assigned
Dirichlet values and the equation space C 1,b . Its individual solutions meet the C 3 hypothesis of
Lemma 6.1; no uniform third-derivative bound is assumed before that lemma is applied. The
monotonicity ∂Z/∂t = 1 + pv/4 > 0 defines t = t(x, Z, Df ) smoothly for every finite input,
including Df = 0. The principal matrix has no dependence on the value of f . The right side
is increasing in h = τ f , with derivative at least one. At a height extremum the collar terms
vanish and Df = 0, so the equation gives fixed constant upper and lower barriers. Including all
prescribed boundary heights, we obtain

                                              |h| ≤ C0 ,                                    (6.47)

where the constant can be chosen independently of Z and the homotopy parameter. We include
the fixed boundary values in its choice.
    Large slopes. Only |Z| ≤ M is assumed here. As σ → ∞, the implicit equation forces t → −∞
uniformly in Z. There p = N and l = eN t , so its exact form is

                                     e8Z = e8t + e(2N +8)t σ 2 .                            (6.48)

Consequently
                              log σ
                        t=−         + O(1),           l ≍ σ −N/(N +4) ,
                              N +4
                                                                      √                     (6.49)
                              −8/(N +4)             −8/(N +4)         D
                        d≍σ               ,   χ≍σ               ,       ≍ σ.
                                                                      l
The constants are uniform for |Z| ≤ M at fixed N . Since N ≥ 4, the exponent 8/(N + 4) is at
most one. Combining the large-slope estimates with compactness of the remaining variable range
yields
                                      c
                               χ≥         ,     |Gf | ≤ C(1 + σ)                           (6.50)
                                    1+σ
whenever Equation (6.47) holds.
    Boundary slopes. The estimate χ ≥ c/(1 + σ) suggests a concave logarithmic barrier: its axial
second derivative can dominate terms of linear growth in its slope. Let δ be smaller than a fixed

                                                 47
collar radius, half the distance between distinct boundary components, and a normal injectivity
radius of a smooth extension of the finite domain. On a boundary distance collar use

                           ψ(r) = k −1 log(1 + Br),      ψ ′′ = −k(ψ ′ )2 .                   (6.51)

The upper and lower barriers are the assigned face value plus or minus ψ. On these tests the axis
is normal to the distance leaves, so their principal trace is χψ ′′ + ψ ′ ∆r. At slopes at least one,
Equation (6.50) gives
                                      −kχ(ψ ′ )2 ≤ − 21 ckψ ′ .                                (6.52)
Choose k large enough to dominate all the bounded geometry terms and the linear growth in
Equation (6.50). Next shrink δ so that 1/(2kδ) exceeds any fixed large-slope threshold used here.
Finally choose B large enough that Bδ ≥ 1 and ψ(δ) > 4C0 /τ . The latter exceeds twice the
bound for osc f from (6.47). Then ψ ′ ≥ 1/(2kδ) on this collar and the two barriers compare by
the height monotonicity. This gives a uniform boundary gradient bound.
   Interior slopes. Use the same modulus for differences at two points. After increasing B if
needed, consider
                      f (x) − f (y) − ψ(dist(x, y)),   0 < dist(x, y) < δ.                 (6.53)
The distance is taken in the fixed smooth extension. The boundary barriers exclude a positive
maximum with one endpoint on a face: the other point lies in that face’s collar, its distance to
the face is at most the distance between the pair, and ψ is increasing. The separation choice
prevents endpoints on distinct faces. The inequality ψ(δ) > 2 osc f excludes the other boundary
of the two-point domain. At a positive interior maximum let r = dist(x, y) and q = ψ ′ (r). The
two gradients have length q and are parallel transports along the connecting geodesic. Vary both
endpoints simultaneously in parallel transverse directions. The second variation formula for the
short geodesic yields
                             tre⊥
                                x
                                  Hess f (x) − tre⊥
                                                  y
                                                    Hess f (y) ≤ Cqr.                      (6.54)
Varying each endpoint separately along that geodesic gives f;ex ex (x) ≤ ψ ′′ (r) and f;ey ey (y) ≥
−ψ ′′ (r). Subtracting the two scalar equations therefore gives

                     −2C(1 + q) ≤ Gf (x) − Gf (y) ≤ Cqr + (χx + χy )ψ ′′ (r).                 (6.55)

By Equations (6.50) and (6.51), the last term is at most −ckq at the large comparison slopes.
Our choice of k gives a contradiction. Thus Equation (6.53) is nonpositive. Reversing x, y proves
the same bound for the absolute difference and, on letting y → x, a global Lipschitz bound for
f . The endpoint variations are interior variations in the smooth extension; the connecting short
geodesic need not stay inside ΩR . This argument used no continuity modulus for χx − χy ; only
the common transverse eigenvalues and the radial lower bound were needed.
    Continuation and uniqueness. The height and gradient estimates supply compactness for a
scalar continuation. Interpolate the normalized equation with ∆f = τ f , retaining its prescribed
boundary values. Explicitly, for a ∈ [0, 1] take principal matrix (1 − a)I + aAχ and right side
(1−a)τ f +aGf . The height sign, the linear gradient growth, and Equation (6.50) persist uniformly
in a. The height and logarithmic gradient bounds just proved are therefore uniform. After these
bounds the interpolating equations are uniformly elliptic. Their matrices still have the axial form,
with axial eigenvalue (1 − a) + aχ, so Lemma 6.1 applies. Schauder estimates then give a uniform
C 2,θ bound at fixed input Z. The coefficients and right side are now Lipschitz in position on
bounded input sets, which permits their C 0,b estimates and an upgrade to C 2,b . Differentiating
once, or using the next Schauder estimate, yields f ∈ C 3,b because Z ∈ C 1,b .

                                                 48
    The scalar linearization has the form aij Dij φ + bi Di φ − cφ with c ≥ c1 τ > 0 on the√bounded
variable range. Indeed the principal matrix is independent of the height, while ∂f Gf ≥ τ D/l > 0.
The Dirichlet maximum principle gives uniqueness and a zero kernel; the linear elliptic Dirichlet
theory gives an inverse from C 1,b to C03,b , where the subscript denotes zero data on the entire
boundary. The implicit-function theorem gives openness of the interpolation parameter set. The
uniform classical bounds and compactness give closedness, starting from the invertible Laplace
endpoint. This proves existence. The same maximum principle applied to the difference of
two solutions proves uniqueness of the nonlinear solution. The implicit-function theorem, or
compactness and uniqueness, gives continuous dependence on Z and on the homotopy parameter.
At the finitely many concatenation points the scalar equations agree, so the same continuity holds
there.

6.5   Degree on the admissible set and exhaustion
We can now construct the compact map. The homotopy parameter s ∈ [0, 4] has a separate
role from the deformation integer N : in Proposition 4.4, s = 0 is the original system, s = 1
removes the drift, s = 2 completes the collar modification, and s = 3 makes the scalar solution
f identically zero. On [3, 4] the divergence source and the inner flux are multiplied together by
4 − s. The final compact map is consequently the zero map. The remaining task is to show that
no fixed point can cross the boundary of the admissible set along this path.

Lemma 6.5 (Degree on varying open sets). Let I = [a, b], let X be a real Banach space, and let
T : I × X → X be continuous and map bounded sets to relatively compact sets. Let U ⊂ I × X be
bounded and relatively open. Suppose no fixed point Z = T (s, Z) belongs to the relative boundary
of U. Then, for Us = {Z : (s, Z) ∈ U}, the Leray–Schauder degree

                                        deg(IX − Ts , Us , 0)

is well defined and independent of s. Empty sections have degree zero. If Tb is the zero map and
0 ∈ Ub , the common degree is one, and every section contains a fixed point.

Proof. The fixed points in U form a compact set F. Indeed, boundedness of U, compactness of
I and of the images of T give a convergent subsequence of any sequence of such fixed points;
continuity preserves the fixed-point equation. The hypothesis places F inside U. Also {s} × ∂Us
lies in the relative boundary of U, so each section degree is defined.
    Fix s0 ∈ I. Cover its compact fixed-point slice by finitely many open balls in X whose closed
balls remain in Us for all s sufficiently close to s0 . Relative openness and the finite cover permit
this common parameter interval. Let U be their union. All fixed points in the closed sections
for these nearby parameters lie in U after shortening the interval: otherwise compactness of F
would give an omitted fixed point in the slice at s0 . Excision identifies the degree on each Us
with that on U , and fixed-domain homotopy invariance makes the latter constant. If the slice is
empty, compactness instead gives no nearby fixed points in the closed sections, so those degrees
are all zero. Thus the section degree is locally constant, and connectedness of I makes it constant.
These are the excision and homotopy properties of Leray–Schauder degree [27, Sections 12–14,
pp. 59–61]; see also [13]. At the stated terminal map, normalization gives degree one; the existence
property of nonzero degree proves the last assertion.

Theorem 6.6 (Existence on finite truncations). For the prepared domain of Proposition 3.7 and
every sufficiently small fixed ϵ > 0, there is an integer N0 ≥ 4 and, for each N ≥ N0 , a radius

                                                 49
Rmin (N ), with Rmin (N ) → ∞, such that the original deformation system has a smooth solution on
every ΩR with R ≥ Rmin (N ). It satisfies t > −ϵ on the closure. At fixed N , these solutions have
the local bounds of Proposition 6.3, uniformly as R → ∞. In particular, any sequence Rj → ∞ of
such radii has a subsequence converging smoothly on compact subsets of Ω to a solution of the
original equations with t ≥ −ϵ.
Proof. Choose the parameters before defining degree. Fix the prepared data, thresholds, collars
and ϵ. Choose an integer N0 so that
                                      N0 ≥ max{4, ⌊2/ϵ⌋ + 1}                                    (6.56)
and so that the floor test and all estimates of Proposition 5.6 apply for N ≥ N0 . Let Rap (N )
dominate every lower-radius requirement in the a priori estimates and in the floor exclusion; let
Rgeo contain all compact boundary faces, collars and supports of the construction. The end barrier
of that proposition gives a constant Cout , independent of N, R, s, such that every admissible or
floor-contact homotopy solution has
                           |∇f | ≤ Cout τ −1 R−1−γ      on the outer sphere.                    (6.57)
The scalar last stage has f = 0 and satisfies the same bound. Choose, enlarging to a radius of a
smooth truncation if necessary,
                                              
                             Rmin (N ) = max N, Rgeo , Rap (N ),
                                                                                                (6.58)
                                                       2C ℓ
                                                                 1/(1+γ) 
                                                       √ out                   .
                                                      τ e8ϵ − 1
At Z = 0, the value of its implicit expression at t = −ϵ is −ϵ + 18 log(1 + ℓ2 σ 2 ). Equations (6.57)
and (6.58) make this strictly negative. Strict monotonicity of that expression in t therefore
excludes an outer floor contact. The term N in Equation (6.58) ensures Rmin (N ) → ∞.
   The sublevel and collar estimates apply to arbitrary smooth admissible or floor-contact solution
sequences with N → ∞ and expanding outer cutoffs, uniformly over the compact ranges of the
homotopy parameters. If the needed conclusion failed for arbitrarily large N with R ≥ Rmin (N ),
one could select such a violating sequence. The chosen radius convention would make it precisely
an expanding sequence excluded by those proofs. Thus a single N0 , followed by the radius choices
above, suffices for every homotopy parameter. No upper bound for Z is inserted in the earlier
contact estimates: there σ ≍ τ −1 and l ≥ ℓ alone give d ≲ (τ /ℓ)2 .
   Define the compact map on all bounded input sets. For the rest of the degree argument fix
N ≥ N0 and R ≥ Rmin (N ). Work in the Banach space
                 Xb = {Z ∈ C 1,b (ΩR ) : Z = 0 on the outer face},                 0 < b < 1.   (6.59)
For an input Z ∈ Xb , first solve f = Ss (Z) by Lemma 6.4. Compute t, u, Aχ , T and the full

The sign of the boundary term comes from using −ν as the integration normal. The scalar

The nonempty outer Dirichlet face supplies Poincaré’s inequality, so the bilinear form is coercive
and has a unique solution.
    On bounded input sets in C 1,b , Lemma 6.4 bounds f in C 3,b . Hence the leading coefficient
and drift in Equation (6.60) are C 1,b , the bulk source is C 0,b , and the prescribed boundary data
are C 1,b . The derivatives of t(x, Z, Df ) involve only DZ and D2 f , so this assertion includes the
quadratic expression T . Linear regularity gives a bounded output set in C 2,b . The inclusion
C 2,b ,→ C 1,b is compact on the finite domain. Continuous dependence of the scalar solve and the
linear problem makes (s, Z) 7→ Ks (Z) a continuous compact homotopy on bounded sets. These
assertions hold also at the concatenation parameters because the defining equations coincide
there. A fixed point solves the coupled system; repeated Schauder estimates make it smooth.
    Exclude fixed points from the boundary of admissibility. Write ts [Z] = t(x, Z, DSs (Z)). The
scalar solution must be computed before this admissibility test. Define

                    O = {(s, Z) ∈ [0, 4] × Xb : min ts [Z] > −ϵ, ∥Z∥C 1,b < M1 }.                (6.61)
                                                   ΩR

It is relatively open because the scalar solve and the implicit function defining t depend continuously
on (s, Z). Propositions 5.6 and 6.3 give a uniform bound in C 1,b for all fixed points satisfying
t ≥ −ϵ. If initially needed, use their higher classical bounds to reach the chosen exponent b.
Choose M1 larger than this bound. No fixed point lies on the norm boundary of O. No fixed
point lies on its floor boundary either: outer contacts were excluded above and all remaining
contacts are excluded by Proposition 4.9.
     Apply degree on the varying admissible sections. The sections Os need not be constant in s.
Lemma 6.5 applies with I = [0, 4], X = Xb , Ts = Ks and U = O. We have proved continuity and
compactness on bounded input sets; the norm condition makes O bounded and the scalar solve
makes it relatively open. Its relative boundary can contain only norm contact or floor contact,
and neither can be a fixed point by the preceding estimates. Thus its section degree is constant.
     At s = 4, the scalar equation has the unique solution f = 0. The drift vanishes, and both
E4 and q4 vanish for every input. Equation (6.60) then has output identically zero. Moreover,
t4 [0] = 0 > −ϵ, so 0 ∈ O4 and
                                        deg(I − K4 , O4 , 0) = 1.                                 (6.62)
Homotopy invariance gives the same degree at s = 0. There is therefore a fixed point in O0 ,
which is the desired smooth solution with t > −ϵ.
    Remove only the outer truncation. Keep N and all other deformation parameters fixed.
Proposition 6.3 gives estimates independent of R on an exhaustion by compact sets, using
boundary charts at the fixed inner faces. A diagonal subsequence converges smoothly on compact
subsets of Ω. Both equations and their inner boundary conditions pass to the limit. The strict
finite-domain inequality gives the non-strict limit inequality t ≥ −ϵ. The following section
establishes end normalization, decay and mass flux; those conclusions do not follow from compact
exhaustion alone.

                                                    51
7     The complete exterior and its energy
We now obtain the three geometric outputs stated at the beginning: nonnegative scalar curvature,
control of every cut, and an arbitrarily small upper energy error. Their combination will also
yield the numerical comparison. Fix the prepared initial data of Definition 2.2, and the prepared
exterior Ω of Proposition 3.7. Its boundary B separates the original inner obstacle from the end.
Let A∗ be the enclosing-area infimum of that original obstacle, measured in the reduced metric g.
In particular every cut enclosing B has g-area at least A∗ .
    We use the notation of Section 4 and take the physical endpoint of the homotopy:

          F = h + C,       h = τ f,      div V = uΞ,        Ξ = δ0 T + ρ + δ0 τ aσ − m0 (t)P.

Here 0 < δ0 < 1, ℓ = e−ϵN , τ = ℓ3/2 , and all constants in the prepared data are fixed before
N is taken large. Constants marked CN in this section are bounded by C(1 + N )C , with fixed
exponents. Other constants explicitly allowed to depend on fixed N need not have such a bound.

7.1    The limiting solution and its end
Lemma 7.1 (End control). Fix ϵ > 0 and a sufficiently large N ≥ max{4, ⌊2/ϵ⌋ + 1}. The
truncation solutions of Theorem 6.6 have a subsequence converging smoothly on compact subsets
of Ω to a solution of the physical system with t ≥ −ϵ. On the asymptotically flat end, for some
cN > 0 and fixed-N constants Cj (N ),

                    |∇j f | ≤ Cj (N )e−cN r   for each fixed j,    Z, t = O2 (r−1 ).

The differences t − Z, ḡ − g, and their coordinate derivatives of any fixed order decay exponentially.
The vector field V satisfies
                                          V = 4∇g t + O(r−3 )                                    (7.1)
and has a finite outward flux limit.
Proof. The compact convergence, including at the fixed inner boundary, follows from Theorem 6.6
and its uniform fixed-N classical estimates. All equations and inner boundary conditions pass to
this limit. To preserve the end normalization, we prove the decay estimates on the truncations
before passing to infinity.
    Outside a fixed large sphere, K = C = 0, and the first equation is
                                                      √
                                                     τ D
                                      trAχ Hessg f =       f.
                                                       l
At fixed N the preceding estimates make the left side uniformly elliptic, and its positive height
coefficient is bounded below by a positive constant depending on N . It is bounded above as well.
For sufficiently small cN > 0, the functions exp[−cN (r − r0 )] are positive supersolutions of the
corresponding linear equation outside a sufficiently large r0 : their radial second derivative has size
c2N , while their tangential second derivatives have size cN /r, and the fixed positive height term
dominates both. Multiplying by a constant bounds the solution on Sr0 ; the zero outer Dirichlet
value is also bounded. The maximum principle applied to both signs of f gives the uniform
exponential estimate. The fixed-N unit-patch estimates, followed by the local homogeneous linear
estimates for this equation, give the same decay for derivatives. The corresponding boundary
estimates apply on the large outer spheres. Iterating the smooth equations gives each required
finite derivative order.

                                                   52
   The relation Z = t + 18 log(1 + l2 |∇f |2 ) now shows that t − Z is exponentially small, with
derivatives. The graph errors have the same property. On the end the second equation becomes
                ∆g Z + bN (Z)|dZ|2g = O(r−4 ),          bN (s) = pL (s) − δ0 pL (s) − 1 .     (7.2)

Indeed Aχ = I and u = L(Z) up to exponentially small errors, whereas T = 4(pL (Z) − 1)|dZ|2
up to such errors. The ρ0 penalty weight is O(r−4 ); the term with ρ1 is multiplied by the
exponentially small v. All coefficients in this statement are bounded on the fixed-N solution
range. Differentiably controlled exponential errors can absorb any fixed polynomial power of r.
   Define an increasing smooth function on that range by
                                                               Z s            
                          ΦN (0) = 0,       Φ′N (s) = exp              bN (z) dz .
                                                                   0
Its derivative is bounded above and below by positive fixed-N constants. The chain rule in (7.2)
gives
                                     ∆g ΦN (Z) = O(r−4 ).
The function is bounded and vanishes at the outer cutoff. To obtain a bound independent of that
cutoff, choose 0 < η < 1. For large r, a positive multiple of r−1 − r−1−η satisfies
                                   −∆g (r−1 − r−1−η ) ≥ cr−3−η .
Here the Euclidean leading term is η(1 + η)r−3−η , and the metric error is O(r−4 ). It dominates
the right side after increasing the multiple and the fixed inner radius. Comparison for both
signs yields |ΦN (Z)| ≤ C ′ (N )r−1 , where C ′ (N ) is a fixed-N constant with no polynomial claim.
Consequently Z = O(r−1 ).
    At sufficiently large radius this estimate and the exponential graph error give t > −ϵ + 1/N ;
hence the floor penalty vanishes there. Rescale each annulus of radii comparable to r to unit
size. The equation for ΦN (Z) has a bounded rescaled source and uniformly controlled metric
coefficients. Local W 2,p estimates with p > 3 first give the gradient decay. Its source is then a
smooth weight times a smooth function of the bounded variables, plus the already controlled
exponential errors. Schauder estimates, or a further Sobolev bootstrap followed by them, give
Z = O2 (r−1 ). Inverting ΦN preserves these bounds, and the same holds for t.
    Since L(0) = 1, we have u = 1 + O(r−1 ) and Aχ = I + O(e−cN r ). With K = 0 on the end, the
definition of V gives (7.1). Moreover div V = uΞ is integrable there: T = O(r−4 ), ρ = O(r−4 ),
the penalty has vanished, and τ aσ decays exponentially. The divergence theorem on annuli proves
the existence of the outward flux limit.

inner boundary term into the positive bulk source and then estimate the penalty near the floor.
Both steps use only polynomial constants.

before any Riemannian Penrose comparison.

7.3    The Riemannian horizon and the inequality
The deformed metric has nonnegative scalar curvature, strictly negative inner mean curvature
toward the end, and the required asymptotic decay. We replace its inner boundary by an outer

Theorem 7.4 (Reduced energy inequality). The reduced data satisfy

Proof. For each fixed sufficiently large N , minimize ĝ-area among filled cuts enclosing B, using a

A fixed enclosing competitor bounds the area, and local perimeter density estimates prevent
minimizers from escaping to infinity. The positive mean curvature of sufficiently large outer
spheres excludes contact there, as in that enclosure theorem. For the inner barrier, let Us
be a short outward normal collar of the filled obstacle and Y its outward unit foliation field.
Strict negativity gives divĝ Y < 0 throughout the collar. If a perimeter minimizer E omitted a

its minimizing property. Equivalently one may first use the ordinary trapped-region theorem for
(ĝ, 0) and then take the outer minimizing enclosure. The boundary version of the Riemannian

     Finally apply the weak three-dimensional rest-preparation theorem of [34, Proposition 7.13]
to an exterior satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem 2.4. Write (gj , Kj ) for the resulting data.
Their tensor fields have compact support, their momenta vanish, and their metrics have all-order
r−1 decay and the stipulated scalar falloff. Discard a finite initial segment so that Ej > 0.
Strict trapping and strict DEC hold on the compact core. The positive minimum there of
8π(µj −|Jj |gj )ϱ3+δ , together with the end margin, gives the global weighted bound after decreasing
cj . The full enclosing area is positive by the geometric enclosure lemma. Thus each fixed j
satisfies Definition 2.2; no constant uniform in j has been used in its deformation solve. The
reduced energies converge to its invariant ADM mass and the full enclosing infima converge to
the original enclosing infimum. Applying the reduced bound to each member of that sequence
and passing to the limit gives Equation (2.10).

                                                  56
8     A boundary deformation in four dimensions
The construction in this section keeps a genuine inner boundary throughout the graph deformation.
The boundary has two jobs: it must become strictly mean concave, so that a minimal enclosure
can detach from it, and its flux must be small enough to leave the energy at infinity almost
unchanged. These requirements explain the signed collars and the boundary condition in the
coupled equations. They also distinguish this construction from a solve on a manifold obtained
by filling the boundary.
    We first state the geometric output and deduce its numerical consequence. The rest of this
part constructs that output. The proof is independent of the numerical spacetime inequality: its
scalar identity and local analytic estimates apply before any Riemannian comparison is made.

8.1    Data, normalization and the full enclosing area
Here the spatial dimension is four. Write
                                                1
      ω3 = |S 3 | = 2π 2 ,   τ = trg K,      µ = (Rg + τ 2 − |K|2g ),         Ji = ∇j (Kij − τ gij ).
                                                2
Here τ denotes the trace of K; the small height regularizer called τ in the three-dimensional
construction is denoted by ηN in this part. The tensor K is symmetric, and ∇ is the Levi–Civita
connection of g. Our Laplacian is ∆g = divg gradg . A hypersurface with unit normal ν has mean
curvature H = divtan ν and future expansion H + trtan K. At the inner boundary the normal
points into the exterior. In a compact auxiliary region it points out of that region. Every change
of sign below specifies both the tensor and the orientation.
    The notation Oj (r−a ) includes coordinate derivatives of order k ≤ j, bounded by O(r−a−k ).
In the Euclidean end coordinates our energy and momentum conventions are
                                   1
                                             Z
                             Eg =      lim      (∂j gij − ∂i gjj )νδi dAδ ,                             (8.1)
                                  6ω3 R→∞ |x|=R

                                           Z

                                  3ω3 R→∞ |x|=R

The prepared tensor below has compact support, hence P = 0. No additional normalization of
mass accompanies these density conventions.

Definition 8.1 (Full cuts in a one-ended exterior). Let M be a smooth connected manifold with
nonempty compact boundary S and one Euclidean end with compact complement. A full cut
is the entire intrinsic boundary of a smooth connected codimension-zero outer domain D ⊂ M
that is closed in M , contains the entire distant end, has compact boundary, and has its manifold
interior in int M . Its boundary is embedded and two-sided; all components and every portion
coincident with S count. Set
                                         A∗ = inf |∂man D|g .
                                                 D

In particular D = M is permitted. The infimum need not be attained.

   These are full cuts rather than a selected connected component of a cut. Filling bounded
pockets on the obstacle side removes irrelevant internal frontiers, while leaving a connected
exterior containing the end. The metric comparison in the construction will apply on every
tangent three-plane, so it will control this entire class at once.

                                                     57
8.2   The geometric output and its immediate use
Theorem 8.2 (Four-dimensional boundary deformation). Let (M 4 , g, K) be smooth, connected
and orientable, complete with its nonempty compact smooth boundary S included, with exactly one
Euclidean coordinate end and compact complement. Suppose K is compactly supported. Let r be a
smooth positive extension of the coordinate radius. Assume that, for some c0 > 0 and 0 < δ1 < 1,

          µ − |J|g ≥ c0 r−4−δ1    on M,       H + trS K < 0        on every component of S,

with the normal into M . On the end assume

                  g − δ = Oj (r−2 )    for every fixed j ≥ 0,       Rg = O(r−4−δ1 ),

and finite ADM energy in Equation (8.1).
    There is a smooth closed connected one-ended exterior Ω ⊂ M with nonempty compact
boundary B ⊂ int M such that every full cut of B in Ω has g-area at least A∗ . For every 0 < ϵ < 1
and all sufficiently large integers N there is a smooth metric gbϵ,N on Ω, complete with B included,
satisfying

                         Rgbϵ,N ≥ 0,      Hgbϵ,N (B) < 0,        gbϵ,N ≥ e−2ϵ g,               (8.3)

The boundary mean curvature uses the normal into Ω. The constant ΠN = C(1 + N )apol is
polynomial in N , with C and apol depending only on the fixed data, ϵ and the fixed geometric
choices. The constants in Equation (8.4) may depend arbitrarily on N .

   The metric has the form e2t (g + l(t)2 df ⊗ df ): the graph term cannot shorten a tangent
vector, and the floor t ≥ −ϵ protects all enclosing areas. Its inner boundary is not itself used in
the Riemannian inequality. Strictly negative mean curvature supplies an outward barrier for a
new minimal enclosure.
   We use the following precise form of the independent smooth enclosure theorem. It concerns
perimeter alone and uses no stationary-field, variation or equality conclusion.

Proposition 8.3 (Smooth full-perimeter enclosure input). Let (X 4 , h) be a smooth connected
orientable one-ended exterior, complete with nonempty compact smooth boundary B included.
Suppose the end has compact complement and h − δ = O2 (r−q ) for some q > 1. Minimize
the full perimeter of the filled obstacle among enclosing sets; contact with B and all frontier
components count. The minimizing enclosure can be chosen with connected complete exterior, and
its full perimeter equals the infimum over the smooth full cuts of B. Its exterior frontier is outer
area-minimizing against all enclosing competitors, including disconnected ones. If Hh (B) < 0 for
the normal into X, that frontier is a nonempty smooth compact embedded minimal hypersurface
contained in int X.

     This is the smooth enclosure theorem from the independent Riemannian geometric preliminar-
ies [35, Lemmas 2.1–2.3]. Its proof uses confinement, BV compactness, smooth-cut approximation
and obstacle regularity. Only its strict-boundary case is used here.
