8.3   The construction and its scales
There are four stages in the proof of the deformation theorem. Section 9 chooses two trapped-
region frontiers and their signed collars. The frontiers allow a small interval of prescribed graph
trace while the original strict dominant-energy margin pays for the unrestricted τ . Section 10 fixes
the coupled equations and excludes first contact with the conformal floor. In Section 11, geometric
support comparisons first confine the scaled height to the correct interval; signed boundary slopes
then make the inner flux small, before any uniform ellipticity is known. Sections 12 and 13 obtain
boundary regularity, construct the compact return map and complete exhaustion and energy
control.
    The local scalar identities are given with their proofs and the exact four-dimensional constants.
Their common content is reusable in other graph constructions. The degree argument, signed
boundary estimates, reflected measure estimates and four-dimensional interpolation remain specific
verifications for the boundary problem studied here.
Remark 8.5 (Order of constants and limits). Fix the prepared data, the thresholds and collars,
and ϵ > 0 first. The deformation integer N is then sufficiently large. A bound denoted ΠN is
polynomial in N and uniform in the outer truncation radius, homotopy parameter and solution.
Constants denoted C(N ) in elliptic estimates may depend arbitrarily on N . Exhaust the outer
radius at fixed N , then pass N → ∞ using only polynomial estimates, and finally ϵ ↓ 0. In an
application to weakly decaying original data, strictification is removed at each fixed repaired end
only after this prepared construction is finished; the end-replacement radius tends to infinity last.

                                                   59
9     Threshold exteriors and geometric barriers
Our input is the prepared exterior in Theorem 8.2. Thus the original boundary has strictly
negative future expansion, K has compact support, and, for a smooth positive extension r of the
end radius,
                        µ − |J|g ≥ c0 r−4−δ1 , c0 > 0,      0 < δ1 < 1.                   (9.1)
The enclosing infimum for this prepared metric is denoted by A∗ , with the intrinsic-boundary
convention of Definition 8.1. This section constructs an exterior on which both signs of a
small prescribed trace can be controlled. The original trapping convention is always the future
convention H + trS K < 0. The tensor −K below is used only to construct additional interior
barriers.

9.1   Total regions inside a compact barrier
For a smooth symmetric tensor Q and an oriented hypersurface Σ, write

                                       ΘQ (Σ) = HΣ + trΣ Q.

When Σ bounds a compact region, its normal points out of that region. A smooth compact region
may have several components. It includes its entire manifold boundary and is the closure of its
interior. For a compact manifold X with boundary and a number c, define

          Tc (X, Q) =       {E : E ⋐ int X is a smooth compact region,    ΘQ (∂E) ≤ c}.        (9.2)
                        [


The empty union has empty closure. All closures in this definition are taken in X.

Lemma 9.1 (Compact total region). Let (X 4 , g) be smooth, compact, connected, and orientable,
with nonempty smooth boundary, and let Q be smooth. Suppose that ΘQ (∂X) > c, with the normal
pointing out of X on every boundary component. Then Tc (X, Q) is either empty or a smooth
compact region contained in int X. In the latter case its frontier has expansion c and is stable
for outward variations. It contains every region in (9.2) and is itself one of those regions. In
particular, no component of its complement whose closure is contained in int X can be a bounded
hole.

Proof. Replace Q by Qc = Q − (c/3)g. Since the hypersurfaces have dimension three,

                                       ΘQc (Σ) = ΘQ (Σ) − c                                    (9.3)

for every oriented hypersurface. It suffices to prove the result for c = 0, and we use Qc throughout
this proof.
    The global input is the smooth-frontier part of [6, Theorem 4.6]: for smooth complete
asymptotically flat data in spatial dimensions 2 ≤ n ≤ 7, a nonempty total trapped region has
smooth embedded outermost stable MOTS frontier. No dominant energy condition is required
for this conclusion. The higher-dimensional construction uses the Perron and almost-minimizing
method developed by Eichmair [14], who also explains its adaptation to MOTSs. We now reduce
the compact problem to the boundaryless asymptotically flat setting of the cited total-region
theorem, preserving exactly its trapped competitors.
    First attach an outward cobordism from ∂X to one three-sphere. Each component of ∂X is a
closed orientable three-manifold. Relative one-handles join these components, and the smooth

                                                60
surgery theorem for orientable three-manifolds gives a framed link surgery from the resulting
connected three-manifold to S 3 [42, Theorem 6 and its proof, pp. 521–522]. Its trace consists
of relative two-handles: reversing a four-dimensional two-handle trace again uses two-handles.
Thus the cobordism has a handle decomposition relative to ∂X with indices at most two. Attach
S 3 × [0, ∞) at its other end, obtaining a boundaryless manifold X. e The handle Morse function
provides a proper smooth height q on the extension, with q = 0 on ∂X, increasing in an outward
collar, with only critical points of index at most two, and equal to a radial coordinate sufficiently
far out. Extend q a short distance into X as a collar coordinate with negative values.
    Extend g smoothly across ∂X. We may choose its values near every critical point of q so that
                 trV Hessg q > 0    for every three-dimensional subspace V ⊂ Tx X
                                                                                e                 (9.4)
at that critical point, and hence throughout a smaller neighborhood. Indeed, at an index-
two point the eigenvalues of the Hessian can be arranged as −a, −b, A, B, with a, b > 0 and
min(A, B) > a + b; the sum of the three smallest is positive. At indices zero or one the same
choice is easier. This is achieved by rescaling the positive and negative coordinate directions
in the metric in a Morse chart. The neighborhoods are disjoint and away from the prescribed
original data, so these local metrics extend to a smooth positive metric on the whole extension.
Choose that metric Euclidean on the remote end.
    At a regular point of a level of q, its mean curvature toward increasing q is
                                                     tr(grad q)⊥ Hess q
                                   H{q=constant} =                      .
                                                            | dq|
It is positive near the critical points by (9.4). Choose a smooth preliminary tensor extension
agreeing with Qc in the prescribed boundary collar. Near each critical point there is a > 0 such
that trV Hess q ≥ a on every unit three-plane. After shrinking its neighborhood, H ≥ a/| dq|
dominates the bounded tangential trace of the preliminary tensor. On the remaining compact
regular part the level mean curvature is bounded below. Retain the tensor near the original
boundary, and add a sufficiently large nonnegative multiple of the metric on the remaining
compact part of the extension. The tangential trace of this addition is three times its coefficient.
We can therefore ensure strictly positive expansion on every regular level of q. The given strict
boundary inequality permits the matching in a collar of ∂X, including a collar on the original
side. On the Euclidean remote end take the tensor to be zero; its round levels already have
positive mean curvature. The transition to zero can be made inside that Euclidean region with
a nonnegative metric multiple. We have obtained smooth complete one-ended data without
boundary, exactly Euclidean with zero tensor sufficiently far out.
    Every compact smooth weakly trapped region in this extension stays a positive distance
inside X. To see this, extend q to a smooth function on the interior core with values below
those of a fixed smaller collar. If a region enters that collar or the attached extension, the
maximum of q on it occurs at a point in that part. A regular maximum cannot lie in the interior
of the region. At a regular boundary maximum the region is on the inner side of a positively
expanding level, with the same outward normal at contact. Tangential Hessian comparison gives
ΘQc (∂E) ≥ ΘQc ({q = qmax }) > 0, a contradiction. At a critical maximum in the interior, a
direction of positive Hessian contradicts maximality. At a critical boundary maximum, the inward
open half-space of a smooth domain contains a vector v with Hess q(v, v) > 0: choose the sign
of a positive Hessian direction to point inward, and perturb it inward if it is tangent. A curve
entering the region with this initial velocity has q(γ(t)) = q(γ(0)) + t2 Hess q(v, v)/2 + o(t2 ), again
a contradiction. Thus one fixed original-side collar is avoided by every such region.

                                                     61
    The global total region consequently coincides with (9.2); all its candidate regions lie in the
same compact subset of int X. The cited theorem gives its smooth stable frontier. With the
closed representative used here it is a smooth compact region, its frontier has ΘQc = 0, and it
contains every candidate. This proves the assertion at threshold c by (9.3). If a complementary
component has closure in int X, filling it deletes whole smooth boundary components and changes
no remaining outward expansion. The resulting larger region would still be a candidate in (9.2),
contradicting maximality.

    The extension in this proof is auxiliary. Neither a constraint inequality nor a topological
restriction is imposed on it. We will also apply the lemma after compactly supported changes of
Q that leave the strict outer barriers unchanged.

9.2   Threshold continuity and collars
Lemma 9.2 (Continuity thresholds). Suppose that the strict barrier condition in Lemma 9.1
holds for every c in an open interval I. The closed regions Tc (X, Q) increase with c. Except
at a countable set of thresholds, they are right-continuous in Hausdorff distance. At an empty
right-continuity threshold the regions are empty for all sufficiently close larger thresholds.

Proof. Inclusion of the defining classes in (9.2) proves that Tc (X, Q) increases with c. These are
closed subsets of one fixed compact metric space (X, distg ). Apply Lemma 3.2 to this family. Its
empty-set convention gives precisely the asserted eventual emptiness. Since the exceptional set is
countable, every nonempty open subinterval of I contains a right-continuity threshold.

Lemma 9.3 (Outward leaf collars). Let Tc (X, Q) be nonempty, and let Σ be one connected
component of its frontier. On the side exterior to this region, Σ has a smooth foliated collar,
parametrized by s ∈ [0, s0 ], with | ds| > 0. With normal νs = grad s/| ds|, every leaf satisfies

                                            ΘQ (Σs ) ≥ c.                                     (9.5)

The collars of distinct components can be chosen disjoint.

Proof. Lemma 9.1 supplies a compact smooth frontier with ΘQ = c, stable for the outward
normal. Its component Σ is closed, connected, and two-sided. Replacing only Σ by a sufficiently
small positive outward graph with expansion at most c would produce a larger admissible region
in (9.2), contradicting that lemma’s maximality. The graph collar can be chosen inside int X and
disjoint from the other frontier components.
    Apply Lemma 3.3 with ambient dimension d = 4 and tensor Q. Here the shifted tensor is
Q − (c/3)g; (9.3) holds for every nearby graph, so its linearization is the same operator L used in
that lemma. In particular, when the principal eigenvalue is zero, the normalized linear map is
                                                                            Z          
                 C   2,α
                           (Σ) × R −→ C (Σ) × R,
                                       α
                                                        (v, a) 7−→ Lv − a,       v dA         (9.6)
                                                                             Σ

for 0 < α < 1. This is the common zero-mode isomorphism (3.11), whose inverse and resulting
foliation are proved there. The lemma gives ΘQ = c at s = 0 and ΘQ > c for s > 0, hence
(9.5), with a smooth leaf coordinate and positive normal speed. Apply it to the finitely many
components and shorten the collars to be disjoint.




                                                   62
9.3   Exterior supports and smooth enclosing regions
A smooth exterior support to a closed set D at x ∈ ∂D is a smooth regular level {ψ = 0} through
x, defined in a neighborhood of x, such that D is locally contained in {ψ ≤ 0}. Its outward
normal is grad ψ/| dψ|. Saying that D has expansion at most k in exterior supports means that
every such support has ΘQ ≤ k at contact. This definition makes no regularity assumption on D.
    The next argument is useful because the curvatures of these supports need not be bounded.
Ordinary approximate transport of tangent metrics would produce an uncontrolled error multi-
plying their second fundamental forms. We use a map whose differential is an exact isometry at
the contact point.

Lemma 9.4 (Exact support transport). Let g, Q be smooth on a neighborhood of a compact set,
and let D be a compact closed subset of its interior. Suppose that D has expansion at most k in
exterior supports. There are z0 > 0 and C < ∞ such that, for 0 < z < z0 , every exterior support
to
                                   Dz = {y : distg (y, D) ≤ z}
has expansion at most k + Cz. The constant C depends only on bounds for the smooth background
geometry and Q in the fixed compact neighborhood, and not on the support or its curvature. The
allowable z0 is also smaller than the distance required to keep these neighborhoods in the interior.

Proof. Apply Lemma 3.5 with ambient dimension d = 4, tensor Q, and the compact closed set D.
Choose its fixed smooth compact neighborhood inside the interior in the present hypotheses, and
shorten z0 to keep the distance neighborhoods there. That lemma includes the empty case and
gives exactly the required estimate, uniformly in the support curvature. We record its contact
identities in the present notation.
    Let a support {ψ = 0} touch Dz at x′ , choose a nearest point x ∈ ∂D, and let γ : [0, z] → X
be the unit-speed minimizing segment from x to x′ . Put u0 = γ̇(0) and let Pt be parallel transport
along γ. The support normal at x′ is Pz u0 . The common proof constructs, for each X ∈ Tx X,
the Jacobi field

                   Dt2 JX + R(JX , γ̇)γ̇ = 0,        JX (0) = X,           JX (z) = Pz X.      (9.7)

Writing JX (t) = Pt (X + WX (t)), its short-interval estimates give

                          sup |WX | ≤ Cz 2 |X|,           |Dt JX (0)| ≤ Cz|X|.                 (9.8)
                           [0,z]

For Az X = Dt JX (0) the first-jet compatibility is

                                   ⟨Az X, u0 ⟩ = 0,          |Az | ≤ Cz.

In normal coordinates y i at x, let Ev (y) denote radial parallel transport of v ∈ Tx X. The unit
field of the common construction is
                                                                               V (y)
                       V (y) = Eu0 (y) +        y i EAz ei (y),    U (y) =
                                           X
                                                                                      .
                                           i
                                                                              |V (y)|

Its prescribed first jet and uniform second-jet bounds are those established in that proof. The
endpoint map is
                                      Fz (y) = expy (zU (y)).

                                                     63
The exact endpoint identity and its covariant second-derivative bound specialize to

                       Fz (x) = x′ ,         dFz (x) = Pz ,       |∇ dFz (x)| ≤ Cz.              (9.9)

   The pulled-back level ψ ◦ Fz is an exterior support to D at x, with normal u0 and the same
gradient length as ψ at x′ . For an orthonormal basis e1 , e2 , e3 of u⊥

the common proof reads

                Hess(ψ ◦ Fz )(ea , ea ) = Hess ψ(Pz ea , Pz ea ) + dψ (∇ dFz )(ea , ea ) .

There is no error involving Hess ψ. The tensor estimate is

                                  tru⊥ Q(x) − trPz (u⊥ ) Q(x′ ) ≤ Cz.
                                       0                    0


These identities specialize the common proof’s comparison of the two support expansions; the
assumed inequality at x gives the bound k + Cz at x′ .

Lemma 9.5 (Smooth support enclosure). Assume the hypotheses of Lemma 9.1 at threshold c′ .
Let D ⋐ int X be a compact closed set whose every smooth exterior support has ΘQ ≤ k, where
k < c′ . Then
                                       D ⊂ Tc′ (X, Q).
In particular, the right side is nonempty whenever D is nonempty.

Proof. Assume D ̸= ∅. Choose β > 0 small enough that Dβ lies in int X, Lemma 9.4 applies for
0 < z ≤ β, and
                                      k + Cβ < c′ .                                   (9.11)
Smooth approximation of the distance function, followed by a regular-value choice, gives a smooth
compact region E with
                                Dβ/4 ⊂ int E ⊂ E ⊂ int D3β/4 .
For example, approximate the distance uniformly to accuracy β/16 and choose a regular level
between 7β/16 and 9β/16.
     Choose a smooth function a ≥ 0, supported where dist(·, D) < β, that is a sufficiently large
constant near ∂E. For Q′ = Q − ag we then have ΘQ′ (∂E) = ΘQ (∂E) − 3a < c′ . The boundary
barriers of X are unchanged. Lemma 9.1 applied to Q′ produces a nonempty smooth total region
T ′ containing E. Its frontier is nonempty because T ′ is compactly contained in the interior of the
connected manifold X with nonempty boundary. That outward frontier satisfies

                                           ΘQ (∂T ′ ) = c′ + 3a ≥ c′ .                          (9.12)

Since D has an open neighborhood in E ⊂ T ′ , the number z = dist(D, ∂T ′ ) is positive and
attained.
    Suppose z < β. Then Dz ⊂ T ′ . Indeed, a minimizing segment of length at most z from D
to a point outside T ′ would first cross ∂T ′ at distance strictly less than z. Such segments stay

                                                       64
in X by our choice of β. At a pair realizing the minimum distance, ∂T ′ is therefore an exterior
support to Dz . Its expansion is at most k + Cz < c′ by Lemma 9.4 and (9.11), contradicting
(9.12). Consequently z ≥ β.
    The frontier of T ′ is outside the support of a, so its expansion for the original tensor is exactly
c . Thus T ′ itself is an admissible region in (9.2) for (X, Q, c′ ). Since it contains D, the defining
 ′

maximality gives D ⊂ T ′ ⊂ Tc′ (X, Q).

9.4      The two colors and the trace allowance
Proposition 9.6 (Geometric preparation). For the prepared data satisfying (9.1), there are a
closed connected exterior Ω ⊂ M with one end and nonempty compact smooth boundary B, two
negative numbers cb , cw , and a decomposition of B into black and white components with the
following properties. With the normal ν into Ω and PB = trB K,

         H + PB = cb     on black components,      H − PB = cw      on white components.         (9.13)

The components come from closed total-region families Dc and Wc at right-continuity thresholds
cb and cw , respectively. The white family is formed with the black region fixed, and is allowed to
be empty. Every face has a disjoint smooth collar in Ω, with parameter s ≥ 0, | ds| > 0, and the
corresponding expansion toward increasing s is at least cb or cw .
    There are a0 > 0, a compactly supported smooth function C, and a positive smooth weight ρ,
equal to a positive constant times r−4−δ1 on the end, such that |C| ≤ a0 and, on smaller collars,

      C = a0 − κ B s   on black collars,    C = −a0 + κB s     on white collars,      κB > 0.    (9.14)

Set
                                  b− = cb − a0 < 0 < −cw + a0 = b+ .                             (9.15)
Every real-valued function h taking values in [b− , b+ ] on supp K ∩Ω satisfies the following inequality
on Ω:
                        |(h + C)τ | + | dC|g + ρ ≤ µ − |J|g ,       τ = trg K.                    (9.16)
There is a smooth compactly supported extension b on Ω of the boundary values b− on black faces
and b+ on white faces, constant on smaller collars. Finally, every smooth enclosing cut of B in
Ω has g-volume at least A∗ .

Proof. Choose a distant sphere SR0 beyond supp K, sufficiently large that it and all farther
coordinate spheres have positive mean curvature. Their expansions for both K and −K are
positive. Let X0 be the original compact core between S and SR0 . It is connected: paths using
the remote end can be replaced at its connected spherical cross section. Attach a smooth compact
filling F behind the entire original boundary. Such a filling can be constructed by taking an
oppositely oriented copy of X0 and capping its spherical boundary by a four-ball. Its remaining
boundary identifies with S. Extend g and K smoothly across S into this filling, keeping the metric
positive. Extension of the collar jets followed by a partition of unity does this; no constraint
condition is required in F . The resulting compact manifold X has only the connected outer
boundary SR0 .
     For later parameter choices, fix now

                               σ∗ = min(µ − |J|g ) > 0,     T∗ = max |τ |.
                                     X0                            X0



                                                   65
Choose γ > 0 so small that
                                           γT∗ ≤ σ∗ /8                                       (9.17)
and the original boundary has expansion less than −2γ. By strictness and continuity, F together
with a fixed short exterior collar of S is a smooth compact region whose outward boundary has
expansion less than −γ. For every c ∈ (−γ, 0) form

                                         Dc = Tc (X, K).

Here K denotes its smooth extension into F . Lemma 9.1 applies and Dc contains that entire filled
neighborhood. Its smooth frontier therefore lies strictly in the original data. Its complement in X
is connected. Indeed the collar of the connected outer sphere belongs to a unique complementary
component; every other component would be an interior hole and could be filled, contrary to
maximality.
    Choose cb ∈ (−γ, 0) at a right-continuity threshold by Lemma 9.2. Write Y for the closed
complement of int Dcb in X. This is a smooth compact connected manifold with the outer sphere
and the black frontier as its boundary. For the white tensor −K, all these boundary components
have strictly positive outward expansion. On a black component the outward normal of Y is the
reverse of the outward normal of Dcb , so the expansion is

                                   −H − tr∂Dcb K = −cb > 0.                                  (9.18)

This changes both the normal and the tensor in an auxiliary problem; the original boundary still
has its prescribed future sign.
   For c ∈ (−γ, 0) define the white family

                                        Wc = Tc (Y, −K).

It may be empty. Lemmas 9.1 and 9.2 give a right-continuity threshold cw ∈ (−γ, 0). Every
nonempty white region lies strictly inside Y , so Dcb and Wcw are disjoint closed sets, a positive
distance apart. Notice that the black threshold and manifold Y are fixed before making this
second threshold choice.
    In the filled one-ended manifold take the component of the open complement of Dcb ∪ Wcw
that reaches the end, and let Ω be its closure. Its boundary is the union of those entire frontier
components adjoining this exterior. To justify the word “entire,” a smooth connected frontier
component has a connected thin collar on each side; the component of its exterior side is therefore
constant along the frontier. Both frontiers are compact and smooth with finitely many components,
and they are disjoint. Thus Ω is smooth, closed, and connected, and its boundary B is a union of
complete smooth components. It has one end, the original end, and lies in the original manifold
because the black region contains the filling and a collar of S. Its boundary is nonempty: the
component reaching infinity is separated from that nonempty filled region. The inherited metric is
complete up to B; boundary charts give local completeness at finite limit points, and completeness
of the original end prevents escape in finite distance.
    Call its retained Dcb faces black and its retained Wcw faces white. The normals into Ω are
the outward normals of these regions. Their threshold equations give (9.13). Figure 1 illustrates
these orientations; ΩR there denotes the part of Ω inside a distant coordinate sphere SR . Apply
Lemma 9.3 to their respective total regions and shorten the collars so they lie on the Ω side
and are mutually disjoint. This gives all the asserted leaf inequalities. The support enclosure

Lemma 9.5 is available for the full families Dc and Wc on X and Y , respectively; in the latter
case the reversed black barriers remain fixed.
    All these collars lie in the fixed original core X0 , so the lower bound σ∗ was fixed before either
threshold or any collar width. For each collar choose a smooth profile qB (s) ∈ [0, 1], supported
in that collar, with qB (s) = 1 − αB s near its face and αB > 0. Such a profile is obtained by

                                       a0 (2T∗ + M∗ ) ≤ σ∗ /8.                                (9.19)
For b− ≤ h ≤ b+ we have |h + C| ≤ γ + 2a0 , and hence on X0 ∩ Ω

                         |(h + C)τ | + | dC| ≤ γT∗ + a0 (2T∗ + M∗ ) ≤ σ∗ /4.                    (9.20)

At points outside supp K the trace term vanishes, so this estimate requires no restriction there
on the value of h.
   Finally choose a constant ρ∗ > 0 so small that

                     ρ∗ ≤ c0 /4,           ρ∗ max r−4−δ1 ≤ σ∗ /4,        ρ = ρ∗ r−4−δ1 .

On X0 ∩ Ω, (9.20) plus this choice bounds the left side of (9.16) by σ∗ /2. Outside X0 , K = C = 0,
and (9.1) gives the required inequality directly. This proves (9.16). This order of choices uses no
lower bound on collar widths as cb , cw approach zero: the thresholds are chosen first, the collars
next, and their amplitude last. Ordinary collar cutoffs also give the stated compactly supported
smooth extension of the constants b− and b+ .
   If De is an admissible closed connected outer domain for a cut in Ω, it is also closed in M ,
because Ω is closed in M . It is a smooth codimension-zero submanifold of M , its interior lies in

                                                         67
int M , and it contains the distant original end. Its entire intrinsic boundary, including every part
coincident with B, is therefore an admissible original enclosing cut. Its area is at least A∗ . This
proves the final assertion without assuming the existence of a minimizing cut.


10     A boundary system for scalar curvature
Fix the prepared exterior and the geometric choices of Proposition 9.6. All contractions and
derivatives in this section use its background metric g, unless another metric is indicated. In
particular, τ = trg K is unrestricted. The boundary normal ν points into Ω. Let ΩR denote the
truncation at a large coordinate sphere SR , so its ordinary outward normal at its inner boundary
B is −ν.
    The purpose of the system is to produce a metric gb = e2t (g + l(t)2 df 2 ) with nonnegative
scalar curvature and negative boundary mean curvature. The parameter ηN below makes the
prescribed trace depend strictly increasingly on f . A separate penalty prevents t from reaching
−ϵ. This section proves that prevention using a coarse height bound. The narrower height interval
needed to use (9.16) will be proved subsequently.

10.1    Variables and a scalar-curvature identity
The warped-graph scalar identity and coupled-Penrose strategy of Bray and Khuri [9, 10] extend
the earlier Jang–Schoen–Yau graph method [22, 38]. The computation below fixes the dimension-
four coefficients and retains the unrestricted trace of K. The trace-discrepancy term already
appears before imposing the generalized Jang equation in [10, Identity 9, p. 580]. The prescribed
F = h + C, nonzero divergence source, and floor penalty are part of the present system; its
existence is proved independently, not inferred from an older zero-divergence coupling.
    Fix 0 < ϵ < 1 and a smooth nonincreasing function ϑ : R → [0, 1] equal to one on (−∞, 0]
and to zero on [1, ∞). For every integer N ≥ 4 define

10.2    Coercivity and the boundary identity
Throughout the proof, ΠN denotes a bound of the form C(1 + N )apol for a finite nonnegative
integer apol . Its coefficient C and exponent apol may depend on the fixed prepared data, ϵ, the
chosen collars and smooth switches, and any explicitly fixed small absorption constant. They
never depend on R, a homotopy parameter, or a solution. Different occurrences may denote larger
such bounds. Constants denoted by C(N ) in later fixed-N analytic estimates have no polynomial
restriction and will not enter a limit requiring polynomial control.
Lemma 10.2 (Polynomial coercivity). For all inputs in (10.2),

                             |T |2 + |M |2 + dj 2 + F 2 + | dt|2Ad ≤ ΠN T .                  (10.14)

               Vν                                                       √

Stage 1: Decrease λ from one to zero, keeping F = h + C and h|B = b.
Stage 2: Keep λ = 0, increase α0 from zero to one, and use F = h + C + α0 D0 (x′ , w, h) and

The endpoints agree, so this is a continuous homotopy. All trace right sides have derivative
at least one with respect to h, with (x′ , w, t) fixed. This is also the relevant derivative when
comparing scalar solutions at a shared gradient and shared Z, since t is independent of the value
of f .
    Two consequences of these choices will be useful. First, at a stage 3 face, evaluate the
prescribed right side at h = (1 − ζ)b and at the limiting values w = ±ν, d = χ = 0. It obeys
                                F (ν) ≥ PB + H,            F (−ν) ≤ PB − H.                      (10.24)
Indeed, on a black face b + C = PB + H, D0 (ν, b) = 0, and D0 (−ν, b) = −A0 ; on a white face
b + C = PB − H, D0 (−ν, b) = 0, and D0 (ν, b) = A0 . The terminal expressions are PB ± A1 .
Each inequality follows for both endpoints from the choices of A0 , A1 , and hence for their convex
combination.
   Second, throughout stage 4 the scalar solution is f = 0, for every trial Z. Its equation is
trAχ H f = ηN f + D1 , with zero boundary values. At a positive interior maximum w = 0, D1 = 0,
Aχ = Id, and l∆f = ηN f > 0, a contradiction; a negative minimum is excluded in the same way.
Consequently in this stage
               f = 0,   t = Z,      T = TK + 3(p + 1)| dt|2 ,           TK = 21 (|K|2 + τ 2 ),   (10.25)
and its two remaining equations are exactly
                   div(3u grad t) = γuΞ,             3∂ν t = γ[1 − j0 (t)](−H − NB ).

Lemma 10.4 (Height bound before the floor). There exist fixed r0 and C∗ > 0, independent of

                   |h| + |F | ≤ C∗ ,    |h| ≤ C∗ (r−3/2 − R−3/2 )          (r0 ≤ r ≤ R).             (10.26)

                                                   −1 −5/2

                                         t > −ϵ on SR .                                       (10.28)

        l∆f = h + C − τ      in stages 1 and 2,           l∆f = h + (1 − ζ)(C − τ )    in stage 3.

D0 , D1 are bounded and ∥Aχ ∥ ≤ 1.

10.5    The stationary comparison at a minimum of t
The scalar identity is useful at a floor contact because a second, Riemannian Gauss calculation
gives an upper bound for the same scalar curvature. Define, for a symmetric tensor A and a
chosen unit axis e,
                             1          1
                       S(A) = (tr⊥ A)2 − |A0⊥ |2 + dAee tr⊥ A − d|Ae⊥ |2 .                     (10.30)
                             3          2
Lemma 10.5 (Stationary Gauss comparison). At an interior local minimum of t,
                            1 2t        1
                              e Scalgb ≤ Scalg −v Ricg (e, e) + S(H f ).                       (10.31)
                            2           2
At every point where dt = 0 there are an absolute c2 > 0 and a polynomial bound ΠN such that

                               T − S(H f ) ≥ c2 T − ΠN vF 2 + |K|2 .                           (10.32)
                                                                         


These conclusions extend to df = 0 with any unit axis.
Proof. Use the Riemannian warped product g + l2 ( db′ )2 and its graph b′ = f . At dt = 0 one has
dl = 0, so the graph second form is H f , up to its immaterial overall sign. Its inverse metric is Ad .
The contracted second-form contribution is

                               2 {(trAd H ) − |H |Ad ⊗Ad } = S(H ),
                               1         f 2    f 2             f


as is seen by writing Ad = diag(1, 1, 1, d). The ambient scalar curvature and Ricci blocks are

                           Scalg −2∆l/l,       Ricg − Hess l/l,      −∆l/l,

for the scalar, horizontal block, and unit vertical direction; the
                                                                 √ mixed block is zero. The graph
unit normal has horizontal part −w and vertical component d. Therefore the ambient scalar
minus twice its normal Ricci, divided by two, is
                               1
                                 Scalg −v Ricg (e, e) − v tr⊥ (Hess l/l).
                               2
At a stationary point Hess l/l = p Hess t, and the conformal term is −3 trAd Hess t. Thus we have,
more precisely, the equality
                       1 2t        1
                         e Scalgb = Scalg −v Ricg (e, e) + S(H f )
                       2           2                                                           (10.33)
                                   − (3 + pv) tr⊥ Hessg t − 3d Hessg t(e, e).

At a minimum the last two terms are nonpositive, proving (10.31).
   For (10.32), first subtract S(Sf ). An expansion using tr T = F − χj gives exactly

                      T − S(Sf ) = |T 0 |2 + (1 + d)|M |2 + χ(1 + d − 2χ/3)j 2

At d = χ = 1 the (F, j) quadratic is F 2 /3 − 2F j/3 + 4j 2 /3, whose matrix has determinant 1/3
and positive trace. It is uniformly positive in a fixed neighborhood of this point. Since
                                                            (3 + p)v

In that region (10.34) controls an absolute fraction of |T 0 |2 + |M |2 + χj 2 + F 2 .

                        d2   d(3 + pv)   3+N                     κ0    κ0

Lemma 10.6 (Errors from the trace switches and drift). Let K be a fixed compact enlargement
of the supports of K, C, b, D0 , D1 . For every fixed δ2 > 0, every smooth trace solution in stages
1–3 satisfies, at a point where dt = 0,
                                            w(F ) ≥ ηN aσ − δ2 T − ΠN 1K ,
                            −1
                                                                                               (10.36)
                          u      div(uAχ K(w, ·)) ≤ δ2 T + ΠN 1K .
The polynomial may depend on δ2 and the fixed geometric choices, but no negative power of ℓ or
ηN is required.
Proof. At dt = 0, differentiation gives
                   ∇i w = Ad Hi·f ,     d log u = H f (w, ·),     dp = p′ (t) dt = 0.
These formulas and (10.15) show
                                                              √
                           |∇w| + | d log u|Aχ + |∇Aχ | ≤ ΠN ( T + |K|).                       (10.37)
For clarity, the axial entry in ∇w is dHee
                                         f , and its control uses d2 /χ ≤ (3 + N )/3. In | d log u|
                                                                                                    Aχ
                                √
the axial entry has the factor χ. Differentiating Aχ = Id −(3 + p)w ⊗ w/(3 + pv) uses only the
preceding ∇w and coefficients bounded by powers of 1 + N , since 3 + pv ≥ 3. This proves (10.37)
without dividing by l or ηN .
   Write the relevant prescribed trace as Φ(x′ , w, t, h). Its height derivative is at least one, so
the chain rule contains the favorable term
                                       Φh w(h) ≥ w(h) = ηN aσ.
All other terms are supported in K. Lemma 10.4 bounds their height arguments in a fixed range;
derivatives of D0 , D1 and b are consequently bounded. Derivatives of trAχ K use ∇K and (10.37).

                                                  76
10.6    Exclusion of first contact with the floor
Proposition 10.7 (First-floor exclusion). There are a fixed δ0 > 0 and a positive polynomial CN
in (10.19) such that, for every N ≥ 4 and every R ≥ Rmin (N ), no smooth solution at any stage
of the homotopy satisfies
                                          min t = −ϵ.
                                             ΩR

The choices depend only on the prepared data, ϵ, and the fixed switches and collars. They do not
use a height bound sharper than Lemma 10.4.

Proof. Suppose first that a floor minimum is interior and the parameter is in one of stages 1–3. At
that point dt = 0 and Hess t ≥ 0. Subtract (10.31) from (10.5) and use Vλ = V −(1−λ)uAχ K(w, ·).
This gives

            u−1 div Vλ ≥ µ − 21 Scalg +J(w) + v Ricg (e, e) + T − S(H f ) + w(F ) − F τ
                          − (1 − λ)u−1 div(uAχ K(w, ·)).

Choose δ2 > 0 in Lemma 10.6 so small that the two losses δ2 T leave at least c2 T /2 in (10.32). The
remaining tensor expressions µ − Scalg /2 = (τ 2 − |K|2 )/2, J, and F τ are compactly supported
and bounded by fixed constants, using Lemma 10.4. On the complement of K, F = h and
F 2 ≤ Cr−3 ; also | Ricg | ≤ Cr−4 . On K the positive weight ρ0 has a fixed positive minimum.
Hence
                               1K + vF 2 + v| Ricg | ≤ C(ρ0 + vρ1 ).
Combining these bounds yields an absolute c3 > 0 and a polynomial QN ≥ 0 such that, at the
proposed minimum,
                        u−1 div Vλ ≥ c3 T + ηN aσ − QN (ρ0 + vρ1 ).                (10.38)
Fix 0 < δ0 < min(c3 /2, 1/4). Since t = −ϵ gives m0 (t) = 1, choose CN to exceed QN + 1 +
supΩ (ρ/ρ0 ). The right side of (10.38) then strictly exceeds δ0 T + ρ + δ0 ηN aσ − CN (ρ0 + vρ1 ) = Ξ,
contradicting (10.21). This choice is polynomial in N .
   At an interior minimum in stage 4, (10.25) gives f = 0, t = Z, and v = 0. If γ > 0, its
equation at the stationary point is

                                    3∆t = γ δ0 TK + ρ − CN ρ0 .
                                                                


Enlarge CN , still polynomially, so that CN > 1 + supΩ (δ0 TK + ρ)/ρ0 . The right side is strictly
negative, contradicting ∆t ≥ 0. This additional bound is finite because K is compactly supported.
    Consider next an inner boundary minimum in stages 1–3. There j0 (−ϵ) = 0, so the same drift
correction appears on both sides of (10.21). Cancelling it gives Vν /u = B. Equation (10.18) and
d > 0 imply
                                      3∂ν t = −H − NB < 0.
This contradicts the nonnegative derivative into ΩR at a boundary minimum. In stage 4 with
γ > 0, the corresponding exact formula from (10.25) is 3∂ν t = γ(−H − NB ) < 0, giving the same
contradiction. The outer boundary is excluded by (10.28).
   Finally, at γ = 0, f = 0 and the remaining problem Ris div(3u grad t) = 0 with homogeneous
inner conormal data and t = 0 on SR . Testing by t gives ΩR 3u| dt|2 = 0. Connectedness and the
nonempty Dirichlet boundary imply t = 0. Thus no stage admits floor equality.



                                                  77
    The order of choices is now fixed: choose the background geometric data and ϵ, then δ0 and
the polynomial penalty CN . At each N , enlarge the outer radius as in Lemma 10.4. Subsequent
estimates may increase the lower bound on N or Rmin (N ), but none will replace CN by an
arbitrary fixed-N constant. In particular, the first-floor argument has paid for F τ using a
bounded compact error and has not used the eventual small-height absorption in (9.16).


11     Separating the graph from the two frontiers
Throughout this section the prepared geometry, its thresholds and collars, and ϵ ∈ (0, 1) are fixed.
We consider arbitrary smooth solutions of the four stages of the homotopy in Section 10, on ΩR ,
satisfying t ≥ −ϵ. The admissible radii satisfy R ≥ Rmin (N ), where Rmin (N ) → ∞ and is large
enough for the outer-boundary conclusion of Proposition 10.7. Every assertion of uniformity
includes the homotopy parameter and R. As in Remark 8.5, ΠN denotes a polynomial bound in
N , whereas C(N ) can depend arbitrarily on the fixed integer N . We shall keep this distinction
through the boundary-flux estimate.

11.1    The limiting heights and the two total regions
The large factor l/ηN forces a nonconstant test level of h to approach a hypersurface whose
expansion is controlled by the trace equation. A sublevel of the relaxed limiting height need not
have a regular boundary. We first establish the expansion inequality for every smooth exterior
support, including at a plateau and at a face of the opposite color.

Proposition 11.1 (Separation from the two total regions). Let A ⊂ Ω be compact. In the first
two homotopy stages the following statements hold.

  (i) If A ∩ Dcb = ∅, there exist ξA > 0 and NA such that h ≥ b− + ξA on A for every N ≥ NA .

 (ii) If A ∩ Wcw = ∅, there exist ξA > 0 and NA such that h ≤ b+ − ξA on A for every N ≥ NA .

Here the total regions are the full closed regions of Proposition 9.6, including components whose
frontiers do not border Ω.

Proof. We give the lower-height argument first. Consider any sequence Ni → ∞ of the indicated
solutions; its radii tend to infinity. Extend hi by the constant b+ into a small collar across each
white face. The functions on these enlarged neighborhoods are continuous. Define their lower
relaxed limit by
                                         h(x) = lim inf hi (y).
                                                 i→∞
                                                 y→x

It is lower semicontinuous and locally bounded by Lemma 10.4. Fix levels

                         b− < k < k ′ < min{0, b− + ξ1 },      k ′ + a0 < 0,                   (11.1)

where ξ1 is the height-cutoff constant in D0 . We initially work away from black faces.
   Suppose a smooth ϕ touches h from below at x, with h(x) < k ′ and dϕ(x) ̸= 0. Subtracting a
small fourth-order function of distance makes the contact strict on a small closed neighborhood
without changing its second jet. The definition of the relaxed limit and minimization on this
neighborhood give, after passing to a subsequence, points xi → x and constants ci such that
ϕ + ci touches hi from below at xi , and hi (xi ) → h(x). These contacts are interior contacts of the

                                                 78
original exterior: their heights are eventually strictly below k ′ < b+ , so they cannot occur on a
white face or in its constant extension. This remains true when x itself is on a white face.
    At contact, dhi = dϕ and Hess hi ≥ Hess ϕ. The positive trace matrix therefore gives
                           1
       trAχi K + q                     trAχi Hess ϕ ≤ hi + C + αi D0 ,         0 ≤ αi ≤ 1.       (11.2)
                  (ηNi /li )2 + | dϕ|2

Here αi = 0 in the first stage. The cutoff construction gives D0 ≤ 0 at these low heights,
everywhere: its white contribution vanishes there, and its black contribution is nonpositive.
Moreover
                                  li    ℓi
                                     ≥     = eϵNi /2 −→ ∞.
                                 ηNi   ηNi
Consequently |wi | → 1, di → 0, χi → 0, and Aχi tends to the orthogonal projection onto (grad ϕ)⊥ .
Passing to the limit in (11.2) proves
                                                   tr(grad ϕ)⊥ Hess ϕ
                       ΘK [ϕ] := tr(grad ϕ)⊥ K +                      ≤ k ′ + a0 .               (11.3)
                                                          | dϕ|
The normal here points toward increasing ϕ.
    We next transfer this test inequality to the closed set Dk = {h ≤ k} by Lemma 5.1, with
ambient dimension four, u = h, tensor Q = K, and q0 = k ′ + a0 . The coarse height bound makes
u finite, its defining relaxed limit makes it lower semicontinuous, and Equation (11.3) gives the
required bound for every nonzero-gradient lower test at a height below k ′ . Away from black faces
these hypotheses hold on open neighborhoods; at white faces use the constant extensions already
constructed. The lemma therefore bounds every exterior support of Dk there by k ′ + a0 , including
when the sublevel has a plateau.
    This argument also rules out a boundary layer at a white face. The constant extension need
not make h continuous there. Nevertheless all approximating low contacts used above are interior,
so the support inequality holds at a limiting white-face contact. At every point strictly on the
added side the relaxed limit equals b+ > k, so Dk lies locally in the original closed exterior. The
reversed white face would therefore be an exterior support of Dk with expansion

                                        −H + PB = −cw > 0,

contradicting k ′ + a0 < 0. Hence Dk misses every white face.
     Adjoin the full black total region and consider Dk ∪ Dcb in the filled black barrier manifold.
At a frontier point belonging to ∂Dcb , an exterior support of this union also supports the smooth
region Dcb , so smooth tangency bounds its expansion by cb . At other frontier points the bound is
k ′ + a0 > cb . The coarse end-height decay makes the additional sublevel compact. It lies strictly
inside the distant barrier sphere: otherwise a sphere at its greatest radius is an exterior support
with positive expansion, a contradiction. There are no remaining contacts with the boundary of
the filled barrier manifold. Lemma 9.5 therefore gives

                            Dk ∪ Dcb ⊂ Dc′     whenever k ′ + a0 < c′ < 0                        (11.4)

in the chosen threshold range.
    If A is disjoint from Dcb , its distance from that closed region is positive. Right continuity first
permits c′ > cb close enough that A ∩ Dc′ = ∅. We then choose k, k ′ as in (11.1), close enough to
b− that k ′ + a0 < c′ . It follows that A ∩ Dk = ∅. If a uniform eventual lower bound h ≥ k on A

                                                   79
failed, a sequence of violating points in the compact set A would give a point of A ∩ Dk in its
lower relaxed limit. This contradiction proves the first claim, with, for example, ξA = k − b− .
    For the second claim apply the same proof to (−h, −K, −C). The low-height modification is
now −D0 and again has the required nonpositive sign. Extend across black faces by the high
constant −b− . At a limiting contact, the reversed black face has expansion −cb > 0 for −K, and
is excluded before using the white barrier manifold. Adjoining Wcw and applying Lemma 9.5 now
places the sublevel in Wc′ . Right continuity at cw finishes the proof. This also covers an empty
chosen white region: the distance-cap formulation of right continuity makes the nearby regions
empty if necessary, and a nonempty enclosed sublevel is then impossible.

11.2    Collar comparisons and boundary slopes
We shorten the disjoint collars to 0 ≤ s ≤ s0 so that the offset is exactly linear there. Their
outer leaves are a positive distance from the corresponding closed total region. Proposition 11.1
therefore provides a strict height gap on those leaves in the first two stages. All these leaves and
the compact sets needed below are fixed before increasing N .

    At any hypothetical crossing extremum, the test and the solution have the same gradient and
the same Z, hence the same t and positive trace matrix. The prescribed right side is increasing
in h with derivative at least one. The strict residual signs, Hessian comparison, and the endpoint
inequalities exclude such a crossing. Differentiating the resulting barriers at s = 0, and using
upper and lower positive bounds for | ds|, proves (11.5) on black faces. Sign reversal of h, K, C
proves the white assertion. The compatible-direction modification again vanishes there.

                         Fpresc (ν) ≥ PB + H,         Fpresc (−ν) ≤ PB − H.

Choose the minimum slope to dominate the Cs error and the coarse height at the outer collar
leaf. The logarithmic curvature contribution in (11.7) has the favorable sign for both tests and
dominates Cd on s ≤ 1/N ; on s ≥ 1/N the linear height term dominates it. The same comparison
proves (11.6). All constants are uniform in ζ ∈ [0, 1] by smoothness of the prescribed convex
combination. The assertion for stage four is the scalar maximum-principle conclusion already
proved in the homotopy construction.

of smaller own-color collars yields

                                        M (r) ≤ qM (s) + C(N, q)(s − r)−2 .                                           (11.29)

Choose increasing radii rj tending to a fixed larger radius, with rj+1 − rj proportional to 2−j , and
take q < 1/4. Iteration sums a geometric series with ratio 4q < 1. The remaining q j M (rj ) tends
to zero: for each individual smooth solution all these patches lie in one compact neighborhood
with finite supremum. Thus M (r0 ) ≤ C(N ) uniformly. A finite cover gives a uniform Z bound
on a compact set containing B and the support of K.
    Outside this set K = 0. At an interior maximum with Z > Z0 (N ), dZ = 0 and

                                  div(3uAχ grad Z) = 3u trAχ Hess Z ≤ 0,

whereas uΞ > 0 by (11.19). The outer value is Z = 0, so this maximum argument extends the
compact bound through the end. This proves the stage-one Z estimate.
    In stages two and three λ = 0, so the same maximum argument works everywhere in the
interior. On B the already established face-gradient bounds show that Z can be high only if
t ≥ 1: if t < 1, then
                                                                                −2
                       Z = t + 16 log(1 + l2 | df |2 ) ≤ 1 + 61 log(1 + e2 C42 ηN  ).

                                                               86
For t ≥ 1 the boundary switch satisfies j0 = 1 and the prescribed conormal flux in (10.21) is zero.
A high boundary maximum would contradict the boundary point principle applied locally on the
high-level set to div(3uAχ grad Z) = uΞ > 0. For this application the individual smooth solution
gives a smooth positive elliptic matrix; no uniform ellipticity constant is being assumed before
the bound. Equivalently, the outward derivative at a nonconstant boundary maximum is strictly
positive, whereas the zero conormal flux forces it to vanish. This bounds Z in these two stages.
    In stage four f = 0, hence t = Z. At a positive scale multiplying the source and boundary
data, the same high-level interior and boundary arguments apply. At zero scale the homogeneous
mixed problem gives Z = 0. The resulting bound is uniform in the scale.
    Finally Z − t = 16√log D ≥ 0, so the floor and the upper bound give D ≤ e6(C(N )+ϵ) . Since
l ≥ ℓ > 0, | df | ≤ ℓ−1 D − 1 ≤ C(N ). The coarse bound for h = ηN f gives |f | ≤ C(N ). This
proves all of (11.25).

    The bounded range now permits fixed-N regularity and exhaustion. The final mass estimate
will use only the earlier polynomial bounds in Lemma 11.3 and (11.14); the graph Sobolev and
Moser constants are not needed in that limit.


12     Four-dimensional regularity up to the boundary
The second equation has quadratic growth in both grad Z and Hess f . A bound for its ellipticity
constants therefore does not close the regularity argument. We first prove an independent scalar
estimate which gives a positive Morrey exponent for | Hess f |2 . That exponent permits a measure
correction in the second equation. All constants in this section may depend arbitrarily on a fixed
N ; none of them is subsequently used as a polynomial bound ΠN . In coordinate calculations Dx ,
or D when there is no ambiguity, denotes ordinary differentiation, whereas D = 1 + l2 | grad f |2 in
the system retains its earlier meaning.

12.1    An independent scalar estimate
Theorem 12.1 (Measurable axial coefficient). Let U be a four-dimensional coordinate ball B2 ,
or a half-ball B2+ = B2 ∩ {x4 > 0}. Assume that the metric is smooth, Cg−1 Id ≤ (gij ) ≤ Cg Id,
and that its coordinate C 2 norm is at most Cg . Assume also uniformly bounded C 3 norms for the
charts and inverse charts. On a half-ball use boundary normal coordinates, so g44 = 1 and ga4 = 0
for a < 4. Let z be C 3 , up to the flat face when present, and constant on that face. Suppose,
almost everywhere, that

                         Az : Hessg z = G,         Az = Id −(1 − e
                                                                 χ)ez ⊗ ez ,
                                      grad z                                                   (12.1)
                               ez =            ,        0 < χ0 ≤ χ
                                                                 e ≤ 1.
                                     | grad z|

Here χ e and G are measurable. At grad z = 0, any measurable unit axis making the equation true
is allowed. If | grad z| ≤ M1 and |G| ≤ N1 , then there are α ∈ (0, 1), r0 > 0 and C < ∞ such that
                                           Z
     ∥ grad z∥C α (U1 ) ≤ C(M1 + N1 ),                  | Hessg z|2 dVg ≤ C(M1 + N1 )2 r2+2α   (12.2)
                                             Br (x)∩U

for x ∈ U1 and 0 < r ≤ r0 , where U1 = B1 or B1+ . The constants depend only on χ0 , the stated
geometry bounds and the separation from the curved patch edges. The C α norm may equivalently


                                                   87
be taken for the coordinate gradient components. In particular, no modulus of continuity of G or
χ, and no bound for a derivative of either, is assumed.
e

Proof. We prove the assertion on uniformly small full balls, using a reflection at the flat face.
Rescaling back and a finite local cover give the stated form. The argument first establishes a
local Hessian estimate and a dichotomy: on a smaller ball, either the gradient bound decreases or
the solution is close to a nonconstant affine function. A fixed-axis estimate then improves the
affine approximation. Iterating these alternatives gives Hölder gradient control, from which the
local Hessian estimate yields the Morrey bound.

Doubling and a quantitative Hessian estimate. Subtract the constant boundary value.
With R4 = diag(1, 1, 1, −1), extend the tangential metric block evenly and set ze(x) = −z(R4 x)
below the plane. Tangential derivatives of z vanish on the face and the two normal derivatives
agree. Thus ze is C 1 ; its distributional coordinate Hessian has no surface measure. The doubled
metric is Lipschitz and has the prescribed smooth bounds on each closed side. On the reflected
side the gradient and covariant Hessian are −R4 grad z and −R4 (Hessg z)R4 , respectively. Extend
e evenly and G oddly. Equation (12.1) then holds almost everywhere on the double. Each
χ
individual doubled function is locally W 2,p for every finite p, although these norms are not yet
uniformly bounded.
    Make a constant linear coordinate change so that the metric at the center of a ball is the

concentric gaps decreasing geometrically and choose ε so that the resulting geometric series

A divergence inequality for the gradient deficit. Divide by M1 + N1 unless this number is
zero, in which case the claim is immediate. Subtract a constant and rescale space by r and height
by r. The new gradient has norm at most one and the new forcing has norm at most r. Metric
first derivatives and the second fundamental form of a reflecting plane are O(r); curvature on the
smooth sides is O(r2 ). After normalizing the metric at the center, all of these errors can be made
uniformly small. In this paragraph write P = grad z, Hz = Hessg z, and s = 1 − |P |2g ≥ 0.
     There is an exact cancellation of the measurable axial coefficient:

                      Az grad(|P |2g /2) − GP = (Hz − ∆g z g)P =: Q,                              (12.5)
                                         div Q = |Hz |2g − (∆g z)2 + Ricg (P, P ).                (12.6)

Indeed grad(|P |2g /2) = Hz P and ∆g z = G + (1 − e χ)Hz (ez , ez ). The axial parts in the first formula
cancel. In the second formula, div Hz = grad ∆g z + Ricg (P, ·) cancels the third derivatives. Both
identities hold also at P = 0, because the first has both sides zero there. At no point is a derivative
of G or χe taken. Choose ε0 > 0 with (1 + ε0 )(1 − χ0 )2 < 1 when χ0 < 1. Young’s inequality, or
directly ∆g z = G if χ0 = 1, gives

                       |Hz |2g − (∆g z)2 ≥ c|Hz |2g − C|G|2 ,       c = c(χ0 ) > 0.               (12.7)

    We compute the only surface error in this identity. In boundary normal coordinates on the
original side, P = z4 ∂4 at the plane and, for a, b < 4,

                                   (Hz )ab = −Γ4ab z4 = 21 (∂4 gab )z4 .

Writing HB = 12 g ab ∂4 gab for the mean curvature toward increasing x4 , this gives

                                   Q4 = (z44 − ∆g z)z4 = −HB z42 .

Thus the jump of the volume-weighted normal flux on the double is bounded by C| IIB ||P |2 . In
particular it contains no uncontrolled second derivative of z. A bounded plane density j(x′ ) is
the distributional divergence of j(x′ )1{x4 >0} ∂4 ; only a normal derivative is taken in this assertion.
After a linear coordinate change use the corresponding constant normal direction. Subtracting
this bounded step field handles√the signed jump, without imposing any tangential regularity on j.

                                               Z

The coordinate Hessian also tends to zero in L2 , because the connection coefficients tend to zero
and Dz is bounded. After subtracting z(0), the functions are equi-Lipschitz. Poincaré gives
convergence of their gradients in L2 to constants, and compactness gives uniform convergence
to an affine function on smaller balls. Since s → 0 in measure and the metrics converge to the
identity, its slope has length one. If (12.9) failed for every k∗ ↑ 1 and every tolerance tending to
zero, this conclusion would contradict failure of its affine alternative. This proves the claim.

directly. If Lr , Lr/2 are two such approximations, then ∥Lr − Lr/2 ∥L∞ (Br/2 ) ≤ Cr1+α , whence

separated by h, compare the two approximations on overlapping balls of radius 4h; the same
affine norm estimate gives a slope difference Chα . Their limits differ from those slopes by Chα as
well. This proves the first estimate in (12.2).

The difference Hessg z − D2 z has L2 norm at most Cr2 . Since α < 1, squaring and restoring

12.2    The measure estimate needed in dimension four
The second regularity step is Lemma 6.2 with n = 4. Its input is a bounded scalar function
satisfying
                                  div(ADZ + B1 ) = E,                               (12.20)
with symmetric measurable uniformly elliptic A, bounded B1 , and a signed source dominated by

               |E| ≤ C0 (1 + |DZ|2 ) dx + µ1 ,       µ1 (Br (x)) ≤ Cµ r2+β ,   β > 0.     (12.21)

The preceding axial theorem will supply the Hessian part of µ1 . A bounded density on a reflecting
three-plane contributes O(r3 ), which also has the required positive exponent. The shared lemma
absorbs |DZ|2 by its exponential product rule and bounds the remaining Green correction by
C(r + rβ ).
    In the application below each individual solution is smooth, and its reflection is continuous
and smooth on the two closed sides. Its common trace therefore defines the measure products
and verifies that product rule, including the plane flux. The Hölder representative supplied by
the lemma agrees everywhere with this continuous function. We now verify the coefficients, the
reflected source, and the ball-growth bound for the actual homotopy before applying the estimate.

12.3    Application to the homotopy and the boundary estimates
Proposition 12.2. Fix the prepared data, ϵ, a sufficiently large N and any stage of the homotopy.
Every smooth solution on an allowed finite truncation ΩR with t ≥ −ϵ has uniform local estimates
for every finite number of derivatives of f and Z, up to both the inner and outer boundary faces.
The constants on the fixed compact part and on uniform unit patches of the end are independent
of R and of the homotopy parameters. They may depend arbitrarily on N . On each fixed finite
truncation they therefore bound ∥Z∥C 1,α0 for every prescribed 0 < α0 < 1.
Proof. We use the uniform family of interior and boundary patches described in the statement,
shrinking them by fixed factors when necessary.

Linear estimates and absorption of the quadratic term. Fix p > 4. On a ball or smooth

                    ∥D2 Z∥Lp (Qh ) ≤ C∗ ∥DZ∥2L2p (Q2h ) + Ch 1 + ∥Z∥W 1,p (Q2h ) .           (12.30)

Here C∗ is independent of sufficiently small h; Ch may contain inverse powers of h. The constants
are uniform over the locations and the truncations in question.
    We specify the linear estimate underlying this assertion. Freeze the continuous principal
matrix at the patch center. The interior constant-coefficient Hessian estimate follows from the
Euclidean one by a fixed linear change of coordinates. At the boundary, normal coordinates give
no mixed normal–tangential entries in the frozen matrix, because its exceptional axis is normal
there. A tangential linear transformation and a normal dilation reduce the frozen principal part to
the Laplacian and preserve the plane. Subtract a Sobolev extension of the normal datum, or of the
Dirichlet datum, to obtain homogeneous boundary data. Even reflection for homogeneous normal
data and odd reflection for homogeneous Dirichlet data give the constant-coefficient half-space
estimate. The extension costs precisely the W 1−1/p,p norm for the normal datum. Coefficient
oscillation contributes sup |a2 − a2 (x0 )| ∥D2 Z∥p and is absorbed on small patches, uniformly by

                                                  96
the established Hölder bound. Cutoffs and interpolation handle the first order terms. Scaling
shows that the coefficient of the interior Lp source norm is the constant C∗ , independent of h;
the lower order and trace terms account for Ch . This proves (12.30) by the scalar local estimates
[18]. The construction requires no meeting of different boundary types, and the inner and outer
faces here are disjoint.
    For use in its nonlinear right hand side, the scaled scalar interpolation inequality on Qh is
                                                                                            2
                  ∥DZ∥2L2p (Qh ) ≤ C oscQh Z ∥D2 Z∥Lp (Qh ) + Ch4/p−2 oscQh Z .                         (12.31)

One can obtain it on the unit patch by subtracting a constant, using a bounded Sobolev
extension on a smooth ball or half-patch, and applying the bounded-domain Gagliardo–Nirenberg
inequality with derivative orders 1, 2, exponents 2p, p, ∞ and parameter 1/2. A convenient modern
formulation is [28, Theorem 1.3]. To see the interpolation mechanism directly for a compactly
supported function v, integration by parts gives
           Z                      Z                                  Z            1−1/p
               |Dv|2p ≤ Cp ∥v∥∞        |Dv|2p−2 |D2 v| ≤ Cp ∥v∥∞          |Dv|2p            ∥D2 v∥p .

Division gives the required product estimate. Extension and cutoff produce the additional ∥v∥2∞
term on a bounded unit patch. This lower-order term is essential: the bounded-domain Gagliardo–
Nirenberg estimate includes an additive multiple of ∥v∥∞ , so one may not drop its square after
applying the estimate to v = Z − c. Taking c between the maximum and minimum replaces this
norm by the oscillation used here. Finally rescaling x = hy multiplies a squared L2p gradient
norm and the Hessian product by h4/p−2 , which proves precisely the last power in (12.31).
   Here is a cover argument that absorbs larger-patch norms without assuming their uniform
boundedness in advance. Use uniformly comparable patches of radius h covering the finite
truncation, and their fixed-factor enlargements. Each enlargement is covered by at most Mcov
smaller patches, where Mcov is independent of h, R and the total number of patches. Let

                                          S = sup ∥D2 Z∥Lp (Qj ) .
                                                j              h



For each individual smooth solution on the finite truncation, S is finite. The Hölder estimate
for Z gives oscQj Z ≤ C(N )hγ . Apply (12.31) in the enlarged patches and then cover them by
                 2h
                                                                          1/p
smaller ones. Its contribution to (12.30) is at most C∗ C(N )hγ Mcov S + C(N, h). Choose h small
enough, also satisfying the linear freezing condition, that this coefficient is at most 1/4. For the
remaining first order term, local interpolation gives, for any ε > 0,

                       ∥DZ∥Lp (Qj ) ≤ ε∥D2 Z∥Lp (Qj ) + C(h, ε)∥Z∥Lp (Qj ) .

Choose ε so that Ch εMcov ≤ 1/4. The bounded range controls the last term. Taking the
supremum in (12.30) now gives S ≤ C(N, h) + S/2, and hence a uniform bound for S. First order
interpolation supplies the corresponding local W 2,p bounds for Z. All constants may depend on
the now fixed h and on N , but not on the total number of patches or R.

Alternating Schauder estimates and uniform geometry. Since p > 4, Sobolev embedding
                 ′
gives Z ∈ C 1,θ for some θ′ > 0. In conjunction with (12.27), the trace equation now has
     ′′
C 1,θ coefficients and source for 0 < θ′′ ≤ min{θ, θ′ }. One differentiation and the scalar Dirichlet


                                                    97
                                ′′                                   ′′
Schauder estimate give f ∈ C 3,θ . The equation for Z then has C 0,θ source, and its normal datum
                ′′                                                                              ′′
(12.29) is C 1,θ . The scalar normal-derivative or Dirichlet Schauder estimate gives Z ∈ C 2,θ .
The boundary estimate is justified by the same frozen half-space problem used above: its normal
derivative does not annihilate a nonzero decaying solution of the homogeneous principal equation.
Smooth normal coordinates and smooth normal field therefore satisfy its obliqueness condition.
    Repeating in the order f then Z increases the number of derivatives by one each time. The
trace equation uses Z without derivatives; the expanded second equation uses only D2 f and DZ
in its lower terms; and its boundary expression uses only Df . These are exactly the derivative
counts needed for the iteration. The smooth fixed coefficients give all finite orders.
    The compact inner faces have uniform smooth collars. On the prepared end, the metric
and all fixed coefficient fields have uniform bounds on unit patches. Large coordinate spheres
have boundary normal charts with uniform bounds of each finite order, and are separated from
the inner faces. Thus every radius, ellipticity bound, chart constant and local covering number
used above is independent of the sufficiently large truncation radius. Finally a uniform local C 2
bound controls any prescribed C 1,α0 norm, α0 < 1, on a fixed finite truncation. This proves the
proposition.

Remark 12.3. Theorem 12.1 also applies in the scalar trial problem below. Its separate height
and gradient bounds give bounded source and a positive axial eigenvalue without the floor.
Interpolation of the principal matrix with Id preserves its three unit transverse eigenvalues. A
trial Z ∈ C 1,α0 then supplies the coefficient regularity for scalar Schauder estimates through
f ∈ C 3,α0 ; this requires DZ, but no second derivative of the trial input.


13     Solving the boundary system and controlling its energy
We now construct solutions of the system, exhaust the asymptotically flat end, and compare the
resulting Riemannian energy with the energy of the prepared data. The distinction between two
kinds of constants is essential. Constants in the scalar solution map and in local regularity may
depend arbitrarily on a fixed N ; constants used in the final flux estimate are the polynomial
bounds ΠN established earlier.

13.1    A scalar solution map on the whole trial space
Fix 0 < α < 1, a sufficiently large integer N , and an allowed truncation ΩR . In this subsection
constants may depend on R, N , and a bound for the trial input in

                                XR = {Z ∈ C 1,α (ΩR ) : Z|SR = 0}.

The four stages of the homotopy are parametrized consecutively by s ∈ [0, 4], with s = 0 the desired
system and s = 4 its zero terminal scale. Denote their trace prescriptions by Fspresc (x, w, h, t).
All dependence suppressed in this notation is the smooth dependence specified in the homotopy.

Lemma 13.1 (Scalar trial problem). For every Z ∈ XR and s ∈ [0, 4], the prescribed trace
equation with the assigned constant Dirichlet value on every boundary component has a unique
solution fs [Z] ∈ C 3,α (ΩR ). The map (s, Z) 7→ fs [Z] is continuous into C 3,α and maps bounded
trial sets to bounded sets, uniformly in s. No lower bound for the implicitly defined t is imposed
on the trial inputs.



                                                98
Proof. We use the scalar method of continuity in the affine space of C 3,α functions with the
assigned boundary values, with equation space C 1,α . Thus every solution estimated along this
continuation already has the differentiability required by Theorem 12.1; the estimates below are
uniform bounds, not an assumption of uniform third derivatives.
    The normalized equation is

uniformly in both parameters and the bounded trial set.

    On the inward collar of a boundary face with constant value f0 , let r be its distance function.
The functions f0 ± ψ(r) are upper and lower barriers. To check the upper barrier at a hypothetical
positive maximum of f − f0 − ψ(r), the common gradient is q1 grad r. The tangential Hessian of
the barrier contributes at most Cq1 , and its axial contribution is at most −ckq12 /(1 + q1 ). By
enlarging k this is strictly less than −C(1 + q1 ), the lower bound for the equation’s source at the
solution height. Ellipticity and the second derivative test contradict the equation. For the lower
barrier both inequalities reverse. At the outer edge of the collar the comparisons follow from
(13.6). Consequently

                          |f (x) − f0 | ≤ ψ dist(x, face)         in its h0 collar.                 (13.7)

                                       f (x) − f (y) − ψ dist(x, y)

has a positive maximum on pairs of distance at most h0 . Neither the diagonal nor the distance-h0
set contains such a maximum. If an endpoint lies on a face, the other endpoint lies in that face’s
collar; (13.7) and monotonicity of ψ exclude this as well. Thus both endpoints are interior. Let
r > 0 be their distance and orient their unique short geodesic from y to x. The first derivative
test says that their gradients are q1 (r) times the corresponding parallel unit tangent vectors.
    Vary both endpoints in each of three parallel transverse directions. The index-form estimate
for the second variation of distance, using the parallel field along the geodesic, is bounded above
by Cr. The second derivative test for the two-point maximum therefore gives

                              tre⊥ Hess f (x) − tre⊥ Hess f (y) ≤ Cq1 r.

                         Hess f (x)(e, e) ≤ ψ ′′ ,        Hess f (y)(e, e) ≥ −ψ ′′ .

inside ΩR . We have proved the two-point modulus; letting y → x gives | df | ≤ ψ ′ (0) = B ′ /k

    The scalar interpolation is now uniformly elliptic. On the given smooth interior and constant-
Dirichlet boundary patches, its equation has the exact form required by Theorem 12.1: the
gradient and source are bounded, and 0 < χ0 ≤ χβ ≤ 1. That theorem first gives a uniform
Hölder bound for df , at some positive exponent. The coefficients and source in (13.2) are smooth
                                                                                   ′
compositions of x, Z, f, df , so the scalar Dirichlet Schauder estimate gives C 2,α bounds for some
α′ > 0. These bounds make f, df Lipschitz. Together with Z ∈ C 1,α , this yields C 0,α coefficients
and source, hence C 2,α bounds. The compositions are now C 1,α and the next Dirichlet estimate
gives a C 3,α bound.
    Subtract a smooth extension of the assigned boundary values and work in the affine space
with model YR = C03,α (ΩR ), where the zero trace is on the entire boundary. The equation defines
a continuously differentiable map into C 1,α (ΩR ). Its derivative in f is
