Derivatives of Aβ and t with respect to df contribute only first derivative terms in φ. Its
coefficients are C 1,α and the ellipticity and positivity are uniform on the bounded range. The
maximum principle removes the homogeneous Dirichlet kernel. Linear Dirichlet solvability and
Schauder estimates therefore make L : YR → C 1,α an isomorphism [18]. The implicit function
theorem proves openness in β. The bounds above prove closedness: subsequences converge in
lower Hölder norms, their limits solve the equation, and the uniform C 3,α estimates retain that
regularity. At β = 0 the equation is ∆g f = ηN f , with ordinary Dirichlet solvability. Thus the
scalar equation is solvable at β = 1.
    For uniqueness, at a positive maximum of the difference of two solutions their gradients agree.
Their matrices and positive prefactors therefore agree, whereas strict height monotonicity makes
the source difference positive, contradicting the Hessian comparison. The reverse comparison
completes uniqueness. Applying the same implicit function theorem with parameters (s, Z) proves
continuous dependence into C 3,α . At stage junctions use one-sided parameter neighborhoods and
the agreement of the prescriptions. The estimates were uniform on bounded trial sets, which
proves the last assertion.

13.2    The frozen return map and degree
For each trial Z, the scalar problem has determined fs [Z]; the second equation remains to be
imposed. We freeze its coefficients and source at the trial pair and solve for a new scalar function.
A fixed point of this return map solves both equations. Arbitrary trial inputs are not assumed to
satisfy the floor.
    For a trial pair (fs [Z], Z), evaluate all expressions in the homotopy at that pair. Write
A = uAχ , let Bs = λuAχ K(w, ·) be its drift, and write Qs and qs for the prescribed interior
source and inner flux, including the common terminal scale. Define Ts Z = Y by the linear




                                                101
problem
                               divg (3A grad Y + Bs ) = Qs in ΩR ,
                                (3A grad Y + Bs ) · ν = qs        on B,                       (13.8)
                                                     Y =0         on SR .
Thus only the occurrence of 3 grad Z in the flux is replaced by 3 grad Y ; the coefficients, source,
and boundary expression are frozen. In qs the value of F is always its trace prescription.
   The matrix A is symmetric positive definite and C 1,α . On bounded trial sets its ellipticity
constants, C 1,α norm, and those of Bs , qs are bounded; Qs is bounded in C 0,α . In particular the
source uses dZ and Hess f , whose regularity is already available. With

                                HR = {Y ∈ H 1 (ΩR ) : Y |SR = 0},

the weak problem has coercive bilinear form 3 ΩR A grad Y · grad φ. Indeed ΩR is connected and
                                                 R

SR is nonempty, so Poincaré’s inequality holds on HR . The prescribed flux is a bounded linear
functional by the trace inequality. Lax–Milgram gives a unique weak solution. At an inner face f
is constant, so Aχ ν = χν, and its flux condition is equivalently

                                                 qs − B s · ν
                                        ∂ν Y =                .
                                                    3uχ

This is a C 1,α ordinary normal datum. The disjoint Dirichlet and normal faces have no corners.
Linear boundary and interior estimates give Y ∈ C 2,α with bounds uniform on bounded trial sets;
the weak energy estimate supplies the lower norm in these estimates. Coefficient dependence
and uniqueness, or the same linear estimates applied to differences, give continuous dependence.
Consequently

             T : [0, 4] × XR −→ XR     is continuous and compact on bounded sets.             (13.9)

Here compactness uses the compact embedding C 2,α ,→ C 1,α on the fixed truncation.
   Every fixed point is smooth. Initially it has Z ∈ C 2,α and f ∈ C 3,α . The trace equation raises
the regularity of f , and then expansion of the divergence equation, with its normal boundary
datum, raises the regularity of Z. Repetition gives all orders. The smooth a priori estimates from
the preceding sections therefore apply to every fixed point.

Proposition 13.2 (Existence and exhaustion). Fix the prepared geometry and 0 < ϵ < 1. For
every sufficiently large N and every allowed R, system (10.20) has a smooth solution on ΩR with
t > −ϵ. For each such fixed N there is a sequence R → ∞ whose solutions converge smoothly on
compact subsets up to B to a solution on Ω with t ≥ −ϵ. This solution satisfies the prescribed
inner boundary conditions, the height interval (11.11), and the weighted trace and flux estimates
of Lemma 11.3.

Proof. Choose N large enough for Proposition 11.1, the collar estimates, and the polynomial
absorption conditions used in Proposition 11.6. Fix an allowed R. By (11.25) and Proposition 12.2,
choose M0 strictly larger than every C 1,α norm of an above-floor fixed point on this truncation.
Define                  (                                                   )
                  Os =    Z ∈ XR : ∥Z∥C 1,α < M0 ,       min t Z, dfs [Z] > −ϵ .            (13.10)
                                                                            
                                                          ΩR

Lemma 13.1 and smooth implicit dependence of t make their union relatively open in [0, 4] × XR .

                                                 102
    Apply Lemma 6.5 to I = [0, 4], X = XR , Ts from (13.8), and U = {(s, Z) : Z ∈ Os }. The
norm cutoff makes U bounded; the scalar solution map makes it relatively open; and (13.9)
supplies continuity and compactness. Every fixed point is smooth. A fixed point on its relative
boundary would satisfy t ≥ −ϵ and meet either the norm cutoff or the floor. The first is excluded
by the choice of M0 , and the second by Proposition 10.7. The section degree is therefore constant.
This checks the varying-domain hypotheses for this boundary problem without imposing the floor
on trial inputs.
    At s = 4, the scalar prescription is trAχ H f = h + D1 (x, w) with zero boundary heights. At
an interior extremum w = 0 and D1 = 0, so the maximum principle gives f = 0 for every trial Z,
and then t = Z. The terminal scale sets both frozen right sides to zero and the drift is absent.
Testing the frozen equation with Y gives Y = 0; thus T4 is the zero map, even though its positive
coefficients may depend on the input. Its sole fixed point, Z = 0, belongs to O4 and has degree
one. The degree at s = 0 is consequently one, proving existence there.
    Now keep N fixed. The preceding choices of sufficiently large N used only the already proved
uniform height and collar results and polynomial losses such as ΠN ηN /ℓ → 0. The arbitrary
fixed-N constants in the scalar construction impose no subsequent condition on N . The bounds
in Proposition 12.2 are uniform in allowed R on each compact set, including its portions on B.
A diagonal Arzelà–Ascoli argument along R → ∞ gives smooth convergence on all these sets.
The equation and inner data pass to the limit, as do t ≥ −ϵ, the height interval, and the collar
estimates. Smooth convergence on the fixed collars passes the weighted trace and flux inequalities
as well. Strictness of the floor in this exhaustion limit is unnecessary below.

    Here are the differentiated estimates, which do not follow from the pointwise barrier alone.
On an annulus of radius r1 , rescale by x = r1 y and set U (y) = r12 Φ(Z(r1 y)). Its supremum and
its Poisson source in the rescaled equation are bounded: the source is r14 times the right side
of (13.16), of size ON (r1−δ1 ). The rescaled background coefficients have uniform smooth bounds.
Interior W 2,p estimates, for a fixed p > 4, give bounded C 1,θ norms of U on a smaller annulus.
The weights have their differentiated power decay, the compositions with Z now have bounded
scaled Hölder norms, and the exponential errors remain bounded with all scaled derivatives. The
right side therefore has a bounded C 0,θ norm. Schauder estimates give a bounded C 2,θ norm of
U . The inverse of Φ has bounded smooth derivatives on the relevant range. Rescaling back gives
Z = O2 (r−2 ), and (13.11) gives the same statement for t. Finally | dZ|2 = O(r−6 ) in (13.14);
since δ1 < 1, this is lower order than r−4−δ1 . Equation (13.11) now gives the asserted bound for
∆g t.

Proposition 13.4 (Energy comparison). For each fixed ϵ > 0 and all sufficiently large N , the
limiting metric has finite ADM energy and

                                                    106
13.5    Completion of the boundary deformation
Proof of Theorem 8.2. The geometric preparation in Proposition 9.6 chooses a closed connected
one-ended exterior Ω inside the original data. It has nonempty compact smooth boundary
and does not change the asymptotic energy. The last assertion of that Proposition compares
every full cut in Ω with the original infimum A∗ . Fix 0 < ϵ < 1. For each sufficiently large
N , Proposition 13.2 gives the desired smooth solution after exhausting the outer radius at that
fixed N . Equation (13.17) gives nonnegative scalar curvature; Equation (13.18) gives strictly
negative inner mean curvature on every face. Equations (13.19) and (13.20) establish the required
asymptotics, integrability and completeness, as well as the lower metric bound. Proposition 13.4
gives finite ADM energy with the stated error. Its polynomial bound uses only the fixed geometric
data and ϵ. Since ℓ = e−ϵN and ℓ2 /ηN = e−ϵN/2 , this error tends to zero. No estimates uniform
over a further sequence of prepared data, and no convergence of the constructed metrics as
N → ∞, are needed.


14     Returning to weakly decaying four-dimensional data
The prepared boundary theorem can be used without imposing its improved end decay on the
original data. The necessary reduction is the independent rest-end replacement and strictification
proved in [34, Proposition 8.8 and Lemma 8.9]. We record the application with its order of limits,
since its area comparison is one-sided.

15     What changes when the tensor reaches infinity
The boundary construction controls two different interfaces of the same deformation. At the inner
boundary it prescribes the normal flux so that the new mean curvature is negative. At infinity it
estimates the total flux so that the new energy is no larger than the old energy, up to an error
tending to zero. Compact support of the second fundamental form simplifies the second task:
sufficiently far out, the trace equation contains only the graph. We now explain precisely how
maximality replaces that simplification. This calculation is also the reason to retain a separate
maximal construction: it carries the original decaying tensor through the deformation instead of
removing it from the end.
    We work in spatial dimension four. Write g for the background metric, K for its second
fundamental form, and assume trg K = 0. Let N ≥ 4 be a fixed deformation parameter, let
0 < ε < 1, and choose a smooth nonincreasing function ϑ equal to one on (−∞, 0] and zero on
[1, ∞). Set

ends of the real line with t. Put

Lemma 15.1 (The maximal end equation). Suppose 1 < q < 2, 0 < δ < q −1, g −δEucl = O2 (r−q )
and K = O1 (r−1−q ) on a four-dimensional end. Assume trg K = 0 and Rg = O(r−4−δ ). For fixed

                    divg V = u [δ0 T + ρ + δ0 ηN |w||∇f | − m0 (t)CN (ρ0 + vρ1 )] .            (15.4)
Here 0 < δ0 < 1, ρ, ρ0 = O(r−4−δ ) with their differentiated bounds, ρ1 = O(r−2γ ) for a fixed
γ > 1, m0 is a smooth bounded function, and T is the quadratic term in the four-dimensional
scalar identity. Assume that the solutions have a common bounded range and that, for every
fixed derivative order, the background coefficients, weights and solutions have uniform fixed-N
classical bounds on end unit patches, including the outer boundary patches. Assume also that Aχ
is uniformly elliptic there. These bounds may depend on N and the derivative order; they are
independent of the outer radius. They are separate assumptions from the displayed O2 /O1 decay
rates. Then, uniformly on those truncations,
                   |∇j f | ≤ Cj (N )e−cj (N )r   (j ≥ 0),     |Z| + |t| ≤ C(N )r−2 .

        3∆g Z + 3[p(Z) + 2 − δ0 (p(Z) + 1)]| dZ|2 = δ20 |K|2 + ρ − m0 (Z)CN ρ0 + EN ,          (15.5)

                                                   109
Proof. The coefficients in (15.3) are uniformly bounded and elliptic at fixed N . For sufficiently
small c > 0, substitution of e−cr into its left side gives an upper bound

                               e−cr C(N )c2 + C(N )c − ηN /e < 0
                                                                


outside one fixed sphere. A multiple of this positive supersolution dominates |f | on that sphere;
the zero outer value is already dominated. Comparison for f and −f gives exponential decay,
uniformly in the truncation radius. The assumed unit-patch estimates, followed by the smooth
linear interior and zero-Dirichlet boundary estimates for (15.3), give the asserted differentiated
decay. These are the smooth-coefficient estimates of [18, Chs. 6–9] and [1]. The implicit formula
for t then shows that t − Z and the graph metric error have the same type of bounds.
    It remains to identify what survives when those graph errors are removed. Set the graph
height and its first two derivatives equal to zero in the coefficients, retaining the exponentially
small difference as an error. Then H f = 0, F = 0, w = 0, D = d = χ = 1, t = Z and u = L0 (Z).
The smooth scalar quadratic form at this zero graph is

                                 T = 12 |K|2 + 3(p(Z) + 1)| dZ|2 .                          (15.6)

One can also verify this directly from its block formula. Choose any unit vector e, write
j = K(e, e), M = K(e, ·)|e⊥ and T = K|e⊥ , and use tr T = −j. The terms involving K become
2 |T | + |M | + 3 j . Since |T | = |T | + j /3, this is exactly |K| /2. The terms linear in dZ

contain w and vanish, leaving the second term in (15.6). In particular setting the graph to zero

                         −∆g b0 = α(2 + α)r−4−α + O(r−4−q ) ≥ cr−4−α .

A fixed multiple of b0 dominates both the absolute source and |Φ(Z)| on the fixed inner sphere.
Since Φ(Z) = 0 at the outer sphere, comparison for both signs gives |Φ(Z)| ≤ C(N )r−2 , and
hence |Z| + |t| ≤ C(N )r−2 . These estimates hold before exhaustion, so the local limit has the
asserted normalization at infinity.
    Finally rescale annuli R < r < 2R to unit size. The metric has uniformly controlled
scaled C 1,β coefficients for each fixed 0 < β < 1. Interior W 2,p1 estimates with p1 > 4 first
give | dZ| = ON (r−3 ). The first derivative bound on K, the differentiated weights and the
exponential error then give a controlled scaled Hölder norm for the source. Schauder estimates
yield ∇2 Z = ON (r−4 ). The same follows for t from the graph error.

                                                 110
    The term |K|2 /2 has two consequences for the rest of the construction. First, the end proof
must use the original decay of K; it cannot invoke a result whose hypothesis is K = 0 outside a
compact set. Second, the continuation must control the corresponding noncompact drift before
these fixed-parameter estimates are available. The appropriate tail weights follow from a short
calculation. Choose

                      1 < γ < min{2, q − δ/2},         ρ0 ≍ r−4−δ ,   ρ1 ≍ r−2γ .

This interval is nonempty because δ < q − 1. For a = |w| one has

                         ar−2−q ≤ 21 a2 r−2γ + 12 r−4−2q+2γ ≤ C(vρ1 + ρ0 ).                    (15.7)

The first inequality is Young’s inequality; the second uses 2γ < 2q − δ. Also |K|2 ≤ Cρ0 . Thus
the drift error controlled by ΠN (|K|2 + a|∇K|) fits the same kind of floor penalty as in the
boundary construction, with exponents adapted to the original end. Since 4γ > 4, the square
of ρ1 is integrable. That last fact is exactly what permits its contribution to the flux loss to be
absorbed with an error tending to zero.
    The local comparison is now explicit. Signed boundary faces and their inner flux formula
are shared. The maximal route supplies its own strict approximation and an all-black threshold
boundary; it proves the noncompact drift estimate and its complete boundary continuation
with the weights above. Once these yield the fixed-N estimates, Lemma 15.1 gives the correct
end normalization. In applying that lemma, unit-scale convolution of the strict end coefficients
supplies their uniform bounds at every fixed derivative order; a subsequent trace-free projection
preserves maximality. This is an actual preparation step, not a consequence of smoothness and
O2 /O1 decay alone. The displayed decay rates are the ones retained from the original data and
used in the final annular estimates. The final comparison still uses the actual metric gb, its minimal
enclosure, and its ADM flux. Maximality preserves a method with the original tensor tail; it does
not replace those existence, boundary, or enclosure arguments.


16     The maximal comparison and its scope
Maximality allows the boundary deformation to retain a decaying second fundamental form all
the way to infinity. The reason is the homogeneous trace equation in Section 15: when trg K = 0,
the tensor contributes a drift proportional to the graph gradient. Thus the graph can decay
exponentially even though K does not vanish outside a compact set. The conformal equation still
sees |K|2 /2. We now construct the solutions for which that end calculation applies.
    There are two geometric tasks before solving the equations. We must create strict dominant-
energy slack without destroying maximality or changing the limiting energy and enclosing area.
We must then select an inner boundary whose outward collars control the large graph slopes.
The remaining analysis has the same input and output as the boundary method: nonnegative
scalar curvature, negative inner mean curvature, a lower metric comparison on every cut, and an
upper error in energy. The noncompact drift requires the tail weights and continuation estimates
proved in this part.
    The following theorem records the maximal-vacuum application considered here. Corollary 14.1
gives a broader numerical comparison under its one-ended weak-decay and dominant-energy
hypotheses. That corollary first replaces the distant end and tensor. The construction below
retains the decaying tensor tail through the deformation.


                                                 111
    Throughout, four refers to the spatial dimension. The associated spacetime dimension is
five, and a horizon has dimension three. Write ω3 = |S 3 | = 2π 2 . The notation Oj (r−q ) includes
coordinate derivatives through order j, with the corresponding additional decay.

    An enclosing hypersurface separates S from the selected end and bounds with S a compact
region. The original S is itself allowed when taking the enclosing-area infimum. All hypersurface
areas in this part are three-dimensional volumes. In particular, outer area-minimization in
Theorem 16.1 is not restricted by the torus action. The torus action and outermostness specify the
stated application class; the construction below does not use them. Ball-exterior topology supplies
the compact filling, and vacuum supplies the initial strictification. Outer area-minimization
identifies the final enclosing-area infimum with A. Neither HS = 0 nor trS K = 0 is imposed.
The conditions P = 0 and E > 0 identify the energy in (16.3) with the ADM rest mass.

16.1    The geometric output and the route to it
It is useful to state what the construction will produce for strict data before introducing its
equations. This is the point at which the analytic problem rejoins the mass–area comparison.

Proposition 16.2 (Maximal deformation output). Let (M, g, K) be a smooth connected orientable
four-dimensional ball exterior, complete including its compact boundary S, with one Euclidean
coordinate end. Let r ≥ 1 be a fixed smooth positive extension of its Euclidean radius. Suppose
1 < q < 2, 0 < δ < q − 1,

trg K = 0,     g−δEucl = O2 (r−q ),    K = O1 (r−1−q ),     Rg = O(r−4−δ ),      µ−|J| ≥ c0 r−4−δ > 0,

and suppose HS + trS K < 0. Assume finite ADM energy and uniform bounds of every fixed
derivative order on end patches of unit size. Write A∗ = Ag (S) for the infimum over all smooth
enclosing cuts, including disconnected cuts.
    There is a smooth cut B enclosing S, with connected one-ended exterior Ω, such that for each
0 < ϵ < 1 and all sufficiently large integers N there is a smooth metric gbN on Ω satisfying
                                                                          ΠN −ϵN
         gbN ≥ e−2ϵ g,       RgbN ≥ 0,      b B < 0,
                                            H               EgbN ≤ Eg +       (e + e−ϵN/2 ).
                                                                          3ω3
Here ΠN has polynomial growth at fixed strict data and ϵ. The new metric is complete including
B, has gbN − δEucl = O2 (r−q ) and RgbN = O(r−4−δ ), and every smooth cut enclosing B has gbN -area
at least e−3ϵ A∗ .

    The proof occupies Sections 17– 22. First we obtain B and its outward collars. Next we prove
a lower bound for t, followed by height, boundary-flux and range estimates for every possible
continuation solution. Local regularity and an unrestricted scalar solve then define the compact
continuation map. Only after existence and fixed-N exhaustion do we use the end calculation of
Lemma 15.1. The final flux estimate proves the displayed energy bound. This order separates the
polynomial estimates needed for the mass limit from arbitrary fixed-N regularity constants.

                                                   113
    The trace trg K vanishes throughout this construction. Its positive regularizing parameter is
ηN = ℓ3/2 ; this parameter is distinct from the trace. The scalar weight is denoted by L0 = e2t l(t).
First fix the strict geometry and its decay exponents, then ϵ > 0 and a sufficiently large N .
Exhaust the outer radius at fixed N , send N → ∞ using polynomial constants only, then send
ϵ ↓ 0 and remove the strict approximation. Arbitrary fixed-N regularity constants do not enter
the final mass limit.


17     Strict data and the geometric inner boundary
This section produces the strict data and the inner boundary used in the deformation. The
approximation is controlled in ADM energy and in the infimum over all enclosing areas. The
geometric construction also supplies a comparison principle for closed sets described by smooth
exterior supports; this will be used before any derivative estimates for the deformation are
available.

17.1    Strict conformal approximation
The spacetime Poisson construction of Jaracz [25] is the model for this reduction. Here the
conformal exponents are four-dimensional, the inner Neumann datum is nonzero, and we prove
preservation of maximality and comparison for the full enclosing-area infimum.
    Decrease the decay exponent, if necessary, so that 1 < q < 2, and fix 0 < δ < q − 1. Extend
the end radius to a positive smooth function r ≥ 1 on the exterior. We use the enclosing-area
infimum Ag (S) defined above.

Proposition 17.1 (Reduction to strict data). The data of Theorem 16.1 admit smooth approxi-
mations (gj , Kj ) on the same exterior such that

                         trgj Kj = 0, µj − |Jj |gj ≥ cj r−4−δ ,
                                                                                                (17.1)
                        ΘKj (S) < 0,           Rgj = O(r−4−δ ),        cj > 0.

They have the prescribed O2 (r−q ) metric decay and O1 (r−1−q ) tensor decay, and all their coordinate
derivatives are bounded uniformly over end patches of unit size, with constants allowed to depend
on j and the derivative order. Moreover,

                                Egj −→ Eg ,       Agj (S) −→ Ag (S).                            (17.2)

Consequently, it suffices to prove Eg ≥ 12 (Ag (S)/ω3 )2/3 for such strict maximal data on this
exterior. Neither vacuum nor marginality is required of the intermediate approximations.

Proof. Choose a positive smooth function b ≥ |K|g which equals a constant multiple of r−1−q
sufficiently far out. We first solve
                  q
       −∆g ϕ = b | dϕ|2g + r−6 + r−4−δ ,       ∂ν ϕ = −1 on S,       ϕ −→ 0 at infinity,        (17.3)

where ν points into the exterior. In particular, the normal derivative in this equation is the
negative of the domain’s outward normal derivative at its inner boundary.




                                                 114
    Here are the existence and decay details. On the compact truncation MR , impose ϕ = 0 on
the outer coordinate sphere and replace b on the right by λb, 0 ≤ λ ≤ 1. The linearization, with
homogeneous boundary data, is
                                                   ⟨ dϕ, dv⟩g
                                 v 7−→ −∆g v − λb q               .
                                                    | dϕ|2g + r−6

Its drift is bounded by b. Its kernel is zero by the strong maximum principle and the boundary
point lemma, since the outer Dirichlet face is nonempty and the inner face has homogeneous
normal derivative. The mixed operator has Fredholm index zero, by deformation to the mixed
Laplacian, and hence is invertible. The two boundary components are disjoint, smooth, and
compact, so ordinary scalar elliptic estimates apply without a junction between boundary types;
see [18, Chapters 3, 6, and 9] and [1, Theorems 7.3 and 15.2–15.3].
    For fixed R, the global mixed W 2,p estimate, p > 4, and interpolation give
                                                                      ′
             ∥ϕ∥W 2,p (MR ) ≤ CR,p 1 + ∥ dϕ∥Lp (MR ) + ∥ϕ∥Lp (MR ) ≤ CR,p (1 + ∥ϕ∥∞ ).    (17.4)
                                                                    


These constants are uniform in λ. If the suprema were unbounded, divide by their sup norms and
take a subsequence. Compact embedding of W 2,p in C 1,α , with a smaller α > 0, gives a nonzero
limit v satisfying

               −∆g v = λ∗ b| dv|g ,    ∂ν v = 0 on S,      v = 0 on the outer face.

This is a homogeneous linear equation with a bounded measurable drift: set the drift to
λ∗ b grad v/| dv| where dv ̸= 0, and to zero otherwise. The maximum principle and bound-
ary point lemma again exclude a nonzero solution. Thus (17.4) is bounded, and Schauder
estimates and bootstrapping give closedness in the continuity argument. The λ = 0 mixed Poisson
problem is solvable, so the implicit function theorem and compactness give a smooth solution for
λ = 1. It is nonnegative: a negative minimum is excluded in the interior by −∆ϕ > 0, on the
outer face by its zero value, and on the inner face by ∂ν ϕ = −1.
    We next bound these solutions independently of R. On the end consider

                                      T (r) = r−2 (1 − Ar−δ ).

Choose a fixed A > 0, then a large fixed r0 , so that T > 0 and

                            (−∆g − b| d · |)T ≥ c r−4−δ          (r ≥ r0 ).               (17.5)

Indeed, −∆R T = Aδ(2 + δ)r−4−δ , while both the metric error and b| dT | are O(r−4−q ). Also

                            −∆g ϕ − b| dϕ| ≤ br−3 + r−4−δ ≤ Cr−4−δ .

Comparison on {r0 ≤ r ≤ R}, including the zero outer value, therefore yields

                      0 ≤ ϕR (x) ≤ C(1 + mR )r(x)−2 ,       mR = max ϕR .                 (17.6)
                                                                        {r≤r0 }

If mR were unbounded along expanding truncations, divide by mR . Local versions of (17.4),
including the inner boundary estimates, give a nonnegative limit attaining value one in the fixed
core. It solves −∆v = b| dv|, has homogeneous inner normal derivative, and is O(r−2 ) by (17.6).
Its positive maximum is therefore attained at a finite point. The strong maximum principle or

                                                115
the boundary point lemma excludes it. We have a uniform core bound, and diagonal compactness
gives a smooth solution of (17.3).
    Rescale annuli by their radii. The bound ϕ = O(r−2 ), the scaled metric bounds furnished
by g − δ = O2 (r−q ), and the linear gradient growth in (17.3) first give scaled W 2,p estimates by
interpolation. They give Hölder control of the gradient; the right side then has the scaled Hölder
control needed for Schauder estimates. Hence

                                           ϕ = O2 (r−2 ).                                     (17.7)

The source in (17.3) is O(r−4−q ) + O(r−4−δ ), and is integrable. The divergence theorem shows
that                                           Z
                                    Iϕ = lim       ∂νg ϕ dAg
                                           R→∞ SR

exists. Replacing this by its Euclidean-coordinate flux changes it by O(R−q ), which tends to zero.
    For small s > 0 set gs = e2sϕ g and Ks = esϕ K. The trace remains zero. The connection
change is
                             Γ(gs )kij − Γ(g)kij = s(δik ϕj + δjk ϕi − gij ϕk ).

                                  e2sϕ µs = µ − 3s∆g ϕ − 3s2 | dϕ|2 ,

The ratio | dϕ|2 /r−4−δ is bounded, by (17.7) and δ < 1. Thus small s > 0 gives a positive gap of
the asserted order. The boundary expansion is −3se−sϕ < 0. Moreover,

                       Rgs = e−2sϕ |K|2 − 6s∆ϕ − 6s2 | dϕ|2 = O(r−4−δ ),
                                                               


because 2 + 2q > 4 + δ. The original metric and tensor decay is preserved, since q < 2.
   Only −6s∂i ϕ survives from the conformal change in the ADM integrand. Products with a
metric error or with ϕ have vanishing integrated flux by (17.7). Thus
                                                    s

Since ϕ is bounded, the comparison e−2s∥ϕ∥∞ g ≤ gs ≤ e2s∥ϕ∥∞ g holds on the entire exterior. Its
three-dimensional area comparison holds for every enclosing cut, and therefore for their infima.
In particular, Ags (S) → Ag (S), with no compactness assumption on minimizing cuts.




                                                116
Smoothing the end. The conformal step has produced strict dominance and convergence of
energy and enclosing-area infima. The differentiated end estimates will also need bounds of every
fixed derivative order on patches of unit size. The original O2 /O1 hypotheses do not supply those
bounds; we obtain them by smoothing each fixed strict pair sufficiently far out.
    Fix one of these strict pairs and temporarily denote it by (g, K). Let h = g − δ in the end
chart. Convolve h and K with a nonnegative smooth kernel of integral one supported in a unit
ball. Interpolate to the original data with a cutoff which is zero for r ≤ Rsm , one for r ≥ 2Rsm ,
and has derivatives of order i bounded by Ci Rsm −i . Call the resulting metric and tensor g  K, and
                                                                                           e, f
project the tensor to its ge-trace-free part:

                                       K sm = K
                                              f − 1 (tr K)
                                                  4    g
                                                       e
                                                         f ge.

For sufficiently large Rsm , the metric remains positive definite. The available original derivatives
give

       h ∗ κ − h = O(r−q−1 ),        D(h ∗ κ − h) = O(r−q−2 ),            K ∗ κ − K = O(r−q−2 ).

Here D denotes coordinate differentiation only in this paragraph. For the trace projection, use
the original trace-free identity in the form

                                trδ K = (δ ij − g ij )Kij = O1 (r−1−2q ).

Convolution and interpolation preserve this first-derivative bound. Moreover

                           trge K
                                f = trδ K                 fij = O1 (r −1−2q ).
                                        f + (geij − δ ij )K

Differentiating the last product uses only the available first derivatives of the metric and tensor.
Thus the projection changes the tensor by O1 (r−1−2q ), without requiring a second derivative of
the original K.
    For trace-free data with these decay bounds, the constraint pair has the flat linear principal
expressions                                                     
                        (µ, J) = 21 (∂i ∂j hij − ∆R hii ), ∂j Kij + O(r−2−2q ).             (17.11)

The scalar error consists of hD2 h, (Dh)2 , and |K|2 ; the momentum error consists of hDK and
(Dh)K. The displayed linear operators commute with convolution. Cutoff derivatives multiply
the preceding convolution differences and contribute O(r−q−3 ). The trace projection changes the
momentum by O(r−2−2q ). Hence the new constraint pair is the interpolated average of the old
pair, up to
                                 O(r−2−2q + r−q−3 ) = o(r−4−δ ).                         (17.12)
Replacing the old momentum norm by its Euclidean norm, and the averaged Euclidean momentum
norm by the new metric norm, each costs O(r−2−2q ): both metrics differ from the Euclidean
metric by O(r−q ), while both momenta are O(r−2−q ). The triangle inequality and positivity of
the kernel therefore preserve a fixed positive fraction of the strict dominance gap once Rsm is
sufficiently large. This radius is chosen separately for each fixed strict pair, so the little-o errors
are smaller than its particular positive gap coefficient. The two needed exponent inequalities are

                                 2q − 2 − δ > 0,         q − 1 − δ > 0.

The same calculation shows that the scalar curvature is still O(r−4−δ ).

                                                   117
    Beyond 2Rsm , derivatives of the convolution kernel bound every higher derivative on unit
patches, using the original zeroth, first, and second derivative bounds. The transition region
is smooth and compact. At infinity the metric’s first derivative changes by O(r−q−2 ), whose
integrated ADM contribution is O(r1−q ) → 0. Thus this smoothing leaves the energy exactly
unchanged. As Rsm → ∞, its uniform metric ratio tends to one, so the enclosing infimum
converges as well. Choosing first s ↓ 0, and then a sufficiently large smoothing radius for each s,
proves (17.1) and (17.2).

   For the remainder of the proof fix strict data from Proposition 17.1, and write A∗ = Ag (S).
Constants may depend on this fixed approximation. Write c0 > 0 for a fixed constant such that
µ − |J| ≥ c0 r−4−δ for these data.

17.2    Threshold regions and outward collars
We use the following part of the total trapped-region theorem: on a complete smooth asymp-
totically flat initial data set of spatial dimension 2 through 7, the nonempty union of bounded
regions with smooth boundary and outward expansion at most zero has a smooth embedded
outermost stable MOTS as its outer frontier. We take its closed, filled representative. Filling a
bounded complementary component deletes boundary components and preserves admissibility.
This is the existence and regularity conclusion of [6, Section 4.3, Theorem 4.6]; it requires no
energy condition. We do not use the additional topology conclusions of that theorem.

Lemma 17.2 (Threshold selection and collars). There is a number c with maxS ΘK < c < 0,
and a closed filled compact region Dc , containing a smooth compact filling of S and an exterior
collar of S, with the following properties. Its boundary B is a smooth embedded stable threshold
surface, ΘK (B) = c. It belongs to an increasing family Db , maxS ΘK < b < 0, of total regions at
threshold b, all lying in a fixed compact set, and

                           Db −→ Dc     in Hausdorff distance as b ↓ c.                    (17.13)

The exterior Ω of Dc is connected and one-ended. Every smooth cut enclosing B has g-area at
least A∗ . Each component of B has a smooth outward collar with leaf coordinate s ≥ 0, s = 0 at
B, | ds| > 0, whose leaves satisfy

                                                                 grad s
                           ΘK ({s = constant}) ≥ c,       νs =          .                  (17.14)
                                                                  | ds|

Proof. The given diffeomorphism of the exterior permits a smooth compact filling behind S.
Extend g, K smoothly over it; the extended metric can be chosen positive definite by interpolation
with any interior metric away from a collar. This extension is used only in the present geometric
construction.
     Fix a large radius R0 beyond which every coordinate sphere has ΘK = 3/r + O(r−1−q ) > 0.
Outside a still larger fixed radius, smoothly cut the metric to the Euclidean metric and the
unshifted tensor to zero. Dilated cutoffs preserve the bounds g − δ = O2 (r−q ), K = O1 (r−1−q ),
and consequently the positive expansion of all spheres in the cutoff annuli. Denote these complete
auxiliary data by (g aux , K aux ). Choose a fixed nonnegative smooth cutoff ξ, equal to one on the
filling and throughout a large neighborhood of {r ≤ R0 }, and zero far out. For maxS ΘK < b < 0,
use the tensor
                                        Kbaux = K aux − 3b ξg aux .

                                               118
Where ξ = 1 and the data are uncut, the exact identity is

                                        ΘKbaux (Σ) = ΘK (Σ) − b.                              (17.15)

In the cutoff region the added tangential trace is −bξ ≥ 0, so all the distant spheres still have
positive expansion, uniformly in b.
    A smooth weakly trapped bounded region cannot meet those distant spheres: at a point where
its boundary reaches its greatest radius, its tangent plane and outward normal agree with those
of the coordinate sphere, and its mean curvature is at least the sphere’s mean curvature. The
tensor traces there agree. This contradicts nonpositive expansion. The same argument applies to
the outer frontier furnished by the total-region theorem. Thus all total regions are contained in
the uncut part of the construction.
    For every indicated b, strictness at S gives a short outward collar whose leaves have expansion
less than b. The filling together with this collar is a nonempty trapped region for Kbaux . The
theorem therefore gives a total closed region Db with smooth stable frontier. Its frontier lies
strictly outside S, in the original data, and has ΘK = b by (17.15). We used one filling and one
set of cutoffs for the entire interval of b. Since ξ ≥ 0, increasing b decreases the shifted expansion
on every candidate hypersurface. It follows that

                                    b1 < b2     =⇒        Db1 ⊂ Db2 .                         (17.16)

    Choose a compact enlargement Q containing all these regions. Lemma 3.2 applies to the increas-
ing family of closed subsets Eb = Db of this fixed compact metric space, with maxS ΘK < b < 0.
The preceding construction proves both the common compact containment and monotonicity;
every member is nonempty. Choose c outside the lemma’s countable exceptional set. Its conclusion
is exactly (17.13). Filling bounded complementary pockets ensures that the remaining exterior is
the connected component containing the sole end. Any cut enclosing its entire boundary B also
encloses S, proving the area assertion.
    It remains to construct the collars. Apply Lemma 3.3 with ambient dimension four, Q = K,
threshold c, and each connected component of B. The total-region theorem gives a compact
smooth stable frontier; its principal eigenvalue is nonnegative [5, Proposition 5.1]. On an open
neighborhood of this frontier the auxiliary cutoff ξ equals one and the data are uncut, so the
relevant shifted tensor is exactly K − (c/3)g. Its expansion differs from ΘK by the constant c,
with zero derivative under graph variations. Replacing one component by a sufficiently short
outward graph of expansion at most c would strictly enlarge the total region while preserving
every other component. Thus the local maximality hypothesis of the collar lemma holds in this
application.
    For clarity, its zero-mode construction uses the expansion E(v) of the outward graph on this
one component and L = DE(0). The common augmented inverse specializes to
                                   Z          
               (v, a) 7−→ Lv − a,         v dAg ,         C 2,α (B) × R −→ C 0,α (B) × R.     (17.17)
                                      B

Here the integrals are over the chosen component. The positive principal and adjoint eigenfunctions,
their simple kernels, and the positive pairing used to invert this map are those in [5, Section 4,
Lemma 4.1], as in the common proof. That proof gives strictly larger expansion on every positive
outward leaf, including the zero-eigenvalue case; stability alone would not give this conclusion.
Finitely many components permit disjoint shortened collars, proving (17.14).


                                                    119
17.3    Exterior supports of nonsmooth sets
The threshold regions control smooth surfaces. The graph-height limit will give only a closed
sublevel set, so we need the same comparison for smooth surfaces that touch such a set from
outside. The next argument transports the supporting tangent plane exactly; this prevents an
uncontrolled curvature of the support from entering the error.
   For a closed set A, a smooth exterior support at x ∈ ∂A is a regular level {ψ = 0} in
a neighborhood of x, where ψ(x) = 0, dψ(x) ̸= 0, and A ⊂ {ψ ≤ 0} locally. Its normal is
νψ = grad ψ/| dψ|, and its expansion is

                              trg Hess ψ − Hess ψ(νψ , νψ )
                   ΘK [ψ] =                                 + trg K − K(νψ , νψ ).           (17.18)
                                          | dψ|
Only supports that exist are tested; no regularity is assumed of A itself.

Lemma 17.3 (Enclosure from exterior supports). Let A be a compact closed set containing
Dc . Suppose that every smooth exterior support to its frontier has expansion at most k ′ , where
c ≤ k ′ < 0. Then
                              A ⊂ Dc′      for every k ′ < c′ < 0.
All expansions at the frontier of A are computed in the original strict exterior data.

Proof. First, every set under consideration lies inside one fixed large sphere. Otherwise the
coordinate sphere at its maximum radius is an exterior support with positive expansion, contrary
to k ′ < 0. Choose a compact enlargement containing this sphere and a small metric neighborhood
of it. All constants below depend only on the smooth geometry and tensor on this enlargement.
Distances may be computed in the complete smooth filling, since the contact points and sufficiently
short segments used below lie in the original exterior: A contains the filling and a collar of S.
    For z > 0, put Az = {y : dist(y, A) ≤ z}. Apply Lemma 3.5 in ambient dimension four, with
its set D = A, tensor Q = K, and threshold k ′ . The compact neighborhood and short segments
required by that local lemma are precisely those just fixed. The definition (17.18) is its normalized
expansion. It follows that every exterior support to Az has expansion at most

                                              k ′ + Cz,                                      (17.19)

where C is independent of that support’s curvature.
    The exact geometric feature of this application is worth recording. For a nearest pair x ∈ A,
x′ ∈ ∂Az , let P : Tx M → Tx′ M be parallel transport along the short minimizing segment. The
Jacobi construction in the common proof gives

                         Fz (x) = x′ ,     dFz |x = P,      |∇ dFz |x ≤ Cz.                  (17.20)

If ψ defines the support at x′ , then ψ ◦ Fz supports A at x, and

                        Hess(ψ ◦ Fz )x (X, Y ) = Hess ψx′ (P X, P Y )
                                                                                             (17.21)
                                                  + dψx′ (∇ dFz )x (X, Y ) .
                                                                           


The exact tangent isometry preserves the normalized Hessian trace; only the O(z) second-map
derivative and the C 1 change of K enter the expansion error. Thus no term multiplying Hess ψ
has been discarded. This local transport step uses compact ambient bounds, while the following
enclosure argument still uses the auxiliary total regions specific to the maximal end.

17.4     Fixed collar data
Set b∗ = c. On the disjoint collars supplied by Lemma 17.2, choose a nonpositive smooth
compactly supported function C, equal to −κs near each component of B, with κ > 0 sufficiently
small. Indeed, multiply −κs by nonnegative collar cutoffs. The strict gap has a positive minimum
on their compact support, so κ can be chosen small enough that, for a fixed smooth positive
function ρ equal to a positive multiple of r−4−δ on the end,

                                         | dC| + ρ ≤ µ − |J|.                                 (17.23)

For instance first choose ρ ≤ (c0 /4)r−4−δ , then bound | dC| by half the remaining strict gap on
its support. These data, the collars, the threshold b∗ , and the region Ω are fixed before choosing
any of the deformation parameters below.


18      The scalar-curvature deformation
We now fix the strict data and the exterior Ω constructed in Section 17. Thus B = ∂Ω has
expansion HB + trB K = b∗ < 0, the tensor is maximal, and the fixed functions C ≤ 0 and ρ > 0

satisfy
                                            |dC| + ρ ≤ µ − |J|.                                           (18.1)
The normal ν at B points into Ω. The compactly supported function C vanishes on B and equals
−κs in its fixed collars. Throughout this section the base dimension is four and a transverse block
has dimension three.

18.1      Variables and the exact scalar identity
The pointwise identities used here are those of Section 10. We retain their variables and four-
dimensional density conventions below. Their proofs are local and impose no support condition
on K; maximality will enter through trg K = 0 in the equations and the estimates for the tensor
tail.
    Fix 0 < ϵ < 1. The integer N ≥ 4 is an auxiliary parameter. Fix once and for all a smooth

constants at fixed N will instead be denoted by C(N ).

Proof. This is Proposition 10.1, with its axis e = ef and the same graph variables and density

All outer values remain f = Z = 0. Let νs = grad s/|ds| in the fixed collars. Choose smooth

derivatives for |w| ≤ 1, as follows:

  • D0 ≤ 0, D0 = 0 when w · νs ≥ 0, and D0 (x, −ν) < −2 supB |H| on B;

  • D1 = A1 ζB (x)w · νs , where ζB = 1 near B is a fixed collar cutoff and A1 > 1 + supB |H|.

Such D0 is obtained by a fixed smooth function of w · νs , zero on the nonnegative half-line and
sufficiently negative at −1, times a collar cutoff. In particular D0 (x, 0) = D1 (x, 0) = 0. The
stages, with their endpoints included, are:

  1. Decrease λ from one to zero, keeping F = h + C and h|B = b∗ .

  2. Keep λ = 0 and increase α from zero to one, with F = h + C + αD0 and h|B = b∗ .

  3. Keep λ = 0 and increase ζ from zero to one, with

               F = h + (1 − ζ)(C + D0 ) + ζ trAχ K + D1 ,         h|B = (1 − ζ)b∗ .      (18.27)
                                                           


  4. Keep the terminal trace problem

                                 trAχ H f = h + D1 ,       f |B∪SR = 0,                  (18.28)

     and multiply both Ξ and the right side of (18.26) by a common factor η, decreasing from
     one to zero.

The prescriptions agree at every junction. In every stage the derivative of F with respect to h
is one. The form T is always computed from the actual trace equation and the actual tensor
K + H f , including when λ = 0.


                                              125
    Two features of this choice will be used in the estimates. First, in stage three the limiting
values at w = ν, −ν, with χ = 0 and the assigned face height, obey

                            F (x, ν) ≥ PB + H,      F (x, −ν) ≤ PB − H.                        (18.29)

At ζ = 0 the first equality follows from b∗ = PB + H and D0 (x, ν) = C|B = 0; the second follows
from the choice of D0 . At ζ = 1 the two values are PB ± A1 . Convex combination proves (18.29)
for intermediate ζ. Second, the terminal trace equation forces f = 0 for every C 1,α trial Z and
its classical trace solution: at a positive interior maximum the left side is nonpositive and the
right side is ηN f > 0, while at a negative interior minimum the signs reverse. Boundary values
are zero. Thus in stage four

          f = 0,   t = Z,    d = χ = 1,   u = L0 (t),    T = 12 |K|2 + 3(p + 1)|dt|2 .         (18.30)

    If h − ψ had a positive maximum, it would occur in the interior of this annulus. The gradients
there agree and the Hessian of h is no larger than the Hessian of ψ. The coefficients use the

18.3    Exclusion of the lower boundary of the admissible range
The next argument concerns any smooth homotopy solution whose minimum value of t is −ϵ.
It supplies the strict exclusion needed for continuation before any global gradient or Hessian
estimate is available.

Proof. Differentiating w = l grad f / D covariantly gives

                ∇i w = Ad (Hi·f ) + pd w ∂i t,       d log u = (p + pv + 2)dt + H f (w, ·).   (18.43)

In the first formula, d is the scalar D−1 . Relative to the axis, the Ad factor multiplies the axial

axial entry occurs as d(j − Kee ) and is also controlled because d ≤ d. Furthermore d|dt| ≤ |dt|Ad .

Apply (18.40) and then Young’s inequality to ΠN |K| T . Squaring a polynomial remains

All stage parameters lie in [0, 1], so the estimates are uniform in them. This proves the last
assertion.

Proposition 18.7 (Floor exclusion). There is a choice 0 < δ0 < 1/4 independent of N and a
positive polynomial CN such that, for every sufficiently large allowed R, no smooth solution of
any of the four homotopy stages has
                                         min t = −ϵ.
                                             ΩR

These choices use the fixed data and the preceding pointwise and height estimates only. In
particular they do not use existence, an upper bound for Z, or a uniform Hessian estimate.

Proof. We first record all end losses that will occur. There is a fixed constant C∗ with

                      1K + |K|2 + vF 2 + v| Ricg | + a|∇K| ≤ C∗ (ρ0 + vρ1 ).                  (18.44)

On a fixed compact set this follows from positivity of ρ0 and Lemma 18.4. Outside that set,
|F | ≤ |h|+|K| in all three trace stages, and the same lemma controls h2 by a fixed multiple of r−2γ .
The metric and tensor decay give | Ricg | = O(r−2−q ), |K|2 = O(r−2−2q ) and |∇K| = O(r−2−q ).
Since 2γ < 2 + q and 2 + 2q > 4 + δ, the curvature and squared tensor terms have the stated
bounds. For the derivative term the precise estimate is

                         ar−2−q ≤ 12 a2 r−2γ + 21 r−4−2q+2γ ≤ 12 vρ1 + 12 ρ0 ,

where 2(q − γ) > δ was used. This proves (18.44). Also ρ ≤ Cρ ρ0 for a fixed Cρ , by its prescribed
tail and compact positivity of ρ0 .
    Suppose first that the minimum occurs in the interior in one of the first three stages. Then
dt = 0 and Hess t ≥ 0 there. Proposition 18.1 and (18.35) imply, using tr K = 0,

               u−1 div V ≥ µ − 12 Rg + J(w) + v Ricg (ef , ef ) + T − S(H f ) + w(F ).

Here µ − Rg /2 = −|K|2 /2 and |J(w)| ≤ C∗ a|∇K|. Use (18.37), (18.42), and (18.44); then pass
from V to Vλ = V − (1 − λ)uAχ K(w, ·) by (18.41). Choose the two fixed Young errors smaller
than c2 /4. This yields an absolute c3 > 0 and a polynomial bound ΠfN such that at the proposed
minimum
                            u−1 div Vλ ≥ c3 T + ηN aσ − ΠfN (ρ0 + vρ1 ).                 (18.45)
In particular c3 is independent of N .
    Fix δ0 < min(c3 /2, 1/4). At the floor m0 = 1, and the prescribed right side in the first three
stages is δ0 T + ρ + δ0 ηN aσ − CN (ρ0 + vρ1 ). The lower bound in (18.45) exceeds it by at least

                     (c3 − δ0 )T + (1 − δ0 )ηN aσ + (CN − ΠfN )(ρ0 + vρ1 ) − ρ.

Choose CN ≥ ΠfN + Cρ + 1. The last display is strictly positive because ρ0 > 0, contradicting the
equation. This choice is polynomial and independent of R and of the solution.
   In stage four use (18.30). At an interior floor minimum the left side is u−1 div(3u grad t) =
3∆t ≥ 0, while the right side is

                                     η δ0 |K|2 /2 + ρ − CN ρ0 .
                                                               



                                                  129
Increasing CN by a fixed constant makes the bracket strictly negative everywhere, since |K|2 ≤
C∗ ρ0 . Thus an interior floor minimum is impossible whenever η > 0.
    An inner boundary minimum in the first three stages is also impossible. At t = −ϵ we
have j0 = 0. Since Vλ = V − (1 − λ)uAχ K(w, ·), the drift correction in (18.26) cancels, leaving
Vν /u = B. Lemma 18.3 therefore gives

                                       3∂ν t = −H − NB < 0.

This contradicts the nonnegative derivative into ΩR required at a boundary minimum. In stage
four, where f = 0, the corresponding formula is 3∂ν t = η(−H − NB ) < 0 for η > 0. The outer
boundary is excluded by Lemma 18.4.
   It remains to consider η = 0. The last-stage equation and boundary conditions then reduce to

                     div(3L0 (Z) grad Z) = 0,       ∂ν Z|B = 0,      Z|SR = 0.

Multiplying by Z and integrating gives ΩR 3L0 (Z)| grad Z|2 dVg = 0. Thus Z = t = 0, which is
                                          R

strictly above the floor. This treats every stage and every possible location of a floor minimum.

    The order of the choices is now fixed: strict background data and collars, ϵ and the cutoffs, the
absolute small δ0 , the polynomial CN , and finally sufficiently large N and allowed R. The estimates
in the next Section concern arbitrary smooth admissible solutions with t ≥ −ϵ. Proposition 18.7
separately ensures that a homotopy solution cannot meet the boundary of this range.


19     Global a priori bounds
Throughout this section the strict background data, collars, and ϵ > 0 are fixed. We consider
arbitrary smooth solutions of the homotopy on ΩR satisfying t ≥ −ϵ. Existence is not assumed:
each assertion is an estimate for every solution that might occur. The constants denoted by ΠN
are bounded by a polynomial in N , independently of the solution, homotopy parameter, and
allowed radius. Constants denoted by C(N ) may depend arbitrarily on the fixed integer N . All
integrals without an indicated graph measure use g.

19.1    Separation from the threshold region
Lemma 19.1 (Compact separation). Let Q0 be a compact subset of Ω disjoint from Dc . There
are η2 > 0, N0 , and allowed radii Rmin (N ) → ∞ such that, in stages 1 and 2,

                        h ≥ c + η2   on Q0       (N ≥ N0 , R ≥ Rmin (N )).                     (19.1)

The constants are uniform over both stages.

Proof. We first identify the geometric inequality satisfied by a relaxed limit. Take any sequence
of admissible solutions with Ni → ∞ and allowed Ri → ∞, and write

                         h(x) = lim inf{hi (y) : i ≥ j, dist(x, y) < j −1 }.
                                 j→∞

On compact sets this is a finite lower semicontinuous function, by Lemma 18.4. Let a smooth
function ϕ touch h from below at an interior point x, with grad ϕ(x) ̸= 0. Subtracting a small
multiple of the fourth power of the distance makes the contact strict without changing its two-jet.

                                                130
Minimization on a small closed ball then gives a subsequence of local lower contacts of ϕ plus
constants with hi , at points xi → x, whose contact values converge to h(x).
   At these contacts, ηNi grad fi = grad ϕ, so

                               ℓi
                    li σi ≥       | grad ϕ(xi )| −→ ∞,      di , χi −→ 0,       ai −→ 1.
                              ηNi

In particular this conclusion uses the floor and the fixed test gradient, rather than an estimate
for the solution gradients. Put νϕ = grad ϕ/| grad ϕ|. Positivity of Aχ and the Hessian inequality
at a lower contact give
                                               ai
                               trAχi K +             trAχi Hess ϕ ≤ Fi ≤ hi .
                                           | grad ϕ|

The last inequality holds throughout stages 1 and 2 because C ≤ 0 and D0 ≤ 0. Passing to the
limit proves
                                 trν ⊥ Hess ϕ
                                     ϕ
                                              + trν ⊥ K ≤ h(x).                       (19.2)
                                   | grad ϕ|       ϕ


The left side is the outward expansion of the regular level of ϕ. Neither convergence of the
homotopy parameters nor continuity bounds for the solution coefficients were needed.
    Fix c < k < k ′ < 0. Apply Lemma 5.1 on U = int Ω with u = h, Q = K and q0 = k ′ . The
relaxed limit is finite and lower semicontinuous. At a nonzero-gradient lower test whose contact
value is less than k ′ , (19.2) bounds its expansion by that value, hence by k ′ . The lemma therefore
shows that every smooth exterior support to the closed sublevel {h ≤ k} at an interior frontier
point has expansion at most k ′ . In particular, this conclusion also applies when the sublevel has
a plateau at height k.
    Lemma 18.4 makes this sublevel bounded: for a large fixed r, its lower height bound exceeds
the fixed negative number k. Adjoin Dc to obtain a compact closed set. At a frontier point on B,
each exterior support also supports Dc ; tangency comparison with its smooth boundary gives
expansion at most c. Lemma 17.3 therefore places the union in Dc′ for every k ′ < c′ < 0.
    Hausdorff right continuity at c and the positive distance of Q0 from Dc allow us to choose
c < k < k ′ < c′ < 0 so that Dc′ ∩ Q0 = ∅. If (19.1) failed, choose Ni → ∞, allowed Ri ≥ i,
and xi ∈ Q0 with hi (xi ) ≤ c + i−1 . A subsequence has xi → x ∈ Q0 , and then h(x) ≤ c < k,
contradicting that containment. This also proves uniformity over the two stages. Enlarging the
allowed radius function never invalidates the assertion.

19.2    Collar barriers and polynomial trace estimates
Lemma 19.2 (Inner collar slopes). For all sufficiently large N and allowed R, there are fixed

                c5          C6                                             C6
                   ≤ ∂ν f ≤           in stages 1 and 2,       |∂ν f | ≤         in stage 3.   (19.3)
                ηN          ηN                                             ηN
Here ν points into Ω. In stage 4, f = 0.

Proof. Use a fixed short collar 0 ≤ s ≤ s0 from Lemma 17.2, with C = −κs, νs = grad s/| ds|,
and 0 < cs ≤ | ds| ≤ Cs . All its geometric derivatives are bounded, and Ps + Hs ≥ c, where
Ps = tr{s=constant} K. The finitely many outer collar faces satisfy Lemma 19.1.


                                                    131
   For a positive-slope test h0 = c + ψ(s), the trace operator, with t computed from the solution’s

                                                          Hess s(νs , νs )        ψ ′′

                     Ps + aHs + χK(νs , νs ) + aχ                          + | ds| ′ .          (19.4)
                                                              | ds|               ψ

constants, the floor gives
                                                                  Nχ     3N      3N
              d ≤ C(ηN /ℓ)2 = Ce−ϵN ,           1 − a ≤ d,           =        ≥     .           (19.5)

Thus a ≥ 1/2 and N χ ≥ c4 d for large N .
    Choose a fixed smooth ϑ1 : [0, ∞) → [0, 1] equal to one on [0, 1] and zero on [2, ∞), and put
bN (s) = Λ∗ N ϑ1 (N s), where Λ∗ > 0 is a fixed collar constant, independent of N . The constant
will be chosen large; its crucial property is
                                           Z s0
                                      0≤          bN (s) ds ≤ 2Λ∗ .
                                            0

For the lower barrier in stages 1 and 2, prescribe ψ ′ (s) = q− exp( 0s bN ) and ψ(0) = 0. For the
                                                                           R

chosen value of Λ∗ , take q− > 0 so small that ψ ′ ≤ κ/2 and ψ(s0 ) < η2 . Positive normal direction
makes D0 = 0. Subtracting the prescribed trace right side from (19.4) gives a residual at least

                                     κs/2 − Cd + aχ| ds|bN (s).                                 (19.6)

The constant C in this residual depends only on collar geometry. We choose Λ∗ so large that
the last term dominates 2Cd on [0, 1/N ]. On [1/N, s0 ], the first term dominates Cd for large N ,
because N e−ϵN → 0, and the last term has a favorable sign. This is a strict lower barrier. Its
outer value is below h by separation and its innerR value agrees with h.
      For the upper barrier take ψ ′ (s) = q+ exp(− 0s bN ). Choose its fixed minimum slope larger
than twice the bounded linear variation of Ps + Hs − c + κs, and choose q+ still larger if needed
to dominate the height bound at s0 . The residual is now at most −c7 s + Cd − aχ| ds|bN , for
fixed c7 > 0. The same two intervals prove strict negativity. The bounded integral of bN leaves
all lower and upper test slopes between fixed positive constants, independently of N .
      For completeness, at an interior positive maximum of h0 − h, the test Hessian is at most
the solution Hessian, whereas its height is larger. The positive principal matrix and the strictly
increasing dependence of F on h contradict a positive test residual. This proves lower comparison;
reversing the difference proves upper comparison. In both comparisons Z and the gradient are
the same at contact, so all coefficients other than the height agree. Taking inward derivatives at
s = 0 gives the first assertion of (19.3).
      In stage 3 the face height is bζ = (1 − ζ)c. Use bζ ± ψ(s) with fixed large positive slopes and
ψ ′′ /ψ ′ = −bN . At s = 0 the two unit-direction inequalities in (18.29) say that the positive-slope
residual is nonpositive and the negative-slope residual is nonnegative. Replacing ±νs by ±aνs
costs O(d), including the term χK(νs , νs ); moving to distance s costs O(s), uniformly in ζ. The
change of height then supplies a strict linear margin, with the desired sign, when the minimum
test slope exceeds the fixed spatial error constant. The term involving bN has the correct sign
for both tests and dominates the O(d) error on [0, 1/N ]. On the remainder the linear margin
dominates it. The height bound handles s = s0 . These two comparisons bound the slope in
absolute value in stage 3. The terminal trace equation has f = 0, as established in Section 18.

19.5    Moser iteration and the full range
Proposition 19.4 (Bounded variables). For every sufficiently large fixed N and all sufficiently
large allowed R, every admissible smooth homotopy solution satisfies

                 |f | ≤ C0 /ηN ,      −ϵ ≤ t ≤ Z ≤ C(N ),             | grad f | ≤ C(N ).                  (19.17)

The constants are independent of R, the solution, and the homotopy parameter. No uniform
Hessian or coefficient continuity estimate is used in obtaining these bounds.

Proof. We first finish stage 1. Apply (19.15) to ω = ψekZ and then use (19.16). If ψ = 1 on a
smaller patch and | dψ| ≤ C/gap, the resulting norm step is
                                                                 1/(2k)
             ∥eZ ∥L4k (smaller graph) ≤ C(N )k 2 (1 + gap−2 )              ∥eZ ∥L2k (larger graph) .
                                       


Choose kj = 2j k∗ (N ) and geometrically decreasing gaps between fixed nested base patches. The
logarithms of the factors sum because j (1 + j)/2j < ∞. Writing S(r) = supQr eZ for a nested
                                       P

family of compact patches gives

                        S(r′ ) ≤ C(N )(r′′ − r′ )−A(N ) ∥eZ ∥L2k∗ (Γ∩π−1 (Qr′′ )) .

                                                                   1/k∗

If k∗ = 1 this already proves the bound. Otherwise Young’s inequality gives, for every α > 0,
                                                                               ′
                            S(r′ ) ≤ αS(r′′ ) + C(N, α)(r′′ − r′ )−A (N ) .
                                                                                                       ′
Iterate over radii increasing geometrically to a fixed larger radius and take α < 2−A (N )−1 . The
sum of the remainder terms is finite. The final term αj S(rj ) tends to zero for each individual
smooth solution, since all Qrj lie in a fixed compact patch. The resulting bound is independent


                                                     136
of that individual supremum. Finitely many such patches prove an upper bound for Z on any
fixed compact base set, including B.
    To extend the bound along the end, use (18.41), with a fixed small coefficient:

                                                   δ0
                       u−1 | div(uAχ K(w, ·))| ≤      T + ΠN (|K|2 + a| grad K|).
                                                   4
                 √
Since aσ = lσ 2 / D ≥ e−1 a2 , Young’s inequality bounds the second term by

                                 δ0
                                    ηN aσ + C(N )(|K|2 + | grad K|2 ).
                                 4
The last expression, apart from its favorable term, is o(ρ) on the end. Indeed 2 + 2q > 4 + δ and
| grad K|2 = O(r−4−2q ). Beyond a sufficiently large fixed-N sphere, the high-level source (19.11)
therefore implies
                            div(3uAχ grad Z) > 0 where Z > Z0 (N ).
At an interior maximum its left side is 3uAχ : Hess Z ≤ 0, a contradiction. The compact bound
controls the inner sphere and Z = 0 on SR . This proves the global upper bound in stage 1.
   In stages 2 and 3, λ = 0. The same high-level source is strictly positive, so it excludes interior
high maxima directly. The boundary slope bound implies

                  Z = t + 61 log(1 + l2 |∂ν f |2 ) ≤ t + 61 log(1 + e2 C62 /ηN
                                                                             2
                                                                               )   on B.

Thus sufficiently high boundary Z forces t ≥ 1. At such points j0 (t) = 1, and the boundary
prescription gives zero conormal flux. The boundary point principle excludes a nonconstant high
maximum there: in a neighborhood of the maximum the individual smooth operator is elliptic
and div(3uAχ grad Z) > 0, so its outward conormal derivative at a boundary maximum is strictly
positive, contrary to the zero datum. This uses ellipticity only for each individual solution. The
outer Dirichlet value is zero, so the upper bound follows uniformly through these stages.
    In stage 4, f = 0 and t = Z. For positive scaling η, sufficiently high levels lie outside the
penalty band and have source η(δ0 T + ρ) > 0; the same maximum and boundary          point argument
applies. At zero scaling, multiplying the homogeneous equation by Z gives 3L0 | dZ|2 = 0, and
                                                                               R

the outer value yields Z = 0. These bounds are uniform even as η ↓ 0.
    Finally the height bound is Lemma 18.4, and t ≤ Z follows from D ≥ 1. The implicit equation
gives
              D = e6(Z−t) ≤ e6(C(N )+ϵ) ,    | grad f |2 = (D − 1)/l2 ≤ ℓ−2 e6(C(N )+ϵ) .
This proves (19.17).

Remark 19.5. Separation, the collar slopes, and (19.7)–(19.10) were proved before introducing
any arbitrary C(N ). The graph comparisons, Moser iteration, and subsequent regularity may
have arbitrary fixed-N losses. The final mass comparison uses the earlier weighted trace and
boundary-flux inequalities directly; none of these later constants enters its limit as N → ∞.


20     Local regularity of the coupled system
The bounded-range estimate makes the trace equation uniformly elliptic at fixed N . The second
equation still contains | grad Z|2 and | Hess f |2 , so classical estimates cannot yet be applied to
it. We first use Theorem 12.1 to control df and the local integral of | Hess f |2 . The scalar

                                                   137
measure-source estimate of Lemma 6.2 then gives Hölder continuity of Z, including at both
boundary types. These two gains start the classical bootstrap.
   All constants in this section may depend arbitrarily on the fixed data and N . The earlier
polynomial bounds will control the final energy error. In coordinate calculations D denotes
ordinary differentiation; the graph scalar D = 1 + l2 | grad f |2 retains its earlier meaning in
geometric formulas.

Proposition 20.1. Fix the strict data, ϵ, a sufficiently large N , and any of the homotopy stages
of Section 18. Every smooth solution on an allowed finite truncation with t ≥ −ϵ has uniform
local bounds for every finite number of derivatives of f and Z, including up to the inner and
outer boundary faces. On the uniform unit patches on the end these constants are independent of
the outer truncation radius and of the stage parameter. They may depend arbitrarily on N . In
particular, on a fixed finite truncation they bound the C 1,α norm of Z for each fixed 0 < α < 1.

Proof. Work on the uniform interior and boundary-normal patches of the prepared geometry.

The axial√ estimate and the Hessian measure. Proposition 19.4 bounds f, Z, t, grad f and
keeps l/ D bounded above and away from zero at fixed N . Dividing the trace equation by this
factor gives
                            √

bounded gradient, source, geometry and ellipticity. It gives a uniform Hölder bound for grad f
and                         Z

Coordinate Hessians satisfy the same estimate, since their difference from covariant Hessians is
bounded. At a face the theorem uses the C 1 odd graph double and controls its interface flux
explicitly. It has no support hypothesis on K; the prepared tensor tail and geometry supply the
uniform patch bounds used here.

The scalar source and its boundary reflection. In coordinates, absorb the volume density
in the second equation and write it as

Here A and B1 are the coordinate versions of 3uAχ and λuAχ K(w, ·), respectively, with the

and uniformly elliptic, and B1 is bounded. The implicit relation for t has derivative 1 + pv/3 > 0,
so t = t(x, Z, Df ) is smooth on that range and

                                |Dt| ≤ C(N )(1 + |DZ| + |D2 f |).                           (20.4)

therefore gives

                                                 139
   On an inner face, f is constant and Df is normal, so Aχ ν = χν. Dividing the flux condition

                                     ∂ν Z = b(x, Z, f, Df ),                            (20.9)

                       C(N )(1 + |DZ| + |Df | + |D2 f |) ≤ C(N )(1 + |DZ|).

The trace theorem therefore bounds its W 1−1/p,p norm on the face by C(N )(1 + ∥Z∥W 1,p ) on a
larger collar patch. In particular, differentiating this extension costs DZ and D2 f , and does not
cost D2 Z.

Absorbing the quadratic gradient term. Fix p1 > 4. The linear local W 2,p1 estimates for
(20.8), with zero Dirichlet data or (20.9), give on nested patches
                                                                               
                     ∥Z∥W 2,p1 (U ′ ) ≤ C(N ) 1 + ∥DZ∥2L2p1 (U ) + ∥Z∥W 1,p1 (U ) .             (20.10)

We specify why the coefficient regularity at this point suffices. Freeze the continuous leading matrix
on small patches. The constant coefficient interior Hessian estimate follows from the Laplacian
estimate by a linear coordinate change. At a boundary, use normal coordinates. On that face the
matrix has no mixed tangential-normal entry, by the normal-axis property above. After removing
the boundary datum by its Sobolev trace extension, odd Dirichlet or even homogeneous-normal
reflection gives the constant coefficient estimate. The term (a(x) − a(x0 )) : D2 Z is absorbed when
its oscillation is sufficiently small. Cutoff terms are first order. This proves the local estimate with
the W 1−1/p1 ,p1 normal trace norm just estimated; it is the scalar Dirichlet/oblique specialization
of [1, Theorems 15.1–15.3, pp. 702–706, especially Theorem 15.2, estimate (15.5)], together with
the interior Lp estimate [18, Section 9.5]. Uniform Hölder control fixes the patch size uniformly.
The complementing condition can also be checked directly: a nonzero decaying Fourier mode for
the frozen scalar operator in a half-space has nonzero value and nonzero normal derivative at
its boundary. Thus neither the Dirichlet nor the normal operator annihilates that mode. The
normal field is smooth and has unit transverse component; the boundary is smooth and the
leading coefficients are continuous, as required for this W 2,p1 specialization. The inner and outer
faces are disjoint, so no change of boundary type occurs within such a patch.
    For a ball or smooth half-patch Qh of radius h, subtract a constant from Z. Nirenberg’s
interpolation inequality, with derivative orders 1, 2, endpoint exponent ∞, Hessian exponent p1
and interpolation parameter 1/2, gives

                 ∥DZ∥2L2p1 (Qh ) ≤ C oscQh Z ∥D2 Z∥Lp1 (Qh ) + Ch4/p1 −2 (oscQh Z)2 .           (20.11)

This is the bounded-domain form of [33, Lecture II, (2.2) and Remark 5, pp. 125–126]; extension
on half-patches preserves the estimate. The power of h follows by rescaling from dimension four.
Our choice p1 > 4, j = 1, m = 2 avoids the exceptional endpoint cases in that inequality.
    The Hölder estimate for Z makes its oscillation at most Chθ . Cover each larger patch in
(20.10) by such Qh with bounded overlap. Taking the ℓp1 norm of (20.11) over this cover gives

                      ∥DZ∥2L2p1 (U ) ≤ C sup oscQh Z ∥D2 Z∥Lp1 (U + ) + C(N, h),
                                         Qh


                                                  140
where U + is a fixed enlargement. The number of small patches may affect the harmless constant
C(N, h); their overlap, hence the coefficient of the Hessian norm, is bounded independently of
the truncation. Choose h so that this coefficient, including the constant in (20.10) and the fixed
enlargement factor, is less than 1/4. Ordinary interpolation handles the W 1,p1 term with another
coefficient less than 1/4.
    To make the absorption on enlarged patches precise, use a uniform radius cover of the whole
finite truncation and let S be the supremum of the local W 2,p1 norms on its smaller patches.
Each fixed enlargement is covered by a bounded number of these patches, independently of the
total number of patches. The last two estimates give S ≤ C(N ) + 12 S. For each individual
smooth solution on a finite truncation S is finite before this inequality is used. Thus S ≤ 2C(N )
uniformly. The same argument on nested compact patches gives the local estimate when no global
cover is needed.

Bootstrap and the continuation interface. Since p1 > 4, the bound just proved gives
         ′                                                        ′′
Z ∈ C 1,θ for some θ′ > 0. The trace equation now has C 1,θ coefficients and source after (20.7),
                                                               ′′
so one differentiation and Dirichlet Schauder give f ∈ C 3,θ , with 0 < θ′′ ≤ min(θ, θ′ ). Equation
                                ′′                              ′′
(20.8) consequently has a C 0,θ source, and (20.9) has a C 1,θ datum. The scalar oblique/Dirichlet
                                     ′′
Schauder estimate gives Z ∈ C 2,θ ; the boundary estimate is [1, Theorem 7.3, estimate (7.8),
p. 668], with the complementing condition verified above. Repeating these two steps in this
order raises the differentiability by one each time, because the trace equation contains Z without
its derivatives and the second equation contains at most D2 f and DZ in its lower order terms.
The normal datum has the same derivative count. Smooth prepared geometry and smooth data
complete the bootstrap to every finite order.
    For the scalar solve with trial Z ∈ C 1,α used in Section 21, this argument makes no assertion
that D2 Z exists. Its preliminary individual third derivatives for f require only one differentiation of
the trace equation, hence DZ. After the scalar gradient and axial estimates, bounded D2 f makes
Df Lipschitz; smooth compositions with the trial C 1,α input then give C 0,α scalar coefficients,
Dirichlet C 2,α estimates, and one more differentiation gives C 3,α . The independent scalar gradient
bound and solvability are proved there. Finally, for actual coupled solutions the uniform C 2
bound already controls every fixed C 1,α norm, α < 1, regardless of the first Hölder exponent
obtained above.
    The prepared end has uniform smooth geometry on unit patches. Large outer coordinate
spheres admit boundary normal patches with the same bounds; their curvature and higher
local chart derivatives are bounded uniformly. All the local constants and covering overlap
numbers in this proof can therefore be chosen independently of the outer radius. This proves the
proposition.


21     Scalar solvability and compact continuation
Fix the strict data and 0 < ϵ < 1. In this section N ≥ 4 and an allowed finite truncation ΩR are
fixed. The boundary components B and SR are smooth and disjoint. Fix also 0 < α < 1, with
no requirement that α equal the exponent in Theorem 12.1. We parametrize the four successive
stages of Section 18.2 by s ∈ [0, 4]: s = 0 is the desired system and s = 4 is the final zero-scale
system. This parameter is unrelated to a collar coordinate. Constants in the scalar construction
below may depend on N, ϵ, R, α and on a specified bound for the trial input. They need not be
polynomial in N .


                                                  141
21.1    The trace equation on unrestricted trial inputs
Proposition 21.1 (Scalar solution operator). For every Z ∈ C 1,α (ΩR ) and every homotopy
parameter s, the trace equation with its assigned constant Dirichlet values has a unique solution
f = fs [Z] ∈ C 3,α (ΩR ). No lower bound on t = t(Z, grad f ) is assumed. On bounded sets of trial
inputs, these solutions have bounded C 3,α norms, uniformly in s. The map (s, Z) 7→ fs [Z] is
continuous into C 3,α and is locally obtained by inversion of the scalar Dirichlet operator.

Proof. Write the trace prescription at parameter s as

Its matrix is independent of the value of f , and ∂f Gs = ηN Q > 0.

Height and large-gradient behavior. At a critical point of f , w = 0, Aχ = Id, t = Z,

                                             |f | ≤ Mf .                                      (21.5)

This argument uses neither the divergence equation nor the floor.

Logarithmic collar barriers. Extend the metric smoothly across all faces to a compact
enlargement. Choose a short distance scale below its injectivity radius, the tubular radii of the
faces, and their positive mutual separation. The geometry of all these collars is bounded. For the
inward distance r from a face with value b, consider

Here k and B0 are constants, not any of the geometric tensors. At a comparison contact the

geometric scales. Finally choose B0 large enough that
                                q1 (δ2 ) ≥ 2,         ψ(δ2 ) > 2Mf + 1.                     (21.10)
These choices are compatible: as B0 → ∞ the two quantities tend respectively to 1/(kδ2 ) > 4
and to infinity.
    At a positive interior maximum of f − b − ψ(r) the solution and test have the same gradient
and the solution Hessian is no larger. Evaluating the source at the solution height, which obeys
(21.5), the preceding strict upper bound contradicts (21.3). At the outer end of the collar the
height inequality follows from (21.10), and at its inner end there is equality. The negative test is
handled with the reversed inequalities. Thus
                       |f (x) − b| ≤ ψ dist(x, face)            (dist(x, face) ≤ δ2 ).      (21.11)
