only at solution heights.

                                                    143
The full two-point comparison. The face barriers control graph slopes only at the boundary.
An interior two-point comparison supplies the missing global gradient bound. Its key feature is
that the three transverse eigenvalues agree at both points; the two axial eigenvalues need only
have the same lower bound.
                                                                                    2
     Let r(x, y) be distance in the smooth extension. On the compact set {(x, y) ∈ ΩR : r(x, y) ≤
δ2 } consider
                                      f (x) − f (y) − ψ(r(x, y)).
It is zero on the diagonal and negative when r = δ2 . It cannot have a positive maximum with
a boundary endpoint: if, for example, x belongs to a face of height b, then y lies in that same
face’s collar, its distance to the face is at most r(x, y), and (21.11) and monotonicity of ψ give
b − f (y) ≤ ψ(r(x, y)). The case with y on a face is identical. The separation choice excludes
different faces at such distances.
    Suppose then that a positive maximum occurs at an interior pair with 0 < r < δ2 . The short
geodesic γ from y to x is unique in the extension; the distance is smooth at this pair. Write
ey = γ̇(0) and ex = γ̇(r), with unit speed. First variation gives

                   grad f (x) = q1 ex ,           grad f (y) = q1 ey ,       q1 = ψ ′ (r) ≥ 1.   (21.12)

simultaneously in the corresponding Ea directions, using geodesic endpoint curves. The first
variation of the distance is zero. The second variation is bounded above by Cr: the parallel
comparison field has index form

This estimate is uniform in s, θ and on bounded trial-input sets, and was obtained without
assuming the floor or a positive uniform ellipticity constant in advance.

Regularity and the scalar method of continuity. The gradient bound and the implicit
relation now confine all scalar coefficients to a compact range. In particular 0 < χ0 ≤ χθ ≤ 1, and
the normalized source is bounded. There is a small regularity point before applying Theorem 12.1.
An individual C 2,α solution of (21.3) is C 3,α for a trial Z ∈ C 1,α . Indeed one differentiation of
the equation gives a linear equation for a first derivative of f . Its coefficients and source involve
Z, grad Z, grad f, Hess f and smooth geometric coefficients; they involve no second derivative
of Z. Difference quotients justify this differentiation initially. In a flattened boundary chart
tangential differentiation preserves the zero Dirichlet value after subtracting the constant face
height. The linear boundary Schauder estimate controls these tangential derivatives through
order two. Solving the original equation for the pure normal second derivative, whose coefficient
is positive, and differentiating that identity in the normal direction recovers the remaining pure
normal third derivative. Every other third derivative already has a tangential index. This gives
individual third-order regularity, without presuming a uniform estimate.
    Theorem 12.1 now applies to (21.3), including its constant-Dirichlet faces. It bounds grad f
in C 0,β for some β > 0. Since the trial inputs are bounded in C 1,α , the coefficients and source
                                                             ′
of the scalar equation are consequently bounded in C 0,β for a positive β ′ . Dirichlet Schauder
                                 ′
estimates give a uniform C 2,β bound for f . In particular grad f is now uniformly Lipschitz.
Smooth composition with Z, which also has bounded first derivatives, shows that those same
coefficients and sources have bounded C 0,α norms for the originally chosen, arbitrary α < 1.
Schauder estimates upgrade f to C 2,α . Their coefficients and sources then have bounded C 1,α
norms, and the preceding differentiation argument gives

                                          ∥f ∥C 3,α (ΩR ) ≤ C.                                (21.16)

The linear estimates used here are the interior and Dirichlet boundary Schauder estimates and
their differentiated versions [18, Sections 6.1–6.4]. Their hypotheses hold on the finite smooth
domain: positive uniform ellipticity on the established range, the indicated Hölder coefficient
bounds, and smooth constant Dirichlet data on each disjoint component.
                                                                                            (θ)
   For completeness, the local inverse has the required sign. Linearizing A(θ) : Hess f − Gs in
the f variable gives

                 Lφ = A(θ)ij ∇i ∇j φ + bi ∇i φ − ηN (1 − θ + θQ)φ,        φ|∂ΩR = 0.          (21.17)

Its first-order coefficients are bounded Hölder functions along each solution. Differentiating the
gradient dependence of A(θ) contributes terms involving Hess f only to this first-order part. The
strictly negative zeroth-order coefficient and the maximum principle give a zero kernel. The
linear Dirichlet solvability theorem and Schauder estimate give an isomorphism C02,α → C 0,α [18,

                                                 145
Sections 6.2–6.3]; here the subscript means zero trace on all boundary components. Equivalently,
one can continue the linear operator to ∆ − ηN , using its Schauder estimate and the maximum
principle at each point of that linear continuation. With the additional coefficient regularity it is
also an isomorphism C03,α → C 1,α .
    The endpoint θ = 0 is the solvable linear Dirichlet problem ∆f = ηN f . Subtract a smooth
extension of the assigned face values when working on spaces with homogeneous boundary
data. The implicit function theorem and (21.17) give openness in θ. The bound (21.16) gives
compactness in C 2,α , so passage to a limiting equation gives closedness. Thus the solution exists
at θ = 1.
    If f1 − f2 had a positive maximum in the interior, their gradients and therefore their principal
matrices and Q would agree there. Their Hessian difference would have nonpositive contraction,
whereas the difference of the sources would be ηN Q(f1 − f2 ) > 0. This contradiction, and its
reverse, prove uniqueness. Finally use (21.17) in C03,α → C 1,α with Z as a C 1,α parameter.
Smooth composition and the implicit function theorem show that the unique scalar solution
depends continuously on that input in C 3,α . The same statement holds along each homotopy
stage; the explicit prescriptions and boundary heights agree at their junctions, so it holds across
those junctions as well.

21.2    The frozen linear return problem
The trace equation is now solvable for every trial Z, including trials below the intended floor.
We can therefore define a compact return map on a fixed function space. The floor becomes a
restriction on the degree domain, rather than an unproved prerequisite for defining the map.
    Set
                               X = {Z ∈ C 1,α (ΩR ) : Z|SR = 0}.
Given (s, Z) ∈ [0, 4] × X, first compute f = fs [Z] by Proposition 21.1 and then all of t, l, u, w, χ,
F and T from this pair. Let λs denote the drift parameter, let qs = 1 in the first three stages and
qs = η in the last, and let bs denote the prescribed right side of (Vλs )ν /u in the full homotopy,
including its last-stage scale. Define Ts Z = Ze by
                       
                       div 3uAχ grad Z + uAχ λs K(w,   ·) = qs uΞ          in ΩR ,
                                                          
                       
                                      e
                         3uAχ grad Ze + uAχ λs K(w, ·) ν = ubs              on B,               (21.18)
                                                      
                       
                       
                       Ze=0                                                on SR .

Every quantity in this display except grad Ze is frozen at the input pair, including every occurrence
of grad Z in Ξ.
     The coefficient A = 3uAχ is symmetric and positive definite. On a bounded subset of X its
ellipticity constants are uniformly positive, by Proposition 21.1 and the smooth implicit equation
for t. Let D = uAχ λs K(w, ·). On the Hilbert space of H 1 functions vanishing on SR , the weak
form of (21.18) is
                Z                              Z               Z                   Z
                      A grad Ze · grad φ = −        qs uΞφ −        D · grad φ −       ubs φ.   (21.19)
                 ΩR                            ΩR              ΩR                  B

The last sign uses the inward normal ν on B. Poincaré’s inequality holds because SR is nonempty
and the domain is connected. Consequently the left side is a coercive bilinear form and the right
side is a bounded linear functional, by the trace inequality. To solve it directly, minimize one half


                                                    146
of this quadratic form minus the right-side functional on that Hilbert space. Coercivity bounds a
minimizing sequence in H 1 ; weak compactness and lower semicontinuity give a minimizer. Its first
variation is (21.19), and strict convexity gives uniqueness. There is no Neumann compatibility
constraint.
    We check the precise regularity of this linear problem. The formula
                                                    3+p
                                      Aχ = Id −           w⊗w
                                                   3 + pv

is smooth also at zero gradient, and the derivative of the implicit relation in t is 1+pv/3 > 0. Thus
t, u, Aχ , D have bounded C 1,α norms on bounded input sets. Differentiating t = t(x, Z, grad f )
requires only grad Z and Hess f . The expression for T is quadratic in Hess f and grad t with
smooth bounded-range coefficients, so qs uΞ has a bounded C 0,α norm. The prescribed boundary
expression ubs has a bounded C 1,α norm. At B the scalar Dirichlet data make grad f normal,
hence Aχ ν = χν; the boundary equation is equivalently

                                             bs − λs (Aχ K(w, ·))ν
                                   ∂ν Ze =                         .                         (21.20)
                                                      3χ

This is a C 1,α normal derivative datum, with a denominator bounded away from zero on the
input set. After expanding the divergence, interior and boundary Schauder estimates therefore
give a bounded C 2,α norm for Z.   e The estimates apply locally with Dirichlet data on SR and a
uniformly oblique, here normal, derivative on B; these faces do not meet. We use the Dirichlet
estimates just cited and the oblique derivative estimates of [18, Theorem 6.30, p. 127]; the weak
existence argument above fixes the additive constant that is present in a pure Neumann problem.
     Subtracting two return equations and applying the same linear estimates proves continuity in
(s, Z) into C 2,α . The parameter continuity is uniform on bounded input sets. Indeed the negative
zeroth-order bound in (21.17) and the Schauder estimate bound the scalar inverse uniformly on
such a set. Differentiation in a stage parameter, after extending its Dirichlet data, then bounds
the parameter derivative of fs [Z]; the return equation gives the same conclusion for Ts Z. The
finitely many stage junctions preserve continuity. The inclusion C 2,α ,→ C 1,α on the fixed smooth
compact domain is compact. We have thus constructed a continuous homotopy Ts : X → X
that is compact on bounded sets, jointly in s, before making any floor restriction. A fixed point
satisfies both original equations and their exact boundary conditions. It is smooth: the first gain
is Z ∈ C 2,α , and successive differentiation of the scalar equation, the divergence equation and its
normal boundary condition then alternately improves f and Z to every finite order.

21.3    Degree on the open floor sections
Proposition 21.2 (Existence and exhaustion). For the fixed strict data and every 0 < ϵ < 1
there are N0 and Rmin (N ), with Rmin (N ) → ∞, such that for every integer N ≥ N0 and every
R ≥ Rmin (N ) the desired coupled system has a smooth solution on ΩR with t > −ϵ. At each such
fixed N , a subsequence as R → ∞ converges smoothly on compact subsets of Ω to a solution of
the equations and inner boundary conditions with t ≥ −ϵ. The previously proved height, range
and collar estimates hold for this limit.

Proof. All estimates from Sections 18–20 were conditional estimates for arbitrary smooth homotopy
solutions. Specifically, Lemma 18.4 and Proposition 18.7 precede the bounded-range and derivative
estimates; Lemma 19.1 and Lemma 19.2 apply to solutions satisfying the floor; Proposition 19.4

                                                  147
then bounds their variables; and Proposition 20.1 bounds their derivatives. None presupposes a
fixed point of the map just defined. On this finite truncation these results give, uniformly in s,

                 ∥Z∥C 1,α ≤ M∗    for every smooth fixed point with min t ≥ −ϵ.

The arbitrary exponent α < 1 is permitted because the classical estimates give a C 2 bound, and
higher bounds as needed. Choose M0 > M∗ + 1.
   Let
                 m(s, Z) = min t x, Z, grad fs [Z] ,
                                                    
                             ΩR
                       W = {(s, Z) ∈ [0, 4] × X : m(s, Z) > −ϵ, ∥Z∥C 1,α < M0 },             (21.21)
                      Ws = {Z : (s, Z) ∈ W}.

The minimum is continuous by Proposition 21.1, so W is relatively open and every section is a
bounded open subset of X. Consider

                                   K = {(s, Z) ∈ W : Z = Ts Z}.

This is compact: any sequence has a convergent parameter subsequence, and joint compactness
of Ts on the bounded input ball makes its fixed points converge in X; continuity preserves the
fixed-point equation and membership in the closed set. Every point of K is a smooth solution
with m(s, Z) ≥ −ϵ. Its norm is at most M∗ < M0 , and Proposition 18.7 excludes m(s, Z) = −ϵ.
Therefore
                                           K ⊂ W.                                      (21.22)
    Apply Lemma 6.5 with I = [0, 4], the Banach space X above, the jointly compact homotopy
(s, Z) 7→ Ts Z, and U = W. The uniform norm bound makes W bounded; continuity of m makes it
relatively open. The preceding compactness argument and (21.22) verify that its closed fixed-point
set is compact and contained in W, so no fixed point lies on the relative boundary. The lemma
gives constancy of
                               deg(Id −Ts , Ws , 0) (0 ≤ s ≤ 4).
It applies to these varying sections without a convexity assumption.
    At s = 4 the scalar boundary values are zero and the final trace equation is

                                    trAχ H f = ηN f + D1 (x, w).

At a positive or negative extremum w = 0 and D1 (x, 0) = 0, so the strict height sign forces f = 0.
This holds for every trial Z, not just at fixed points. Thus t = Z, χ = 1 and u = L0 (Z). Both
the drift and the prescribed source and boundary flux in (21.18) vanish. Testing its homogeneous
equation by Ze gives                Z
                                           3L0 (Z)| grad Z|
                                                         e 2 = 0,
                                      ΩR

so T4 Z = 0 for every input. At its sole fixed point Z = 0 one has t = 0, so 0 ∈ W4 and
deg(Id −T4 , W4 , 0) = 1 [27, Chapter II, §8, p. 55]. The same degree at s = 0 yields a desired fixed
point in W0 , proving the assertion on the finite truncation.
    We finally make the parameter order explicit. Fix the strict background, ϵ, the collars and
the end weights first. The pointwise floor argument chooses the small δ0 and a polynomially
large penalty coefficient CN without derivative estimates. The separation statement is uniform

                                                  148
along arbitrary expanding sequences of the first two homotopy stages; applied at the finitely
many fixed collar ends, it supplies the eventual separation needed for the slopes. Increase N0 to
meet this requirement and the finitely many conditions of the form ΠN ηN /ℓ < constant. Increase
Rmin (N ) to meet all the fixed-N outer-radius requirements, requiring also Rmin (N ) ≥ N . These
choices give the estimates for every N ≥ N0 and every R ≥ Rmin (N ). All subsequent scalar,
degree-radius and classical regularity constants may depend arbitrarily on the now fixed N .
    For that fixed N , take Rj → ∞ and the desired fixed points just obtained. Proposition 20.1
gives uniform bounds of every finite order on each fixed compact subset, including patches meeting
B. Successive compact extractions on a countable exhaustion produce a subsequence converging
in C k on each such compact set for every finite k. All equations, inner data and collar inequalities
pass to the limit. The strict floor may become non-strict, which is the asserted t ≥ −ϵ. The
normalization at infinity is supplied by the uniform tail estimates in Proposition 22.1; it is not
inferred from compact convergence alone. Only after this fixed-N exhaustion will N tend to
infinity in the flux comparison. The latter uses the earlier polynomial estimates directly, and does
not use the arbitrary constants of the present section.


22     End normalization and the mass–area comparison
Fix the strict approximation data, the threshold exterior Ω with boundary B, and 0 < ϵ < 1.
Take N sufficiently large as in Proposition 21.2. In the first part of this section, N remains fixed
while the truncation radius tends to infinity. Constants in end estimates may depend arbitrarily
on these fixed parameters. In the flux deficit estimate, by contrast, we will return directly to the
polynomial bounds of Lemma 19.3.

22.1    Normalization inherited from the truncations
Proposition 22.1 (Control of the asymptotic end). The exhaustion solutions of Proposition 21.2
admit a smooth local limit (f, Z) on Ω satisfying the desired system and t ≥ −ϵ. Every such limit
has
                      |∇j f | ≤ Cj (N )e−cj (N )r (j ≥ 0),    Z, t = O2 (r−2 ).            (22.1)
The zeroth-order estimates producing this normalization are uniform on the finite truncations.
The resulting metric gb = e2t (g + l2 df ⊗ df ) is smooth, complete with its boundary included, and
asymptotically flat with

                   gbij − δij = O2 (r−q ),   0 ≤ Rgb = O(r−4−δ ),       b B < 0.
                                                                        H                     (22.2)

Here the normal defining H
                         b B points toward infinity.

Proof. The local compactness and the inner boundary conditions follow from Propositions 20.1
and 21.2. To determine the value at infinity, apply Lemma 15.1 to the finite truncations before
taking their local limit. We verify its hypotheses explicitly.
    The strict background has 1 < q < 2, 0 < δ < q − 1, g − δEucl = O2 (r−q ), K = O1 (r−1−q ),
trg K = 0, and Rg = O(r−4−δ ) by Proposition 17.1. Outside the compact support of C, the trace
right side is ηN f . The divergence equation is exactly (15.4), with ρ0 = r−4−δ , ρ1 = r−2γ and the
smooth penalty switch defined in (18.21). Their differentiated end bounds follow from the fixed
smooth radial choices. Propositions 19.4 and 20.1 supply the common bounded range and all



                                                149
fixed-N classical estimates, on end unit patches and on the zero-Dirichlet outer faces, uniformly
in the truncation radius. The leading matrix is uniformly elliptic on that range.
    The lemma therefore gives exponential decay of f and its fixed derivatives, a truncation-
uniform bound |Z| + |t| ≤ C(N )r−2 , and Z, t = O2 (r−2 ) for every local limit. In particular
normalization at infinity follows from a comparison on the actual truncations, not from local
convergence. The equation used for that comparison is
                             3∆g Z + 3 p(Z) + 2 − δ0 (p(Z) + 1) |dZ|2

Completion of Proposition 16.2. The threshold exterior and its all-cut comparison are supplied
by Lemma 17.2. Propositions 21.2 and 22.1 construct the complete metric with the stated signs
and decay. Its lower metric bound gives the asserted three-dimensional area comparison on each
cut separately. Proposition 22.2 gives the energy estimate, since ℓ = e−ϵN and ηN = ℓ3/2 . Every
stated output is therefore established before the Riemannian Penrose inequality is applied.

22.3    A minimal enclosing hypersurface
The boundary B has strictly negative mean curvature in gb. We obtain the minimal boundary
required for the Riemannian theorem from the full-perimeter enclosure proposition. Perimeter
here means the full area of the measure-theoretic boundary of a set, including any portion
coinciding with an obstacle.

Lemma 22.3 (Minimal enclosure). The metric gb of Proposition 22.1 admits a nonempty compact
smooth embedded minimal hypersurface Σ enclosing B. Its exterior is connected, complete with
boundary, and has the same single asymptotically flat end. The hypersurface is outer area-
minimizing, with disconnected competitors permitted, and its area Ab = |Σ|gb satisfies

                                           Ab ≥ e−3ϵ A∗ .                                 (22.12)




                                                152
Proof. For the classical codimension-one regularity background, see [15, Sections 5.3–5.4] and
[16]; the smooth-Riemannian formulation is [39, Chapter 7, §5, Theorem 5.8, p. 200]. Apply
Proposition 8.3 to (X, gX ) = (Ω, gb). The threshold construction gives a smooth connected
orientable four-dimensional exterior with nonempty compact smooth boundary B and exactly
one end. Proposition 22.1 gives completeness with B included and gb − δ = O2 (r−q ) with q > 1.
These are the full geometric hypotheses of the cited proposition; it imposes no scalar-curvature
condition and does not require decay exponent two. Its perimeter counts the entire frontier,
including contact with the obstacle.
    The strict inequality Hb B < 0 places the minimizing frontier Σ strictly outside B and makes
it a nonempty compact smooth embedded minimal hypersurface. The same proposition gives
its connected complete one-ended exterior and outer area-minimization with every component
counted, including disconnected enclosing competitors.
    Every such cut encloses the original obstacle. The cut comparison in Lemma 17.2 gives
|Σ|g ≥ A∗ . Restriction of gb ≥ e−2ϵ g to each three-dimensional tangent plane gives

                                     |Σ|gb ≥ e−3ϵ |Σ|g ≥ e−3ϵ A∗ .

This proves the asserted area comparison. The argument uses the general full-perimeter proposition
directly, with its full q > 1 decay range. An enclosure result requiring O2 (r−2 ) decay would not
suffice for this application.

22.4    The Riemannian inequality and the ordered limits
We use the following dimension-four instance of the boundary Riemannian Penrose inequality [11,
Theorem 1.4, p. 84]. Let (E , gE ) be a smooth connected four-dimensional Riemannian manifold,
complete with its nonempty compact boundary included, having one asymptotically flat end.
Suppose in that end

                (gE )ij − δij = O2 (r−p0 ),   p0 > 1,          RgE = O(r−s0 ),   s0 > 4,

and RgE ≥ 0 everywhere. If its boundary is minimal and outer area-minimizing, then
                                                               2/3
                                             1       |∂E |gE
                                                 
                                       EgE ≥                          .                    (22.13)
                                             2         ω3

The boundary may have one or more components, and the ADM normalization is 1/(6ω3 ). The
numerical inequality does not require a spin assumption; the additional spin hypothesis in the
published theorem concerns its equality conclusion. We use only (22.13).

Proof of Theorem 16.1. First keep one set of strict approximation data fixed. For fixed ϵ > 0
and each sufficiently large N , take the exhaustion limit of Proposition 22.1. Apply (22.13) to
the exterior of Σ from Lemma 22.3, with metric gb. That exterior is smooth and complete with
boundary, has one end, and has nonnegative scalar curvature. Its decay exponents are p0 = q > 1
and s0 = 4 + δ > 4 by (22.2). Its boundary is compact, minimal, and outer area-minimizing by
the lemma. Its ADM energy exists by Proposition 22.2 and is unchanged by restriction past the
compact region. Thus every hypothesis of the Riemannian theorem has been checked for this new
exterior.
